\langle\Gamma_{1}\widehat{u},\Gamma_{0}\widehat{v}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}\widehat{u},\Gamma_{1}\widehat{v}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}=\langle(I_{\mathcal{H}}+\zeta R_{1}(\zeta))u,R_{2}(\zeta)^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle R_{1}(\zeta)u,(I_{\mathcal{H}}+\overline{\zeta}R_{2}(\zeta)^{*})v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

and so re-arranging the right hand side of the last formula gives the desired generalization of the resolvent difference formula,

\langle\big(R_{2}(\zeta)-R_{1}(\zeta)\big)u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\Gamma_{1}\widehat{u},\Gamma_{0}\widehat{v}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}\widehat{u},\Gamma_{1}\widehat{v}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}\text{ for all $u,v\in{\mathcal{H}}$}

(2.34)

and
\widehat{u},\widehat{v}
as defined in (
2.33
). (Clearly, when
A
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
are operators the resolvent difference equation (
2.34
) becomes (
2.21
)).

\Diamond

3.
Riccati equation for resolvents and Hadamard-type formulas for eigenvalues

In this section we consider a one-parameter family of self-adjoint extensions of a given symmetric operator perturbed by a family of bounded operators. In turn, the extensions are constructed using families of Lagrangian subspaces in a boundary space and boundary traces that also depend on the parameter. Our final objective is to derive a differential (Riccati-type) equation for the resolvents of the perturbed operators and formulas for the derivatives of their isolated eigenvalues with respect to the parameter. The latter abstract formulas generalize, on one side, the classical perturbation results from the case of additive perturbations, see, e.g.,
[
83
, Section II.5]
, and, on another, the Rayleigh–Hadamard-type variational formulas for eigenvalues of partial differential operators depending on a parameter, see, e.g.,
[
72
,
80
]
.

3.1.
Parametric families of operators

We continue to assume that
A
is a densely defined closed symmetric operator with equal (possibly infinite) deficiency indices, that
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}=\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})
is equipped with graph norm of
A^{*}
, and that
{\mathcal{D}}
, the domain of the trace operator, is a dense subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
.
The following hypothesis will be assumed throughout this section.

Hypothesis 3.1
.

We assume that Hypothesis
2.1
holds for the trace operator
\mathrm{T}
and a subspace
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
with
\operatorname{dom}(\mathrm{T})={\mathcal{D}}
,
and, in addition,
we assume that the subspace

{\mathcal{D}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
is equipped with a Banach norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}}
such that the (injective) imbedding
\jmath
of
{\mathcal{D}}
into
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
is continuous with respect to this norm, i.e.
\jmath\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})
.

A typical example that we have in mind is the Laplacian
A=-\Delta
on
L^{2}(\Omega)
with
\operatorname{dom}(A)=H^{2}_{0}(\Omega)
for an open bounded
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

with smooth boundary. In this case, we have

A^{*}=-\Delta,\ {\mathcal{H}}_{+}=\operatorname{dom}(A^{*}):=\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\},

(3.1)

{\mathcal{D}}:={\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
, where the space

{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega):=\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}

is equipped with the norm
\|u\|_{\mathcal{D}}:=(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\Delta u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2})^{1/2}.

For
u\in{\mathcal{D}}
the trace operator is given by

\mathrm{T}u=[\gamma_{{}_{D}}u,-\Phi\gamma_{{}_{N}}u]^{\top}\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}\text{ with }\mathfrak{H}:=H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),

here
\gamma_{{}_{D}}
is the Dirichlet and
\gamma_{{}_{N}}=\nu\cdot\gamma_{{}_{D}}\nabla u
is the (weak) Neumann trace maps
4
4

4

see Appendix
C
for a discussion of trace maps
, and
\Phi
is the Riesz isomorphism between
H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)=(H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))^{*}
and
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
, cf. (
4.31
) below.

Proposition 3.2
.

Under Hypothesis
3.1

one has
\mathrm{T}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
. In addition, if
{\mathcal{A}}
is a self-adjoint extension of
A
with
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\subset{\mathcal{D}}
then there exist
c,C>0
such that

c\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}\leq\|u\|_{{\mathcal{D}}}\leq C\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}\text{ for all $u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})$.}

(3.2)

In other words, the norms in
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are equivalent on
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
for any self-adjoint extension
{\mathcal{A}}
of
A
with
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\subset{\mathcal{D}}
. Furthermore, if
V=V^{*}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}+V)
then

({\mathcal{A}}+V-\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}}).

(3.3)

Proof.

The operator
\mathrm{T}
is bounded as an everywhere defined on the Banach space
{\mathcal{D}}
closable operator (see Proposition
2.3
).
We claim that
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
is a
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}}
-closed subspace of the Banach space
{\mathcal{D}}
. Indeed, suppose that
u_{n}\rightarrow u
in
{\mathcal{D}}
for some
u_{n}\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
. Since
{\mathcal{D}}
is continuously embedded into
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
, the sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is Cauchy in
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
, that is, it is Cauchy with respect to the graph norm of
A^{*}
. Hence,
\{u_{n}\}
is convergent to
u
in
{\mathcal{H}}
and the sequence of vectors
A^{*}u_{n}={\mathcal{A}}u_{n}
converges in
{\mathcal{H}}
. Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is a closed operator, we conclude that
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
, as claimed. Now, we will consider
\jmath
as a mapping from the Banach space
(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}),\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{D}}})
into the Banach space
(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}),\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}})
. This mapping is bounded and bijective, hence its inverse is also bounded yielding (
3.2
).
Assertion (
3.3
) follows from (
2.10
) and (
3.2
).
∎

Remark 3.3
.

It is worth comparing Propositions
2.3
and
3.2
: indeed, (
2.8
) says that the product
\mathrm{T}R(\zeta,{\mathcal{A}})
is a bounded operator while Proposition
3.2
gives that each factor in this product is bounded. The latter fact will be used in the proof of Theorem
3.18
below (specifically, see (
3.39
)) and it comes at the expense of assuming Hypothesis
3.1
.

\Diamond

Hypothesis 3.4
.

We assume that

\mathrm{T}:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}):t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}

is a one-parameter family of trace operators and
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
is a
t
-independent subspace such that
\mathrm{T}_{t}
and
{\mathcal{D}}=\operatorname{dom}(\mathrm{T}_{t})
satisfy Hypothesis
3.1
(and thus, in particular, Hypothesis
2.1
) for each
t\in[0,1]
.
Let
Q:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}),t\mapsto Q_{t}
, be a one-parameter family of orthogonal projections. We assume that
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})\in\Lambda(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
is a Lagrangian plane for each
t\in[0,1]
. We further assume that there exists a family
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
, of self-adjoint extensions of
A
satisfying

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})\subset{\mathcal{D}},

(3.4)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{T}_{t}\big(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})\big)}=\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t}).

(3.5)

Let

V:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
be a one-parameter family of self-adjoint bounded operators. We denote
H_{t}:={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
and
R_{t}(\zeta):=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
for
\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})
and
t\in[0,1]
.

Hypothesis
3.4
gives a rather general setup for boundary value problems parameterized by a one dimensional variable. We briefly list
several families of operators for which the operators per se, their domains, and respective traces depend on a given parameter. Our immediate objective is just to give a glimpse of the typical situations of the setup described in Hypothesis
3.4
.
More examples with detailed analysis are given below, see Subsections
4.2
,
4.3
,
7
,
5.2
,
5.3
, and
5.4
.

Example 3.5
.

A well studied model which fits Hypothesis
3.4
is the family of Schrödinger operators equipped with Robin-type boundary conditions considered on a family of subdomains
\Omega_{t}\subset\Omega
obtained by linear shrinking of a bounded star-shaped domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
to its center. The linear rescaling of
\Omega_{t}
back to
\Omega
leads to a one-parameter family of Schrödinger operators
H_{t}:=-\Delta_{t}+V
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
subject to Robin boundary conditions
(\theta_{t}u-t^{-1}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu})\upharpoonright_{\partial\Omega}=0
, where
\theta_{t}\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega,{\mathbb{R}})
is the rescaled boundary function. In this case, the minimal symmetric operator is given by the Laplacian considered on
H^{2}_{0}(\Omega)
, its self-adjoint extensions
-\Delta_{t}
are determined by the boundary condition
(\theta_{t}u-t^{-1}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu})\upharpoonright_{\partial\Omega}=0
which in turn corresponds to the Lagrangian planes
\{(f,g)^{\top}\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega):\theta_{t}f=g\}
in
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
. That is, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}(\Omega),\mathfrak{H}:=H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\mathrm{T}_{t}:=[\gamma_{{}_{D}},-t^{-1}\Phi\gamma_{{}_{N}}]^{\top},

(3.6)

\displaystyle A:=-\Delta,\operatorname{dom}(A)=H^{2}_{0}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega):=\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}):=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega):\theta_{t}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u=t^{-1}\gamma_{{}_{N}}u\},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t}):=\{(f,g)^{\top}\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega):\theta_{t}f=g\},

(3.9)

here
\gamma_{{}_{D}}
and
\gamma_{{}_{N}}
denote the Dirichlet and (weak) Neumann traces, see Appendix
C
, and
\Phi:H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)\to H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
denotes the Riesz isomorphism, see (
4.31
). Similar models are systematically studied in
[
44
,
45
,
49
]
and discussed in some details in a more general setting in Section
5.4
below.

\Diamond

Example 3.6
.

Our next example is a matrix second order operator posted on a multidimensional infinite cylinder with variable cross sections. We denote by
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
the axial and by
x
the transversal variables, that is, we set

\Omega:=\big\{(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}:t\in{\mathbb{R}},x\in{\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n}\big\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},

where, for instance,
r(t)=1+t/(1+t^{2})
, and
{\mathbb{B}}_{r}^{n}
is the ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
of radius
r
centered at zero. Denoting
\Delta_{(t,x)}=\partial^{2}_{t}+\Delta_{x}
and
\Delta_{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{2}_{x_{j}}
, we will consider in
L^{2}(\Omega;{\mathbb{C}}^{N})
the Schrödinger operator

-\Delta_{(t,x)}+V=-\partial_{t}^{2}+B_{t},\text{ where $B_{t}=-\Delta_{x}(t)+V$ and $V=V(t,x)$}

is a smooth bounded
(N\times N)
-matrix valued potential taking symmetric values while the
x
-Laplace operator
-\Delta_{x}(t)

is acting in
L^{2}({\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n};{\mathbb{C}}^{N})
and equipped with the following domain,

\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{x}(t)):=\big\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n}):\mathrm{T}u:=(\gamma_{{}_{D,\partial{\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n}}}u,-\Phi\gamma_{{}_{N,\partial{\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n}}}u)\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\big\},

where
{\mathcal{F}}:t\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
is a given smooth family of Lagrangian subspaces in the boundary space
H^{1/2}(\partial{\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n})\times H^{1/2}(\partial{\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n})
. We note parenthatically that the spectral flow of the family
\{B_{t}\}_{t=-\infty}^{\infty}
of the self-adjoint operators
B_{t}
is of interest as it is related to the spectrum of the Schrödinger operator
-\Delta_{(t,x)}+V
in
L^{2}(\Omega;{\mathbb{C}}^{N})
; this relation could be established using spatial dynamics, cf.
[
91
,
118
,
117
]
, via a connection to a first order differential operator, cf.
[
96
]
and
[
65
]
.
Rescaling
x\mapsto z=x/r(t)
of
{\mathbb{B}}_{r(t)}^{n}
onto
{\mathbb{B}}_{1}^{n}
gives rise to a family of operators
H_{t}
defined analogously to
B_{t}
by

H_{t}=-(r(t))^{-2}\Delta_{z}(t)+V_{t},\,\text{ where $z\in{\mathbb{B}}_{1}^{n}$, $V_{t}(z)=V(t,r(t)z)$},

(3.10)

the Lagrangian subspace
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}
is obtained from
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
by rescaling as well, and the
z
-Laplacian
-\Delta_{z}(t)
acting in
L^{2}({\mathbb{B}}_{1}^{n};{\mathbb{C}}^{N})
is equipped with the domain

\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{z}(t)):=\big\{w\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}({\mathbb{B}}_{1}^{n}):\mathrm{T}_{t}w:=(\gamma_{{}_{D,\partial{\mathbb{B}}_{1}^{n}}}w,-(r(t))^{-1}\Phi\gamma_{{}_{N,\partial{\mathbb{B}}_{1}^{n}}}w)\in\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\big\}.

(3.11)

The family of operators
H_{t}
can be considered within the setting of Hypothesis
3.4
with
\mathrm{T}_{t}
given in (
3.11
),
V_{t}
given in (
3.10
), and
Q_{t}
being the projection onto
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}
.

\Diamond

Example 3.7
.

The next example is given by a one-parameter family of operators arising in Floquet–Bloch decomposition of periodic Hamiltonians on
{\mathbb{R}}
, see
[
110
, Theorem XII.88]
and Example
4.16
below. We consider the Schrödinger operator
\displaystyle{A:=-\frac{\hbox{\rm d}^{2}}{\hbox{\rm d}x^{2}}+V}
on
(0,1)
with domain
H^{2}_{0}(0,1)
and its sefl-adjoint extensions determined by the following boundary conditions

u(1)=e^{{\bf i}t}u(0),u^{\prime}(1)=e^{{\bf i}t}u^{\prime}(0),t\in[0,2\pi).
In this case the setup described in Hypothesis
3.4
is as follows,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}(0,1),\mathfrak{H}:={\mathbb{C}}^{2},\Gamma_{0}u=(u(0),u(1)),\Gamma_{1}u=(u^{\prime}(0),-u^{\prime}(1)),

(3.12)

\displaystyle A:=-\frac{\hbox{\rm d}^{2}}{\hbox{\rm d}x^{2}},\operatorname{dom}(A)=H^{2}_{0}(0,1),{\mathcal{D}}=H^{2}(0,1);

(3.13)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}):=\{u\in H^{2}(\Omega):u(1)=e^{{\bf i}t}u(0),u^{\prime}(1)=e^{{\bf i}t}u^{\prime}(0)\},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t}):=\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{4}:z_{2}=e^{{\bf i}t}z_{1},z_{3}=-e^{{\bf i}t}z_{4}\}.

(3.15)

\Diamond

Example 3.8
.

This example concerns a
first order
operator related to the perturbed Cauchy–Riemann operator on a two-dimensional infinite cylinder, cf.
[
113
, Section 7]
. Let
a,b:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be smooth functions having limits
a_{\pm}<b_{\pm}
at
\pm\infty
and such that
a(t)<b(t)
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and consider the two-dimensional cylinder

\Omega=\{(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:a(t)<x<b(t),t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}.

For
N\geq 1
we consider the perturbed Cauchy-Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}_{S,{\mathcal{F}}}=\partial_{t}+B_{t}
acting in the space
L^{2}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2N})
of real vector valued functions, where

B_{t}=-J_{N}\partial_{x}(t)+S,t\in{\mathbb{R}},J_{N}=\begin{bmatrix}0&I_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}\\
-I_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}&0\end{bmatrix}\,,

and
S=S(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N\times 2N}
is a given smooth bounded matrix valued function taking symmetric values and having limits
S_{\pm}(x)
as
t\to\pm\infty
. Here and below for each
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
we denote by

\partial_{x}(t)
the operator of
x
-differentiation in
L^{2}\big((a(t),b(t));{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}\big)
with the
domain

\operatorname{dom}(\partial_{x}(t))=\big\{u\in H^{1}\big((a(t),b(t));{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}\big):\mathrm{T}_{t}u:=(u(a(t)),u(b(t)))\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\big\},

(3.16)

where
{\mathcal{F}}:t\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\in\Lambda(2N)
is a given smooth family of Lagrangian subspaces in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4N}
having limits
{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
as
t\to\pm\infty
. Again, we note that the spectral flow of the family
\{B_{t}\}_{t=-\infty}^{+\infty}
of the self-adjoint operators
B_{t}
is of interest since, in particular, it is equal (see, e.g.,
[
65
,
96
]
) to the Fredholm index of the Cauchy-Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}_{S,{\mathcal{F}}}
, see a detailed discussion and various implications of this fact in
[
113
, Section 7]
. Rescaling
u(t,x)\mapsto w(t,z):=u(t,z(b(t)-a(t))+a(t))
,
z\in(0,1)
, gives rise to an analogous to
B_{t}
operator
H_{t}
acting in
L^{2}([0,1];{\mathbb{R}}^{2N})
as

H_{t}=-J_{N}\partial_{z}(t)+V_{t},t\in{\mathbb{R}},z\in(0,1),\text{where $V_{t}(z)=S(t,(b(t)-a(t))z+a(t))$}

(3.17)

and
\partial_{z}(t)=(b(t)-a(t))\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is the operator in
L^{2}([0,1];{\mathbb{R}}^{2N})
with the domain

\operatorname{dom}(\partial_{z}(t))=\big\{w\in H^{1}([0,1];{\mathbb{R}}^{2N})):\mathrm{T}w:=(w(0),w(1))\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\big\}.

The family of operators
H_{t}
can be considered within the setting of Hypothesis
3.4
with the trace given in (
3.16
), with
Q_{t}
being the projection onto
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
, and
V_{t}
given in (
3.17
).

\Diamond

Example 3.9
.

Parameter depended Hamiltonians satisfying Hypothesis
3.4
play an important role in the theory of quantum graphs. For example, the well-known eigenvalue bracketing, see
[
21
, Section 3.1.6]
, is established by studying the dependence of eigenvalues of the
\delta
-type graph Laplacian on the coupling constant. We refer the reader to Section
4.3
for an in-depth discussion of parameter depended quantum graphs satisfying Hypothesis
3.4
.

\Diamond

Remark 3.10
.

Hypothesis
3.4
is satisfied, for example, when
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})\in\Lambda(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
is
({\mathcal{D}},\mathrm{T}_{t})
aligned, cf. Definition
A.4
, and
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
is the operator associated with
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})
and
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})\subset{\mathcal{D}}
,
t\in[0,1]
, see Theorem
A.1
. Conversely, if
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
is a self-adjoint extension of
A
with
\operatorname{dom}(A_{t})\subset{\mathcal{D}}
,
t\in[0,1]
, which is
({\mathcal{D}},\mathrm{T}_{t})
aligned and
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})
is a subspace associated with
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
then
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})\in\Lambda(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
,
t\in[0,1]
, see Theorem
A.2
.

\Diamond

3.2.
Resolvent expansion

Our first major result in the setting of Hypothesis
3.4
is a symplectic formula for the difference of the resolvents
R_{t}(\zeta)=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}

of the operators
H_{t}
at different values of
t
.

Theorem 3.11
.

Assume Hypothesis
3.4
and let

t,s,\tau\in[0,1],

\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})\cup\operatorname{Spec}(H_{s})
. Then for
R_{t}(\zeta):=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
and
H_{t}={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}

one has

\displaystyle R_{t}

\displaystyle(\zeta)-R_{s}(\zeta)=R_{t}({\zeta})(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{s}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{\tau}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J\mathrm{T}_{\tau}R_{s}(\zeta)

(3.18)

\displaystyle=R_{t}({\zeta})(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{s}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}(Q_{t}-Q_{s})J\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{s})R_{s}(\zeta).

(3.19)

The operators whose adjoints enter (
3.18
), (
3.19
) are being considered as elements of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
(cf. Proposition
3.2
) and thus their adjoints are elements of
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},{\mathcal{H}})
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
2.5
for arbitrary
u,v\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathrm{T}_{\tau}=[\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}]^{\top}
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle&R_{t}(\zeta)u-R_{s}(\zeta)u,v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}=\langle R_{t}(\zeta)u-R_{s}(\zeta)u,(H_{t}-\overline{\zeta})R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}\\
&=\langle(H_{t}-\zeta)R_{t}(\zeta)u,R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\langle R_{s}(\zeta)u,(A^{*}+V_{t}-\overline{\zeta})R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}\\
&=\langle u,R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\langle R_{s}(\zeta)u,(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\langle(A^{*}+V_{s}-\zeta)R_{s}(\zeta)u,R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}\\
&\qquad+\langle\Gamma_{1}R_{s}(\zeta)u,\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}R_{s}(\zeta)u,\Gamma_{1}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}\\
&=\langle R_{s}(\zeta)u,(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\langle\Gamma_{1}R_{s}(\zeta)u,\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}R_{s}(\zeta)u,\Gamma_{1}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}\\
&=\left\langle\big(R_{t}({\zeta})(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{s}(\zeta)+(\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\Gamma_{1}R_{s}-(\Gamma_{1}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{s}\big)u,v\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}.\end{split}

Thus

R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{s}(\zeta)=R_{t}({\zeta})(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{s}(\zeta)+(\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\Gamma_{1}R_{s}(\zeta)-(\Gamma_{1}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{s}(\zeta),

yielding (
3.18
).
In order to prove (
3.19
) we note that

\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)=Q_{s}\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)\text{ and }\,\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\zeta)=Q_{t}\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\zeta).

In addition, we have
Q_{s}JQ_{s}=0
since
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{s})
is Lagrangian. This implies

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}_{t}

\displaystyle R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{s}(\zeta)=(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{s})R_{s}(\zeta)

(3.20)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}Q_{t}JQ_{s}\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{s})R_{s}(\zeta)

(3.21)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}(Q_{t}-Q_{s})J\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{s})R_{s}(\zeta).

(3.22)

Utilizing this and letting
\tau=t
in (
3.18
) yields (
3.19
).
∎

Remark 3.12
.

We note that (
3.18
) holds even if
{\mathcal{A}}_{s}
is a non self-adjoint restriction of
A
.

\Diamond

Next, given the one-parameter families of self-adjoint extensions
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
, traces
\mathrm{T}_{t}
and operators
V_{t}
described in Hypothesis
3.4
, we will show that the resolvent operators for
H_{t}={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
are continuous (differentiable) at a given point
t=t_{0}
whenever the mappings
t\mapsto Q_{t}
,
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
are continuous (differentiable) at
t_{0}
.

To introduce appropriate assumptions we recall from Proposition
3.2
(replacing
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
by
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
) that under Hypothesis
3.1
the norms in
{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
are equivalent on
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
for each
t\in[0,1]
, cf. (
3.2
), but with the constant
c
that might depend of
t
. We will need a uniform for
t
near
t_{0}
version of this assertion: In addition to Hypothesis
3.4
we will often assume that, for a given
t_{0}\in[0,1]
, there are constants
C,c>0
such that

c\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{D}}\leq C\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}\text{ for all $u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})$ and $t$ near $t_{0}$.}

(3.23)

These inequalities are equivalent to uniform with respect to the parameter
t
boundedness of the norms of resolvents of
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
as operators from
{\mathcal{H}}
to
{\mathcal{D}}
, see Proposition
3.15
below. We stress that (
3.23
) does
not
mean that the norms
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}}
are equivalent on
{\mathcal{D}}
; they are equivalent only on the domains of the extensions
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
of
A
but uniformly for
t
near
t_{0}
.

Hypothesis 3.13
.

In addition to Hypotheses
3.1
and
3.4
we assume, for a given
t_{0}\in[0,1]
, that

\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}={\mathcal{O}}(1)\text{ as $t\rightarrow t_{0}$}.

(3.24)

Remark 3.14
.

Suppose that
V_{t}
form Hypothesis
3.4
satisfies
V_{t}={\mathcal{O}}(1)
,
t\rightarrow t_{0}
and that
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
. Then (
3.24
) is equivalent to

\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}={\mathcal{O}}(1)\text{ as $t\rightarrow t_{0}$}.

(3.25)

Indeed, we have

({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}=({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}+({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}({\bf i}-\zeta+V_{t})({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}.

(3.26)

Considering
({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}
as a mapping from
{\mathcal{H}}
to
{\mathcal{D}}
,
({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
as a mapping from
{\mathcal{H}}
to itself, and using the bound
\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\leq(|\operatorname{Im}\zeta|)^{-1}
, we infer the claim.

\Diamond

The equivalence of Hypothesis
3.13
and assertion (
3.23
)
is proven next.

Proposition 3.15
.

Assume Hypothesis
3.1
. Then (
3.23
) is equivalent to (
3.24
).

Proof.

If (
3.24
) holds then for any
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
and
t
near
t_{0}
one has

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle=\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})u\|_{\mathcal{D}}\leq c\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})u\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\|{\mathcal{A}}_{t}u\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}})\leq\sqrt{2}c\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}},

thus proving (
3.23
), as
\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}\leq c\|u\|_{{\mathcal{D}}}
by Hypothesis
3.1
.

Conversely, using (
3.23
), for all
t
near
t_{0}
and any
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
one has

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}v\|_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}v\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}

\displaystyle=C\big(\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}v\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}+\|{\mathcal{A}}_{t}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}v\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\|v\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}+(\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}v\|_{{\mathcal{H}}})^{2}\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{5}C\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}},

since
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
is self-adjoint, thus proving (
3.24
).
∎

Assuming that the families
Q_{t}
,
\mathrm{T}_{t}
are continuous at
t=t_{0}
, under Hypothesis
3.13
the resolvent difference formula formula (
3.19
) with
V_{t}=0
shows (as in the proof of Theorem
3.18
(
1
) below) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(1),\\
&\big\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(1).\end{split}

(3.27)

In the proof of differentiability of the resolvent of
H_{t}
we will need, however, a somewhat stronger continuity assumption, given next, regarding the resolvents of
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
considered as operators from
{\mathcal{H}}
to
{\mathcal{D}}
. As we will demonstrate in Sections
4
and
5
below, the stronger assumption does hold in the case of boundary triplets and for Robin-type elliptic partial differential operators on bounded domains.

Hypothesis 3.16
.

In addition to Hypotheses
3.1
and
3.4
we assume that for a given
t_{0}\in[0,1]
one has

\big\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}=o(1),\ t\rightarrow t_{0}.

(3.28)

Remark 3.17
.

Suppose that
V_{t}
from Hypothesis
3.4
satisfies
(V_{t}-V_{t_{0}})=o(1)
,
t\rightarrow t_{0}
and that
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
. Then (
3.28
) is equivalent to

\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}+V_{t_{0}}-\zeta)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}=o(1)\text{ as $t\rightarrow t_{0}$}.

(3.29)

The proof is similar to the proof of Remark
3.14
We also note that (
3.28
) implies (
3.24
).

\Diamond

After these preliminaries we are ready to present the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 3.18
.

We fix
t_{0}\in[0,1]
,
\zeta_{0}\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})
and define

{\mathcal{U}}_{\epsilon}=\{(t,\zeta)\in[0,1]\times{\mathbb{C}}:|t-t_{0}|\leq\epsilon,|\zeta-\zeta_{0}|\leq\epsilon\}\text{ for $\epsilon>0$}.

(1)

Assume Hypothesis
3.13
and suppose that the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
,
t\mapsto Q_{t}
are continuous at
t_{0}
. Then there exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that if
(t,\zeta)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
then
\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})
and
the operator valued function
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta)=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
is continuous at
t_{0}
uniformly for
|\zeta-\zeta_{0}|<\varepsilon
.

(2)

Assume Hypothesis
3.13
and suppose that the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
,
t\mapsto Q_{t}
are Lipschitz continuous at
t_{0}
. Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
(t,\zeta)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
one has

\displaystyle\|R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\leq c|t-t_{0}|.

(3.30)

(3)

Assume Hypothesis
3.16
and suppose that the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
,
t\mapsto Q_{t}
are differentiable at
t_{0}
. Then for some
\varepsilon>0
the following asymptotic expansion holds uniformly for
|\zeta-\zeta_{0}|<\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{t}(\zeta)\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)&+\big(-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\dot{V}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\big)(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}),\ \text{\ in\ }{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}).\end{split}

(3.31)

In particular, the function
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta_{0})=(H_{t}-{\zeta_{0}})^{-1}
is differentiable at
t=t_{0}
and satisfies the following Riccati equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{R}_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})=-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})&+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\\
&+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0}).\end{split}

(3.32)

The operators whose adjoints enter (
3.31
), (
3.32
) are considered as elements of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
, cf. Proposition
3.2
, and their adjoints are elements of
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},{\mathcal{H}})
, the dot denotes the derivative with respect to
t
evaluated at
t_{0}
. We emphasize the generality of formulas (
3.31
)–(
3.32
) where all three objects may vary: the domain of the extension, the trace operator, and the “lower order terms” of the operator itself. In Theorem
3.26
we will give analogous results using a slightly different description of the domains of the self-adjoint extensions. Also, see Theorem
4.14
for the case when the trace operator is
t
-independent. We refer to Remark
3.19
below for somewhat more symmetric versions of the RHS of (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) and to Remark
3.20
for a comment on the continuity and differentiability conditions in the theorem.

Proof.

First, we prove that the mapping
t\mapsto R_{t}({\bf i})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is continuous at
t_{0}
. Hypothesis
3.13
by Remark
3.14
yields

\|R_{t}({\bf i})\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}={\mathcal{O}}(1),t\rightarrow t_{0}.

(3.33)

Using (
3.19
) with
\zeta={\bf i}
,
s=t_{0}
, and (
3.33
) we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{t}({\bf i})-R_{t_{0}}({\bf i})&=R_{t}({{\bf i}})(V_{t_{0}}-V_{t})R_{t_{0}}({\bf i})\\
&\quad+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(-{{\bf i}}))^{*}(Q_{t}-Q_{t_{0}})JQ_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}({\bf i})\\
&\qquad+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(-{{\bf i}}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}})R_{t_{0}}({\bf i})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(1).\end{split}

(3.34)

Proof of (
1
),(
2
)
. Fix
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\subset{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})
. Then by (
3.34
) and
[
109
, Theorem VIII.23]
we have

\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\cap\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})=\emptyset

for
t
sufficiently close to
t_{0}
. Hence,

\sup\{\|R_{t}(\zeta)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}:(t,\zeta)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}\}<\infty

(3.35)

for a sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. We claim that yet a smaller choice of
\varepsilon>0
gives

\sup\{\|R_{t}(\zeta)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}:(t,\zeta)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}\}<\infty.

(3.36)

Indeed, by the resolvent identity one has

R_{t}(\zeta)=R_{t}({\bf i})-({\bf i}-\zeta)R_{t}({\bf i})R_{t}(\zeta).

(3.37)

Using this and (
3.33
), we see that (
3.35
) yields (
3.36
). Next, by (
3.19
) and (
3.36
) we infer

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)&=R_{t}({\zeta})(V_{t_{0}}-V_{t})R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&\quad+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}(Q_{t}-Q_{t_{0}})JQ_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&\qquad+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}})R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&\hskip-28.45274pt\leq c\max\{\|Q_{t}-Q_{t_{0}}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})},\|\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})},\|V_{t}-V_{t_{0}}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\}\end{split}

(3.38)

for some
c>0
and all
(t,\zeta)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
; here we used the inequality

\|\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})}\leq\|\mathrm{T}_{t}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})}\|R_{t}(\overline{\zeta})\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})},

(3.39)

see Proposition
3.2
and Remark
3.3
. Now both assertions
(
1
),(
2
)
follow from (
3.38
).

Proof of (
3
)
. First, we notice that (
3.28
) and the resolvent identity give

\|R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}\rightarrow 0,\ t\rightarrow 0,

(3.40)

uniformly for
|\zeta-\zeta_{0}|<\varepsilon
, with
\varepsilon>0
as above.
Next, by assumptions we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Q_{t}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}Q_{t_{0}}+\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}),\\
&V_{t}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}V_{t_{0}}+\dot{V}_{t_{0}}(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}),\\
&\mathrm{T}_{t}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}+\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).\end{split}

(3.41)

Combining these expansions, (
3.19
), (
3.30
), and (
3.40
) we see that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}(R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)+{\mathcal{O}}(t-t_{0}))(-\dot{V}_{t_{0}}(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}))R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&+\big((\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}+{\mathcal{O}}(t-t_{0}))(R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta})+O_{\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}}(1))\big)^{*}\times\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}))JQ_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&+\big((\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}+{\mathcal{O}}(t-t_{0}))(R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta})+O_{\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})}}(1))\big)^{*}\times\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times J(\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}))R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}\big(-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\dot{V}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}})^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\big)(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}),\end{split}

in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
uniformly for
|\zeta-\zeta_{0}|<\varepsilon
. This shows (
3.31
) which implies (
3.32
).
∎

Remark 3.19
.

The operator
\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
in (
3.31
), (
3.32
) is self-adjoint. Indeed,
since
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})
is Lagrangian, we have
J=JQ_{t}+Q_{t}J
which implies the assertion upon differentiating with respect to
t
. Since
\dot{Q}_{t}J=-J\dot{Q}
we can re-write the term
\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J
in (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) in a more symmetric fashion as

\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J=\frac{1}{2}\big(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J-J\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}).

Furthermore, the identity
Q_{t}JQ_{t}=0
yields

\big(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}J\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)=\big(Q_{t}\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}JQ_{t}\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)=0.

Differentiating this identity at
t=t_{0}
shows that the respective terms in the RHS of (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) could be also re-written as

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\Big((\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})

(3.42)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt-(\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\Big).

(3.43)

\Diamond

Remark 3.20
.

The assumptions of continuity and differentiability of the families
\mathrm{T},V
and
Q
are imposed at a fixed point
t_{0}\in[0,1]
. For many interesting examples these assumptions hold for all
t_{0}\in[0,1]
; a typical situation of this type is described in Example
4.7
. However, these assumptions might fail for some points in
[0,1]
. A typical example of the latter situation is furnished by the classical Hadamard formula setting for star-shaped domains described in Section
5.4
where the trace operator is singular at
t_{0}=0
but is differentiable for each
t_{0}\in(0,1]
.

\Diamond

Remark 3.21
.

Discontinuities of the path
t\mapsto Q_{t}
in general result in discontinuities of the eigenvalues curves. To give an example, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}=-\Delta
be the realization of the Laplacian on a bounded Lipschitz domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n},n\geq 2
, subject to the boundary conditions
\chi_{[0,1/2]}(t)\gamma_{{}_{D}}u+\chi_{(1/2,1]}(t)\gamma_{{}_{N}}u=0
; here
\gamma_{{}_{D}},\gamma_{{}_{N}}
are Dirichlet and Neumann traces, cf. Appendix
C
, and
\chi
is the characteristic function. That is,
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
is the Dirichlet Laplacian for
t\in[0,1/2]
and the Neumann Laplacian for
t\in(1/2,1]
. The corresponding path of Lagrangian planes is piece-wise constant with a jump at
t=1/2
. At this point the boundary conditions change from Dirichlet to Neumann and, due to the celebrated inequality of L. Friedlander
[
62
]
, this produces a non-trivial shift in the spectrum, which, in turn, shows the discontinuities of the eigenvalues. We revisit Friedlander’s Inequality in Example
5.5
below and provide a symplectic proof thereof, cf.
[
46
]
.

3.3.
Hadamard-type variational formulas

In this section, we derive the first order expansion formula for the mapping
t\mapsto P(t)H_{t}P(t)
near
t=t_{0}
. Here, the operator
H_{t}={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
is as in Hypothesis
3.16
and
P(t)
is a spectral projection of
H_{t}
which corresponds to the
\lambda
-group, cf.
[
83
, Section II.5.1]
, consisting of
m
isolated eigenvalues of
H_{t}
bifurcating from the eigenvalue
\lambda=\lambda_{t_{0}}
of multiplicity
m
of the operator
H_{t_{0}}
, see Hypothesis
3.22
below. A subtlety is presented by the fact that the operators
P(t)H_{t}P(t)
act in varying finite-dimensional spaces
\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t))
; we rectify this by means of a unitary mapping
U:\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t_{0}))\rightarrow\text{\rm{ran}}(P_{t})
, as in, e.g.,
[
83
, Section I.4.6]
. After this we use the first order perturbation theory for finite-dimensional operators, cf.
[
83
, Section II.5.4]
, to deduce a formula for the derivative of the eigenvalue curves which we call the
Hadamard-type variational formula
, see (
3.75
). This terminology stems from a classical Rayleigh–Hadamard–Rellich formulas for derivatives of the eigenvalues of Laplacian posted on a parameter-dependent family of domains, cf. Section
5.4
below for details of this particular situation. We note that the approach adopted in this section was originally carried out in
[
95
]
for a specific PDE situation of the one-parameter family of Schrödinger operators with Robin boundary conditions on star-shaped domains mentioned in Example
3.5
.

Hypothesis 3.22
.

For a given
t_{0}\in[0,1]
, we assume that
\lambda=\lambda(t_{0})
is an isolated eigenvalue of
H_{t_{0}}
with finite multiplicity
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let

\gamma:=\big\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:2|z-\lambda|=\operatorname{dist}\big(\lambda,\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})\setminus\{\lambda\}\big)\big\},

and let
B\subset{\mathbb{C}}
denote the disc enclosed by
\gamma
such that
\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})\cap B=\{\lambda\}
.

Throughout this section we assume Hypothesis
3.13
, and that the maps
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t},V_{t},Q_{t}
are continuous at a given
t_{0}\in[0,1]
. By Theorem
3.18
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\gamma
encloses
m
eigenvalues (not necessarily distinct) of the operator
H_{t}
whenever
|t-t_{0}|<\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small. For such
t
we let
P(t)
denote the Riesz projection

P(t):=\frac{-1}{2\pi{\bf i}}\int_{\gamma}R_{t}(\zeta)d\zeta,R_{t}(\zeta)=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}

(3.44)

and recall the reduced resolvent given by

S:=\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}\int_{\gamma}(\zeta-\lambda)^{-1}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)d\zeta

(3.45)

and the identity
P(t_{0})R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)=(\lambda-\zeta)^{-1}P(t_{0})
.

Remark 3.23
.

The Riemann sums defining integrals in (
3.44
), (
3.45
) converge not only in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
but also in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})
. Consequently,
P(t),S\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})
. In addition, one has

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}\int_{\gamma}\mathrm{T}_{t}\big((\zeta-\lambda)^{-1}R_{t}(\zeta)\big)d\zeta

\displaystyle=\mathrm{T}_{t}\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}\int_{\gamma}\big((\zeta-\lambda)^{-1}R_{t}(\zeta)\big)d\zeta=\mathrm{T}_{t}S,

(3.46)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}_{t}P(t))\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}).

(3.47)

This follows from continuity of the mapping
{\mathbb{C}}\ni\zeta\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta)\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{D}})
for every
t\in[0,1]
which can be inferred from

R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{t}(\zeta_{0})=(\zeta-\zeta_{0})R_{t}(\zeta)R_{t}(\zeta_{0}),

(cf. (
3.35
), (
3.36
)), and
\mathrm{T}_{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
.

\Diamond

Next we derive an asymptotic expansion of
P(t)H_{t}P(t)
for
t
near
t_{0}
. To that end, we introduce the operator
D(t):=P(t)-P(t_{0})
satisfying
\|D(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(1)
, which follows from (
3.30
), (
3.44
). In particular, for
t
near
t_{0}
the following operators are well defined

\displaystyle\begin{split}&U(t):=(I-D^{2}(t))^{-1/2}((I-P(t))(I-P(t_{0}))+P(t)P(t_{0})),\\
&U(t)^{-1}=((I-P(t_{0}))(I-P(t))+P(t_{0})P(t))(I-D^{2}(t))^{-1/2},\end{split}

(3.48)

moreover, as in
[
83
, Section I.4.6]
,
[
63
, Proposition 2.18]
, we note that

U(t)P(t_{0})=P(t)U(t),

(3.49)

and that
U(t)
maps
\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t_{0}))
onto
\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t))
unitarily (for
t
near
t_{0}
). Given this auxiliary operators we are ready to expand
P(t)H_{t}P(t)
, which is an
m
-dimensional operator, for
t
near
t_{0}
.

Lemma 3.24
.

For a given
t_{0}\in[0,1]
we assume
that the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
,
t\mapsto Q_{t}
are differentiable at
t_{0}
and that Hypotheses
3.16
and
3.22
hold. Then one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(t_{0})U(t)^{-1}H_{t}P(t)U(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}\lambda P(t_{0})+\Big(P(t_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\\
&\quad-(\mathrm{T}P(t_{0}))^{*}\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}P(t_{0})-(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\Big)(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).\end{split}

(3.50)

Proof.

Our strategy is to expand the left-hand side of (
3.50
) using (
3.31
). Multiplying (
3.31
) by
P(t_{0})
from the right and using identity

R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)P(t_{0})=P(t_{0})R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)=(\lambda-\zeta)^{-1}P(t_{0}),

(3.51)

where
R_{t}(\zeta)=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{t}&(\zeta)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}{(\lambda-\zeta)^{-1}}P(t_{0})+(\lambda-\zeta)^{-1}\Big(-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\dot{V}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\\
&+\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}\,\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})+\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P({t_{0}})\Big)(t-t_{0})\\
&+o(t-t_{0}).\end{split}

(3.52)

The proof is split in several steps.

Step 1.
One has

\displaystyle P(t_{0})P(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}P(t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).

(3.53)

Proof.

For any continuous
F:\gamma\to{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},{\mathcal{H}})
we have

\Big(\int_{\gamma}F(\zeta)\,d\zeta\Big)^{*}=-\int_{\gamma}(F(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\,d\zeta.

Applying this to

F(\zeta)=\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}(\lambda-{\zeta})^{-1}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}({\zeta})
and using
(
3.45
), (
3.46
) yields

\int_{\gamma}\big(\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}(\lambda-\overline{\zeta})^{-1}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}\,d\zeta=\big(-\int_{\gamma}\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}(\lambda-\zeta)^{-1}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta)\,d\zeta\big)^{*}=(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}S)^{*}.

We use this, multiply both sides of (
3.52
) by
-\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}
and integrate over
\gamma
to obtain the following,

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(t)P(t_{0})&\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}P(t_{0})+\Big(-S\dot{V}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})+\left(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}S\right)^{*}\,\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\\
&\qquad+\left(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}S\right)^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P({t_{0}})\Big)(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).\end{split}

(3.54)

Taking adjoints we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(t_{0})P(t)&\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}P(t_{0})+\Big(-P(t_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}S+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0}))^{*}\,\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}S\\
&\qquad+\big(\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P({t_{0}})\big)^{*}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}S\Big)(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).\end{split}

(3.55)

Multiplying this by
P(t_{0})
from the right and using
SP(t_{0})=0
we arrive at (
3.53
).
∎

Step 2.
One has

\displaystyle P(t_{0})U(t)P(t_{0})

\displaystyle=(P(t_{0})U^{-1}(t)P(t_{0}))^{*}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}P(t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}),

(3.56)

\displaystyle(I-P(t_{0}))U(t)P(t_{0})

\displaystyle=(P(t_{0})U(t)^{-1}(I-P(t_{0})))^{*}

(3.57)

\displaystyle\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}(I-P(t_{0}))\Big(-S\dot{V}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})+\left(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}S\right)^{*}\,\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})

(3.58)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\left(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}S\right)^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P({t_{0}})\Big)(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).

(3.59)

Proof.

First we note an auxiliary expansion

D(t)\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}{\mathcal{O}}(t-t_{0})
which follows
from (
3.30
), (
3.44
) and formula
D(t)=P(t)-P(t_{0})
.
Thus

(I-D^{2}(t))^{-1/2}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}I+{\mathcal{O}}(|t-t_{0}|^{2})

and then

\displaystyle\begin{split}U(t)&=(I-D^{2}(t))^{-1/2}((I-P(t))(I-P(t_{0}))+P(t)P(t_{0}))\\
&\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}((I-P(t))(I-P(t_{0}))+P(t)P(t_{0}))+o(t-t_{0}).\end{split}

(3.60)

Using this and (
3.53
) we obtain

P(t_{0})U(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}P(t_{0})P(t)P(t_{0})+o(t-t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}P(t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).

(3.61)

Similarly, employing (
3.60
) one infers

\displaystyle(I-P(t_{0}))U(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}(I-P(t_{0}))P(t)P(t_{0})+o(t-t_{0})

(3.62)

and thus (
3.59
) follows by multiplying (
3.54
)
by
I-P(t_{0})
from the left.
∎

Step 3.
One has

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(t_{0})&U^{-1}(t)R_{t}(\zeta)U(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}(\lambda-\zeta)^{-1}P(t_{0})\\
&+(\lambda-\zeta)^{-2}\Big(-P(t_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})+\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\big)^{*}\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\\
&+\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\big)^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P({t_{0}})\Big)(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}).\end{split}

(3.63)

Proof.

First, we sandwich the middle term in the left-hand side,
R_{t}(\zeta)
, by
P(t_{0})+(I-P(t_{0}))
and write

\displaystyle P(t_{0})U^{-1}(t)R_{t}(\zeta)U(t)P(t_{0})=I+II+III+IV.

(3.64)

Let us treat each term individually, starting with

\displaystyle I:=P(t_{0})U^{-1}(t)(I-P(t_{0}))

\displaystyle\times(I-P(t_{0}))R_{t}(\zeta)P(t_{0})

(3.65)

\displaystyle\times P(t_{0})U(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(t-t_{0}),

(3.66)

by (
3.52
), (
3.56
), (
3.59
)
as the main terms in the RHS of (
3.52
) and (
3.59
) both contain the factor
(t-t_{0})
. Similarly, we infer

\displaystyle II:=P(t_{0})U^{-1}(t)P(t_{0})

\displaystyle\times P(t_{0})R_{t}(\zeta)(I-P(t_{0}))

(3.67)

\displaystyle\times(I-P(t_{0}))U(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(t-t_{0}),

(3.68)

by (
3.52
), (
3.56
), (
3.59
), and

\displaystyle III:=

\displaystyle P(t_{0})U^{-1}(t)(I-P(t_{0}))\times R_{t}(\zeta)

(3.69)

\displaystyle\times(I-P(t_{0}))U(t)P(t_{0})\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(t-t_{0}),

(3.70)

by (
3.59
). The last term admits the required in (
3.63
) expansion because

\displaystyle IV:=P(t_{0})U^{-1}(t)P(t_{0})\times P(t_{0})R_{t}(\zeta)P(t_{0})\times P(t_{0})U(t)P(t_{0})

(3.71)

and we can use (
3.52
), identity (
3.51
) and (twice)(
3.56
).
∎

Step 4.
Recalling the identities

H_{t}P(t):=\frac{-1}{2\pi{\bf i}}\int_{\gamma}\zeta R_{t}(\zeta)d\zeta,\quad\frac{1}{2\pi{\bf i}}\int_{\gamma}\zeta(\lambda-\zeta)^{-2}d\zeta=1,

(3.72)

multiplying (
3.63
) by
-\zeta/2\pi{\bf i}
and then integrating over
\gamma
we arrive at (
3.50
)
∎

We are ready to present the main result of this section that gives a formula for the slopes of the appropriately chosen branches of the eigenvalues curves bifurcating from an isolated eigenvalue of finite multiplicity. We recall that our assumptions on differentiability of
\mathrm{T},V
and
Q
is imposed at a particular point
t_{0}
where
\lambda=\lambda(t_{0})
is the isolated eigenvalue, cf. Remark
3.20
. To avoid confusions we also refer to the classical Rellich’s example
[
83
, Example V.4.14]
recalled as Example
4.8
below to emphasize that we are not claiming global differentiability of all eigenvalue curves. Indeed, in this example there is a point
t_{0}
where one eigenvalue curve has a singularity and so our assumptions do not hold while all others curves are differentiable as claimed in the theorem.

Theorem 3.25
.

Assume Hypotheses
3.16
and
3.22
and suppose that the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
,
t\mapsto Q_{t}
are differentiable at
t_{0}
. We introduce the operator

T^{(1)}:=P(t_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})-(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0}))^{*}\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})-(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0}),

(3.73)

and denote the eigenvalues and the orthonormal eigenvectors of this
m
dimensional operator by
\{\lambda^{(1)}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
and
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t_{0}))=\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda)
correspondingly
5
5

5

We stress that
u_{j}
are eigenvectors of
H_{t_{0}}
corresponding to its eigenvalue
\lambda=\lambda(t_{0})
.
. Then there exists a labeling of the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
H_{t}
, for
t
near
t_{0}
, satisfying the asymptotic formula

\lambda_{j}(t)\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}\lambda+\lambda_{j}^{(1)}(t-t_{0})+o(t-t_{0}),

(3.74)

moreover, one has

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\langle\dot{V}_{t_{0}}u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\omega(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})+\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j}),

(3.75)

for each
1\leq j\leq m
.

Proof.

Recalling that
U(t)
is a unitary map between
\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t_{0}))
and
\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t))
, see
[
83
, Section I.4.6]
,
[
63
, Proposition 2.18]
, we note that
H_{t}\upharpoonright_{\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t))}
is similar to

P(t_{0})U(t)^{-1}H_{t}P(t)U(t)P(t_{0})\upharpoonright_{\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t_{0}))}

for
t
near
t_{0}
. In particular the eigevalues of these operators coincide and it is sufficient to expand the eigenvalues of the latter. To that end we utilize the expansion (
3.50
) together with the finite dimensional first order perturbation theory, specifically,
[
83
, Theorem II.5.11]
, to deduce (
3.74
). Next, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\lambda_{j}^{(1)}=\langle T^{(1)}u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}\\
&=\langle\big(P(t_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})-(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0}))^{*}\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})-(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}P(t_{0})\big)u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}\\
&=\langle\dot{V}_{t_{0}}u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})-\omega(\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})\\
&=\langle\dot{V}_{t_{0}}u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\omega(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})+\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j})\end{split}

(3.76)

which gives (
3.75
). In the last step we used the inclusions

\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}\text{ and }\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}.

The latter inclusion follows from
\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t}u_{j})=0
after differentiating at
t=t_{0}
. To prove the former inclusion we use
JQ_{t}+Q_{t}J=J
to get
J\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}=-\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}J
and write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})=\langle J\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}}\\
&\quad=-\langle J\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}}\\
&\quad=-\omega(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})=\overline{\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})},\end{split}

(3.77)

as claimed.∎

In PDE and quantum graph settings the Lagrangian planes are often defined by operators
[X,Y]
as in (
2.15
)–(
2.17
) rather than by orthogonal projections onto these planes. It is therefore natural to restate (
3.31
), (
3.75
) in these terms which we do next. Given families
t\mapsto X_{t},Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H})
, we will now denote by
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
the self-adjoint extension of
A
with
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}):=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}:\,[X_{t},Y_{t}]\mathrm{T}_{t}u=0\}
, that is, we augment (
3.4
) by requiring that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\overline{\mathrm{T}_{t}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}))}=\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})=\operatorname{ker}([X_{t},Y_{t}]),\\
&X_{t},Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H});X_{t}Y_{t}^{*}=Y_{t}X_{t}^{*},0\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(M^{X_{t},Y_{t}}),\end{split}

(3.78)

where
M^{X_{t},Y_{t}}
is defined in (
2.17
). We recall formula (
2.19
) for the projection
Q_{t}
onto
\operatorname{ker}([X_{t}.Y_{t}])
. A typical example of
X_{t},Y_{t}
are given by
X_{t}=I
and
Y_{t}=-\Theta_{t}
where
\Theta_{t}
is an operator (in general, not local) entering the Robbin boundary condition.

Theorem 3.26
.

Under Hypothesis
3.4
, if
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
satisfies (
3.78
) then the following symplectic resolvent difference formula holds
for the resolvent
R_{t}(\zeta)=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
of the operator
H_{t}={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{s}(\zeta)&=R_{t}({\zeta})(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{s}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\,Z_{t,s}\mathrm{T}_{s}\,R_{s}(\zeta)\\
&\quad+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{s})R_{s}(\zeta),\end{split}

(3.79)

where
\zeta\not\in(\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})\cup\operatorname{Spec}(H_{s}))
,
s,t\in[0,1]
, and the operator
Z_{t,s}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
is given by formula (
2.20
),

Z_{t,s}:=\big(W(X_{t},Y_{t})\big)^{*}(X_{t}{Y}_{s}^{*}-Y_{t}{X}_{s}^{*})\big(W(X_{s},Y_{s})\big).

(3.80)

Moreover, under Hypothesis
3.13
, if the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t},V_{t},X_{t},Y_{t}
are continuous at
t_{0}\in[0,1]
in the respective spaces of operators, then the function
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta_{0})
is continuous at
t=t_{0}
for
any
\zeta_{0}\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})
.
Further, assume Hypothesis
3.16
and suppose that the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t},V_{t},X_{t},Y_{t}
are differentiable at
t_{0}\in[0,1]
. Then the function
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta_{0})=(H_{t}-\zeta_{0})^{-1}
is differentiable at
t=t_{0}
and satisfies the following Riccati equation,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dot{R}_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})=-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\\
&+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}\big(W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\big)^{*}(\dot{X}_{t_{0}}{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\big(W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\big)\times\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\\
&+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0}),\quad\zeta_{0}\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}}).\end{split}

(3.81)

Furthermore, if
\lambda(t_{0})\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})
is an isolated eigenvalue of multiplicity
m\geq 1
then there exists a choice of orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda(t_{0}))
and a labeling of the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
H_{t}
, for
t
near
t_{0}
, such that the following Hadamard-type formula holds,

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\langle\dot{V}_{t_{0}}u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\big\langle(X_{t_{0}}\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-Y_{t_{0}}\dot{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\phi_{j},\phi_{j}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}+\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j}),\

(3.82)

where we denote
\phi_{j}=W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, with the operator
W
defined in (
2.20
), or, equivalently,
\phi_{j}
is a unique vector in
\mathfrak{H}
satisfying

\Gamma_{0}u_{j}=-Y_{t_{0}}^{*}\phi_{j}\,\text{ and }\,\Gamma_{1}u_{j}=X^{*}_{t_{0}}\phi_{j}.

(3.83)

Proof.

The resolvent difference formula (
3.79
) follows from (
3.19
) and the computation

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}(Q_{t}-Q_{s})J\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}Q_{t}JQ_{s}\mathrm{T}_{s}R_{s}(\zeta)

(3.84)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\,Z_{t,s}\mathrm{T}_{s}\,R_{s}(\zeta).

(3.85)

Hypothesis
3.13
and (
3.79
) imply continuity of
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta)
as in the proof of Theorem
3.18
.
To prove (
3.81
) we remark that

X_{t}Y_{s}^{*}-Y_{t}X_{s}^{*}=(X_{t}-X_{s})Y_{s}^{*}-(Y_{t}-Y_{s})X_{s}^{*}
by (
2.16
). Plugging this in (
3.80
), using (
3.79
) at
s=t_{0}
, dividing by
(t-t_{0})
and passing to the limit as
t\to t_{0}
yields (
3.81
). Next we turn to (
3.82
). We recall that
u_{j}
in Theorem
3.25
are the eigenvectors in
\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t_{0}))
such that
T^{(1)}u_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{(1)}u_{j}
. But since
\text{\rm{ran}}(P(t_{0}))=\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda(t_{0}))
the vectors
u_{j}
are also eigenvectors of
H_{t_{0}}
such that
H_{t_{0}}u_{j}=\lambda(t_{0})u_{j}
. By (
3.75
) we only need to show

\omega(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})=\big\langle(X_{t_{0}}\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-Y_{t_{0}}\dot{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\phi_{j},\phi_{j}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(3.86)

Using (
2.19
) and differentiating
Q_{t}
we infer

\displaystyle\omega(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})

(3.87)

\displaystyle\quad=\omega\Big([-Y^{*}_{t_{0}},X^{*}_{t_{0}}]^{\top}\big(\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{{t=t_{0}}}W(X_{t},Y_{t})\big)\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\Big)

(3.88)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\omega\Big(\big(\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{{t=t_{0}}}[-Y^{*}_{t_{0}},X^{*}_{t_{0}}]^{\top}\big)W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\Big)

(3.89)

\displaystyle\quad=\Big\langle\Big(\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{{t=t_{0}}}W(X_{t},Y_{t})\Big)\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},[X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}}]\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}

(3.90)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\omega\Big(\Big(\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{{t=t_{0}}}[-Y^{*}_{t_{0}},X^{*}_{t_{0}}]^{\top}\Big)W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\Big)

(3.91)

\displaystyle\quad=\omega\Big(\Big(\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}\Big|_{{t=t_{0}}}[-Y^{*}_{t_{0}},X^{*}_{t_{0}}]^{\top}\Big)W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\Big).

(3.92)

where we used
[X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}}]\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}=0
. Finally, employing (
2.19
) and

\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}=Q_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}=[-Y_{t_{0}}^{*},X_{t_{0}}^{*}]^{\top}\phi_{j},\,\,\phi_{j}:=W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},

(3.93)

we obtain

\displaystyle\omega(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})

\displaystyle=\Big\langle[\dot{X}_{t_{0}}^{*},\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}]^{\top}\phi_{j},[-Y_{t_{0}}^{*},X_{t_{0}}^{*}]^{\top}\phi_{j}\Big\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}

(3.94)

\displaystyle=\big\langle(X_{t_{0}}\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-Y_{t_{0}}\dot{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\phi_{j},\phi_{j}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}},

(3.95)

thus completing the proof of (
3.82
), while (
3.83
) follows from (
3.93
).∎

Remark 3.27
.

We close with a remark that assertions proved in Theorem
3.26
allow one to make conclusions regarding the behavior of the spectra of the operators
H_{t}
as a function of
t
, see, e.g.,
[
109
, Theorem VIII.23]
. Also, the results of this section can be used to study various properties of strongly continuous semigroups generated by the operators
-H_{t}
. For instance, the Trotter-Kato Approximation Theorem, see, e.g.,
[
58
, Theorem III.4.8 ]
, implies that the semigroups are continuous with respect to the parameter
t
as soon as the continuity of the resolvent of
H_{t}
in Theorem
3.26
is established, see Section
5.3
for an example.

\Diamond

4.
boundary triplets

boundary triplets have been intensively studied since probably
[
34
,
71
]
, see the vast bibliography in and related papers and the bibliography therein.
In this section we revisit main results of Sections
2
and
3
in the context of boundary triplets and present several applications.
The case of boundary triplets is the one that is widely considered in the literature, and in this section we will see that for this case one may impose fewer assumptions to prove the same set of general results. Also, we will demonstrate that this case is sufficient to cover many interesting applications. In particular, we show that conclusions of Theorems
3.18
,
3.25
,
3.26
hold under a mere assumption that the mappings
t\mapsto Q_{t}
,
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
are continuous (differentiable) with respect to
t
and that
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0,t},\Gamma_{1,t})
is an boundary triplet. Utilizing this, we derive Hadamard-type formulas for quantum graphs, Schrödinger operators with singular potentials, and Robin realizations of the Laplace operator on bounded domains.

We recall the following widely used definition, cf. .

Definition 4.1
.

Given a symmetric densely defined closed operator
A
on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
with equal deficiency indices, we equip
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}=\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})
with the graph scalar product and consider linear operators
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{1}
acting from
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
to a (boundary) Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
. We say that

(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is a
boundary triplet
if the operator
\mathrm{T}:=(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}):{\mathcal{H}}_{+}\to\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
is surjective
and the following abstract Green identity holds,

\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}=\langle\Gamma_{1}u,\Gamma_{0}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}u,\Gamma_{1}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}\text{ for all $u,v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{+}$}.

(4.1)

In other words,
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is an boundary triplet provided Hypothesis
2.1
holds with
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{H}}_{+}
and surjective
\mathrm{T}
. In this case, we have
\mathrm{T}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
by Proposition
2.2
(2).

Remark 4.2
.

The setting of boundary triplets gives a particularly simple illustration of Corollary
A.5
. Specifically, if
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is a boundary triplet associated with
A
then
{\mathcal{F}}\subset\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
is Lagrangian if and only if
{\mathcal{A}}:=A^{*}|_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
is self-adjoint. In other words, the Lagrangian plane
{\mathcal{F}}
and the self-adjoint operator
{\mathcal{A}}:=A^{*}|_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
are automatically aligned in the sense of Definition
A.4
as long as
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is a boundary triplet. In particular, if
{\mathcal{A}}
is a self-adjoint extension of
A
then the subspace
\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}))
is closed, cf.
[
119
, Lemma 14.6(iii)]
.

\Diamond

4.1.
Main results for the case of boundary triplets

In this section we discuss our main results, Theorems
3.18
,
3.26
, in the context of boundary triplets. In Proposition 4.5 we verify that Hypothesis
3.16
(and, hence, Hypothesis
3.13
) holds automatically for boundary triplets. This allows us to obtain the central result of the current section, Theorem
4.5
. The latter, in turn, gives a plethora of applications discussed in Sections
4.2
,
4.3
,
7
,
4.5
.

In the setting of boundary triplets Hypothesis
3.4
should be naturally replaced by the following assumption.

Hypothesis 4.3
.

Let

\mathrm{T}:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}):t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}:=[\Gamma_{0t},\Gamma_{1t}]^{\top}

be a one-parameter family of trace operators. Suppose that
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0t},\Gamma_{1t})
is an boundary triplet for each
t\in[0,1]
. Let
Q:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}),t\mapsto Q_{t}
be a one-parameter family of orthogonal projections. Suppose that
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})\in\Lambda(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
is a Lagrangian plane for each
t\in[0,1]
. Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
be a family of self-adjoint extensions of
A
satisfying

\displaystyle{\mathrm{T}_{t}\big(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})\big)}=\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t}).

(4.2)

Let

V:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}):t\mapsto V_{t}
be a one-parameter family of self-adjoint bounded operators. We denote
H_{t}:={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
and
R_{t}(\zeta):=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
for
\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})
and
t\in[0,1]
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Suppose that Hypothesis
4.3
holds for the boundary triplet

(\mathfrak{H},{\Gamma_{0}}_{t},{\Gamma_{1}}_{t})
. If
Q
and
\mathrm{T}
are continuous at a given
t_{0}\in[0,1]
then

\big\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})}=o(1),\ t\rightarrow t_{0}.

(4.3)

In other words, Hypothesis
3.16
is automatically satisfied for the boundary triplets.

Proof.

We claim that

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})}\leq\sqrt{2}\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}.

(4.4)

Indeed, using
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}\subset A^{*}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}\subset A^{*}
we get

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}h-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}h\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}^{2}=\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}h-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}h\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\|A^{*}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}h-A^{*}({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}h\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}=2\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}h-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}h\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}.

(4.6)

Thus it is enough to prove that the right-hand side of (
4.4
) is
o(1)
as
t\to t_{0}
. To this end, we first note that, given
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}u+{\bf i}u=f,u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
, we have

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+{\bf i})^{-1}f\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}=\|u\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}=\|A^{*}u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}+\|u\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\quad=\|{\mathcal{A}}_{t}u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}+\|u\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}=\|{\mathcal{A}}_{t}u+{\bf i}u\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}}=\|f\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{H}}};

(4.8)

hence,

\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})}\leq 1.

(4.9)

By resolvent difference formula (
2.23
) we infer

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\\
&\quad=\|(\mathrm{T}_{t}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+{\bf i})^{-1})^{*}(Q_{t}-Q_{t_{0}})J\mathrm{T}_{t}({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}+{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\\
&\quad\leq\|\mathrm{T}_{t}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})}\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})}\|(Q_{t}-Q_{t_{0}})\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})}\times\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\times\|\mathrm{T}_{t}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})}\|({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}+{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})}\\
&\quad\leq c\|Q_{t}-Q_{t_{0}}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})}\underset{t\rightarrow t_{0}}{=}o(1),c>0,\end{split}

(4.10)

where we used (
4.9
), and continuity of
Q
and
\mathrm{T}
at
t_{0}
. Then (
4.4
), (
4.10
) yield (
4.3
) and so equation (
3.28
) in Hypothesis
3.16
holds.
∎

We summarize our main results for the case of boundary triplets as follows.

Theorem 4.5
.

Assume Hypothesis
4.3
. If
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
is defined as in (
3.78
) and
H_{t}={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
then for
R_{t}(\zeta)=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
the following resolvent difference formula holds,

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{s}(\zeta)&=R_{t}({\zeta})(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{s}(\zeta)+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\,Z_{t,s}\mathrm{T}_{s}\,R_{s}(\zeta)\\
&\quad+(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{s})R_{s}(\zeta),\end{split}

(4.11)

where
\zeta\not\in(\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})\cup\operatorname{Spec}(H_{s}))
,
s,t\in[0,1]
and

Z_{t,s}:=\big(W(X_{t},Y_{t})\big)^{*}(X_{t}{Y}_{s}^{*}-Y_{t}{X}_{s}^{*})\big(W(X_{s},Y_{s})\big),

(4.12)

with the operator
W
defined in (
2.20
). Moreover, if the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t},V_{t},X_{t},Y_{t}
are continuous at
t_{0}\in[0,1]
in the respective spaces of operators, then the function
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta_{0})
is continuous at
t=t_{0}
for
any
\zeta_{0}\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})
.
Further, if the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t},V_{t},X_{t},Y_{t}
are differentiable at
t_{0}\in[0,1]
, then the function
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta_{0})=(H_{t}-\zeta_{0})^{-1}
is differentiable. In this case, the following two assertions hold:

(1) The resolvent operators satisfy the following differential equation,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dot{R}_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})=-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\\
&+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}\big(W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\big)^{*}(\dot{X}_{t_{0}}{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\big(W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\big)\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\\
&+(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}J\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0}),\quad\zeta_{0}\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}}).\end{split}

(4.13)

(2) If
\lambda(t_{0})\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})
is an isolated eigenvalue of multiplicity
m\geq 1
then there exists a choice of orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda(t_{0}))
and a labeling of the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
H_{t}
, for
t
near
t_{0}
, such that

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\langle\dot{V}_{t_{0}}u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\big\langle(X_{t_{0}}\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-Y_{t_{0}}\dot{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\phi_{j},\phi_{j}\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}+\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j}),\

(4.14)

where
\phi_{j}=W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, or, equivalently,
\phi_{j}
is a unique vector in
\mathfrak{H}
satisfying

\Gamma_{0}u_{j}=-Y_{t_{0}}^{*}\phi_{j}\,\text{ and }\,\Gamma_{1}u_{j}=X^{*}_{t_{0}}\phi_{j}.

(4.15)

Proof.

The resolvent difference formula (
4.11
) follows directly from (
3.79
). The continuity of
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta_{0})
at
t_{0}
follows from Theorem
3.26
upon noticing that Hypothesis
3.13
holds in the setting of boundary triplets by Proposition
4.4
. Similarly, Proposition
4.4
combined with (
3.81
), (
3.82
), yield (
4.13
), (
4.14
).
∎

Remark 4.6
.

(1)  In the setting of Theorem
4.5
, the resolvent difference formula (
4.11
) can be also rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{s}(\zeta)&={\mathcal{R}}_{t}({\zeta})(V_{s}-V_{t})R_{s}(\zeta)+{\mathcal{R}}_{t}({\zeta})\mathrm{T}_{t}^{*}\,Z_{t,s}\mathrm{T}_{s}\,R_{s}(\zeta)\\
&\quad+{\mathcal{R}}_{t}({\zeta})\mathrm{T}_{t}^{*}J(\mathrm{T}_{t}-\mathrm{T}_{s})R_{s}(\zeta),\end{split}

(4.16)

where in the RHS we have
{\mathcal{R}}_{t}({\zeta})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{-},{\mathcal{H}})
, that is, as in Proposition
2.10
and Corollary
2.11
, we view
{\mathcal{R}}_{t}({\zeta})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{-},{\mathcal{H}})
as a unique extension of
R_{t}({\zeta})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
to an element of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{-},{\mathcal{H}})
, while
\mathrm{T}_{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
,
\mathrm{T}_{t}^{*}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},{\mathcal{H}}_{-})
. We note that, in a more general setting of Theorem
3.26
, the trace operator
\mathrm{T}_{t}
is unbounded and one only has the inclusion
(\mathrm{T}_{t}R_{t}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\supseteq R_{t}({\zeta})(\mathrm{T}_{t})^{*}
. In this case, (
4.16
) holds provided
\text{\rm{ran}}(Z_{t,s}\mathrm{T}_{s}\,R_{s}(\zeta))\subseteq J\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})
.

(2)  The resolvent difference formula derived in Theorem
4.5
yields continuity of the mapping
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H})\times{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H})\ni(X,Y)\mapsto({\mathcal{A}}_{X,Y}-{\bf i})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
; here, for an boundary triplet
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
, we denote by
{\mathcal{A}}_{X,Y}
the self-adjoint extension of
A
such that
\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{X,Y}))=\operatorname{ker}([X,Y])
, cf. (
3.78
).

\Diamond

In sections
4.2
,
4.3
,
7
,
4.5
below we will give applications of Theorem
4.5
for several important classes of problems that fit the framework of the boundary triplets. To give the simplest possible illustration of the setup described in Hypothesis
4.3
and of Theorem
4.5
we now consider the following two ODE examples where the conclusions of the theorem are probably well-known, see, e.g.,
[
10
,
40
,
41
,
83
]
and the vast bibliography therein.

Example 4.7
.

Let
Au=-u^{\prime\prime}
be the minimal symmetric operator on
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}(0,1)
with domain
\operatorname{dom}(A)=H^{2}_{0}(0,1)
so that
A^{*}u=-u^{\prime\prime}
with
\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})={\mathcal{H}}_{+}=H^{2}(0,1)
, set
\mathfrak{H}={\mathbb{C}}^{2}

and introduce the surjective trace operator
\mathrm{T}=(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
using the Dirichlet and (inward) Neumann traces
\Gamma_{0}u=[u(0),u(1)]^{\top}
and
\Gamma_{1}u=[u^{\prime}(0),-u^{\prime}(1)]^{\top}
. Integration by parts yields (
4.1
), and thus
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is an boundary triplet, cf.
[
119
, Section 14.4]
. For
t\in[0,1]
we let
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
denote the self-adjoint extension of
A
with the domain

\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})=\{u\in H^{2}(0,1):\cos({\pi}t/2)\Gamma_{0}u-\sin({\pi}t/2)\Gamma_{1}u=0\}=\operatorname{ker}([X_{t},Y_{t}]),

(4.17)

where, cf. (
3.78
),

X_{t}=\cos({\pi}t/2)I_{2},\,Y_{t}=-\sin({\pi}t/2)I_{2},\,Q_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}\sin^{2}(\pi t/2)&\frac{1}{2}\sin(\pi t)\\
\frac{1}{2}\sin(\pi t)&\cos^{2}(\pi t/2)\end{bmatrix}.

Given a bounded real-valued potential
V
, we
let
H_{t}u=-u^{\prime\prime}+Vu
,
t\in[0,1]
, be the family of scalar Schrödinger operators on
L^{2}(0,1)
equipped with the boundary conditions
specified in (
4.17
) so that Hypothesis
4.3
holds.
In particular,
H_{0}
is the Dirichlet and
H_{1}
is the Neumann Schrödinger operator.
To apply Theorem
4.5
we first perform a standard calculation of the resolvent
R_{t}(\zeta)=(H_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
, cf., e.g.,
[
122
, Lemma 9.7]
: For
t\in[0,1]
and
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}
we let
v_{t}(\cdot\,;\zeta)
,
w_{t}(\cdot\,;\zeta)
denote the solutions to the equation
-u^{\prime\prime}+Vu=\zeta u
that satisfy the initial conditions

\displaystyle(v_{t}(0;\zeta),v^{\prime}_{t}(0,\zeta))

\displaystyle=\big(\sin({\pi}t/2),\cos({\pi}t/2)\big),

\displaystyle(w_{t}(1;\zeta),w^{\prime}_{t}(1,\zeta))

\displaystyle=\big(\sin({\pi}t/2),-\cos({\pi}t/2)\big),

and let
{\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\zeta)=v_{t}(x;\zeta)w^{\prime}_{t}(x;\zeta)-v^{\prime}_{t}(x;\zeta)w_{t}(x;\zeta)
denote their Wronskian. Then for each
u\in L^{2}(0,1)
the function
R_{t}(\zeta)u
is given by the formula

\big(R_{t}(\zeta)u\big)(x)=({\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\zeta))^{-1}\Big(w_{t}(x;\zeta)\int_{0}^{x}v_{t}(y;\zeta)u(y){\rm d}y+v_{t}(x;\zeta)\int_{x}^{1}w_{t}(y;\zeta)u(y){\rm d}y\Big),

x\in[0,1]
.Using this, it is convenient to write
\mathrm{T}R_{t}(\zeta)=K_{t}(\zeta)L_{t}(\zeta)
where we temporarily introduced the
(4\times 2)
matrix
K_{t}(\zeta)
and the operator
L_{t}(\zeta)
by the formulas

\displaystyle K_{t}(\zeta)=({\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\zeta))^{-1}\big[\sin({\pi}t/2)I_{2},\cos({\pi}t/2)I_{2}\big]^{\top},

(4.18)

\displaystyle L_{t}(\zeta)u=\big[\langle w_{t}(\cdot;\zeta),\overline{u}\rangle_{L^{2}},\langle v_{t}(\cdot;\zeta),\overline{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}\big]^{\top},L_{t}(\zeta)\in{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(0,1),{\mathbb{C}}^{2})

(4.19)

so that
(L_{t}(\zeta))^{*}
maps
(z_{1},z_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
into
w_{t}(\cdot\,;\overline{\zeta})z_{1}+v_{t}(\cdot\,;\overline{\zeta})z_{2}\in L^{2}(0,1)
. Theorem
4.5
and a short calculation now yield

\displaystyle\big(

\displaystyle R_{t}(\zeta)-R_{s}(\zeta)\big)u=({\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\zeta){\mathcal{W}}_{s}(\zeta))^{-1}\sin(\pi(t-s)/2)

(4.20)

\displaystyle\times\big(\langle w_{s}(\cdot;\zeta),\overline{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}w_{t}(\cdot;\zeta)+\langle v_{s}(\cdot;\zeta),\overline{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}v_{t}(\cdot;\zeta)\big),\,\zeta\notin\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})\cup\operatorname{Spec}(H_{s}),

(4.21)

\displaystyle\dot{R}_{t}(\zeta)u

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}({\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\zeta))^{-2}\big(\langle w_{t}(\cdot;\zeta),\overline{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}w_{t}(\cdot;\zeta)+\langle v_{t}(\cdot;\zeta),\overline{u}\rangle_{L^{2}}v_{t}(\cdot;\zeta)\big),

(4.22)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\zeta\notin\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t}),

(4.23)

\displaystyle\dot{\lambda}(t_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi}{2}\big\|\sin(\pi t_{0}/2)\Gamma_{0}u_{0}+\cos(\pi t_{0}/2)\Gamma_{1}u_{0}\big\|^{2}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}},\,t_{0}\in[0,1],

(4.24)

where
u_{0}
is the normalized eigenfunction corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda(t_{0})\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t_{0}})
.

\Diamond

Example 4.8
.

As promised prior to Theorem
3.25
, we now recall the classical Rellich’s example, cf., e.g.,
[
83
, Example V.4.14]
which
shows the singularity at
t_{0}=0
of the smallest eigenvalue
\lambda^{(0)}(t)
of the operator
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}=-\partial^{2}_{xx}
in
L^{2}(0,1)
equipped with the boundary conditions
u(0)=0
,
tu^{\prime}(1)=u(1)
for real
t
; meanwhile, the resolvent
t\mapsto({\mathcal{A}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}
is continuous and all other eigenvalues
\lambda^{(k)}(t)
,
k=1,2,\dots
, are differentiable for each
t
including
t=0
, see
[
83
, Fig. 1, p.292]
. Indeed, letting
\Gamma_{0}u=(u(0),u(1))^{\top}
,
\Gamma_{1}u=(u^{\prime}(0),-u^{\prime}(1))^{\top}
for
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{+}:=H^{2}(0,1)
,
