\mathfrak{H}={\mathbb{C}}^{2}
and

X_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},\,Y_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&-t\end{bmatrix},\,Q_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
0&t^{2}(1+t^{2})^{-1}&0&-t(1+t^{2})^{-1}\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&-t(1+t^{2})^{-1}&0&(1+t^{2})^{-1}\end{bmatrix},

(4.25)

we notice that
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})=\operatorname{ker}([X_{t}\,Y_{t}])=\text{\rm{ran}}Q_{t}
. The maps
t\mapsto X_{t},Y_{t},Q_{t}
are all differentiable at each
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and so Theorem
4.5
(or Theorem
3.26
) applies. In particular, the resolvent operators of
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
are differentiable at each
t
, and a short calculation using (
4.14
), (
4.15
) and (
4.25
) shows that if
u
denotes the norm one eigenfunction with the eigenvalue
\lambda(t)\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
then
\dot{\lambda}(t)=|u^{\prime}(1)|^{2}
provided we know that
\lambda(t)
is an eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
for a given
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus, each of the branches
\lambda^{(k)}(\cdot)
,
k\in\{0\}\cup{\mathbb{N}}
, of the eigenvalues is monotone for all
t
where it is defined.

We proceed with finding the actual location of
\lambda\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
and formulas for
u
dealing with the two possible cases: (i)
\lambda=\kappa^{2}>0
, respectively, (ii)
\lambda=-\kappa^{2}<0
for
\kappa=\kappa(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Solving the equation
u^{\prime\prime}=0
with the boundary conditions we note that
\lambda=0\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})
with
u=x
, and that
0\notin\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
for all
t\neq 1
.
Plugging a linear combination of (i)
\cos(\kappa x)
and
\sin(\kappa x)
, respectively, (ii)
\sinh(\kappa x)
and
\cosh(\kappa x)
into the boundary value problem
-u^{\prime\prime}=\lambda u
,
u(0)=0
,
tu^{\prime}(1)=u(1)
shows that nonzero
\kappa=\kappa(t)
are the solutions to the equation (i)
t\kappa=\tan\kappa
with
u=a\sin(\kappa x)
,

a^{-2}=(1-t\cos^{2}\kappa)/2
, respectively, equation (ii)
t\kappa=\tanh\kappa
with
u=a\sinh(\kappa x)
,
a^{-2}=(t\cosh^{2}\kappa-1)/2
.
By inspection of the graphs in the equations, in case (i), for each
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
there is a unique solution

\kappa\in(-\pi/2+\pi n,\pi/2+\pi n)
, for each
t>1
there is a unique solution
\kappa=\kappa(t)\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
with
\kappa(t)\to 0^{+}
as
t\to 1^{+}
, and for any
t<1
there are no solutions
\kappa\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
. In case (ii), for any
t\leq 0
or
t>1
there are no solutions
\kappa\in{\mathbb{R}}
, while for each
t\in(0,1]
there exists a unique solution
\kappa=\kappa(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\kappa(t)\to 0
as
t\to 1^{-}
and
\kappa(t)\to+\infty
as
t\to 0^{+}
. By squaring
\kappa
we obtain the branches
\lambda^{(0)}(t)<\lambda^{(1)}(t)<\dots

of the eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}
such that
\lambda^{(0)}(t)
are defined for
t>0
with
\lambda^{(0)}(t)\to-\infty
as
t\to 0^{+}
, is negative for
t\in(0,1)
and positive for
t>1
while
\lambda^{(k)}(t)
for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
is defined and positive for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, cf.
[
83
, Fig. 1 p.292]
. Using
\dot{\lambda}(t)=|u^{\prime}(1)|^{2}
and the expressions for
u
just given one obtains very particular formulas for
\dot{\lambda}^{(k)}(t)
for all
t
and
k
except when
k=0
and
t\leq 0
.

\Diamond

4.2.
Laplace operator on bounded domains via boundary triplets

The main result of this section is Theorem
4.11
in which we derive the resolvent difference formula, Riccati equation, and Hadamard-type formula for a family of Robin-type Laplacians. To that end, we employ abstract results of Theorem
4.5
with an ordinary boundary triplet specifically defined for the Laplace operator. The construction of such triplet for second order elliptic operators goes back to the work of M.I. Višik
[
123
,
124
]
who proposed the regularization of the Neumann trace by means of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map, G. Grubb
[
73
]
who investigated the case of higher-order operators building upon the trace theory of J. L. Lions, E. Magenes
[
97
]
. We also note that the work of M. Malamud
[
99
]
provides boundary triplets with dual party in
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)\times L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
as well as important relation between the Weyl function and the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map. Another relevant construction of trace maps is offered in
[
17
]
where a
B-
regularized boundary triplet was originally proposed.

Throughout this section
we assume that
n\in\mathbb{N},n\geq 2
and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain with
C^{1,r}
-boundary with
r>1/2
(although this assumption can be considerably weakened, see Remark
4.12
below).
We define the maximal and minimal Laplace operators as follows,

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\max}

\displaystyle:\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{\max})\subset L^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{\max})

\displaystyle=\big\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\big|\ \Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\max}u

\displaystyle=-\Delta u\ \ (\text{in the sence of distribudtions}),

(4.26)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{\min})

\displaystyle=H^{2}_{0}(\Omega),\,-\Delta_{\min}u=-\Delta u,

and remark that by
[
69
, Theorem 8.14]
6
6

6

one has

\begin{split}\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{\min})&=H^{2}_{0}(\Omega)=\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)|\ \Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega),\ \widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}(u)=0,\ \widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}(u)=0\},\\
-\Delta_{\min}&=(-\Delta_{\max})^{*},\,\,-\Delta_{\max}=(-\Delta_{\min})^{*}.\end{split}

(4.27)

Here and below we use the following extensions of the Dirichlet and (weak) Neumann
traces,

\begin{split}\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}&:\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\,|\,\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\to H^{-1/2}({\partial\Omega}),\\
\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}&:\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\,|\,\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\to H^{-3/2}({\partial\Omega}),\end{split}

(4.28)

and consider the map

\tau_{{}_{N}}:\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)|\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\,\tau_{{}_{N}}u:=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}u+M_{D,N}(\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u),

(4.29)

where
M_{D,N}
is the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map acting by the rule
M_{D,N}:g\mapsto-\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}u
for

u
being the solution of the boundary value problem

-\Delta u=0,\,u\in L^{2}(\Omega),\quad\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u=g\text{\ on\ }\partial\Omega.

(4.30)

More details regarding the definitions of
\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}},\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}
and
\tau_{{}_{N}}
and their properties are discussed in Appendix
C
, cf. Lemma
C.3
,
C.4
,
C.5
taken from
[
69
]
. In the sequel we use the Reisz isomorphism given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Phi:H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\\
&H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)\ni\psi\mapsto\Phi_{\psi}\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\\
&\langle f,\psi\rangle_{{-1/2}}:=\psi(f)=\langle f,\Phi_{\psi}\rangle_{1/2},f\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\psi\in H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega),\end{split}

(4.31)

in particular, for
f,\psi\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\partial\Omega)\hookrightarrow H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)
we have

\langle f,\psi\rangle_{{-1/2}}=\langle f,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}.

(4.32)

We also note that
\Phi
is a conjugate linear mapping. The next lemma is a well-known fact that goes back to
[
73
]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded domain with
C^{1,r}
-boundary,
r>1/2
, and the boundary traces
\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}
,
\tau_{{}_{N}}
are as in (
4.28
), (
4.29
). Then

(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}):=(H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\tau_{{}_{N}},\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}})

(4.33)

is an boundary triplet for
A=-\Delta_{\min}
.

Proof.

The trace operator
\mathrm{T}:=[\tau_{{}_{N}},\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}]^{\top}
is defined on the space

{\mathcal{H}}_{+}:=\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}

with the norm

\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}=(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\Delta u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2})^{1/2}.

Recalling the Green identity (
C.5
)

\displaystyle(-\Delta u,v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-(u,-\Delta v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=-{{}_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\langle\tau_{{}_{N}}u,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}v\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)}}+\overline{{}_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\langle\tau_{{}_{N}}v,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)}},

(4.34)

we rewrite it as

\displaystyle\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}=-\langle\Gamma_{0}u,\Gamma_{1}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}+\overline{\langle\Gamma_{0}v,\Gamma_{1}u\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}},

\displaystyle=\langle\Gamma_{1}u,\Gamma_{0}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}u,\Gamma_{1}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}},

(4.35)

and thus check that (
4.33
) satisfies the abstract Green identity. It remains to show that the map
\mathrm{T}:{\mathcal{H}}_{+}\to H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
is onto.
We fix a vector
(f,g)\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)
.
By (
C.11
) there exists

u_{0}\in H^{2}(\Omega)\cap H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
such that
\tau_{{}_{N}}u_{0}=f
. By
[
69
, Theorem 10.4]
, the boundary value problem (
4.30
)
has a unique solution that we denote by
v_{0}
(we note that zero is outside of the spectrum of the Dirichlet Laplacian). Employing (
C.12
) and
v_{0}\in\operatorname{ker}(\tau_{{}_{N}})
yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}(u_{0}+v_{0})=(\tau_{{}_{N}}(u_{0}+v_{0}),\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}(u_{0}+v_{0}))=(\tau_{{}_{N}}u_{0},\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}v_{0})=(f,\Phi g)

(4.36)

since
\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u_{0}=\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{0}=0
.∎

Remark 4.10
.

In PDE literature, boundary value problems are often formulated in terms of the Dirichlet and Neumann traces defined by

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{D}}:\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\rightarrow H^{1/2}({\partial\Omega}),\gamma_{{}_{D}}:=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}\upharpoonright_{\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}},

(4.37)

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{N}}:\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\rightarrow H^{-1/2}({\partial\Omega}),\gamma_{{}_{N}}:=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}\upharpoonright_{\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}}.

(4.38)

We note that
(-\Delta_{\max},\gamma_{{}_{D}},\gamma_{{}_{N}})
is not an boundary triplet. Firstly,
\mathrm{T}:=(\gamma_{{}_{D}},\gamma_{{}_{N}})
is not defined on the entire space
\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{\max})
. Secondly,
\mathrm{T}
is not onto, see
[
92
, Proposition 2.11]
. However, Hypothesis
2.1
is still satisfied with
{\mathcal{D}}:=\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}
and equipped with the norm
(\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\Delta u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2})^{1/2}
. In fact, Hypothesis
3.1
is also satisfied for this choice of
\mathrm{T},{\mathcal{D}}
. These facts serve as our main motivation for introducing Hypotheses
2.1
,
3.1
. We elaborate on this further in Section
5
.

\Diamond

Having constructed the boundary triplet for the Laplacian, we can now apply the abstract results from Theorem
4.5
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with
C^{1,r}
-boundary,
r>1/2
, and let

t\mapsto\Xi_{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))
,
t\in[0,1]
, be a differentiable family of self-adjoint operators. Then for
t\in[0,1]
the linear operator

\displaystyle-\Delta_{t}:\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{t})\subset L^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega),-\Delta_{t}u=-\Delta u,

(4.39)

\displaystyle u\in\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{t}):=\{u\in\operatorname{dom}(\Delta_{\max}):\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u+\Xi_{t}\tau_{{}_{N}}u=0\},

(4.40)

is self-adjoint.
The following resolvent difference formula holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}(-\Delta_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}&-(-\Delta_{s}-\zeta)^{-1}\\
&=\big(\tau_{{}_{N}}(-\Delta_{t}-\overline{\zeta})^{-1}\big)^{*}(\Xi_{t}-\Xi_{s})\big(\tau_{{}_{N}}(-\Delta_{s}-\zeta)^{-1}\big),\end{split}

(4.41)

for
t,s\in[0,1]
,
\zeta\not\in(\operatorname{Spec}(-\Delta_{t})\cup\operatorname{Spec}(-\Delta_{s}))
. Moreover, for a fixed
t_{0}\in[0,1]
the mapping

t\mapsto(-\Delta_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))

(4.42)

is well defined for
t
near
t_{0}
as long as
\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}(-\Delta_{t_{0}})
. This mapping is differentiable at
t_{0}
and satisfies the following Riccati equaiton

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}\big|_{t=t_{0}}\big((-\Delta_{t}&-\zeta)^{-1}\big)\\
&=\big(\tau_{{}_{N}}(-\Delta_{t_{0}}-\overline{\zeta})^{-1}\big)^{*}\big(\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}\big|_{t=t_{0}}\Xi_{t}\big)\big(\tau_{{}_{N}}(-\Delta_{t_{0}}-\zeta)^{-1}\big).\end{split}

(4.43)

Finally, if
\lambda(t_{0})
is an eigenvalue of
-\Delta_{t_{0}}
of multiplicity
m\geq 1
then there exists a choice of orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\operatorname{ker}(-\Delta_{t_{0}}-\lambda(t_{0}))

and a labeling of eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
-\Delta_{t}
, for
t
near
t_{0}
, such that

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=-\langle\dot{\Xi}_{t_{0}}\tau_{{}_{N}}u_{j},\tau_{{}_{N}}u_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},1\leq j\leq m.

(4.44)

Proof.

By Lemma
4.9
,
(H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\tau_{{}_{N}},\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}})
is an boundary triplet. In order to check that
-\Delta_{t}
is self-adjoint, it suffices to check conditions (
2.16
), (
2.17
) with
X:=\Xi_{t}
,
Y:=I
. Indeed, (
2.16
) holds since
\Xi_{t}
is self-adjoint, (
2.17
) holds since the operator
XX^{*}+YY^{*}
given by
I+\Xi_{t}^{2}>0
is invertible. The fact that (
4.42
) is well defined for
t
near
t_{0}
follows from continuity of
\Xi_{t}
and Theorems
4.5
and
3.18
upon setting
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}:=-\Delta_{t}
,
V_{t}:=0
,
\mathrm{T}_{t}:=[\tau_{{}_{N}},\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}]^{\top}
. In order to prove (
4.41
), (
4.43
), (
4.44
), we use (
3.79
), (
3.81
), (
3.82
), respectively, with

\displaystyle\big(W(\Xi_{t},I)\big)\mathrm{T}R_{t}(\zeta)=(I+\Xi_{t}^{2})^{-1}(-\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\zeta)+\Xi_{t}\Gamma_{1}R_{t}(\zeta))

\displaystyle\quad=(I+\Xi_{t}^{2})^{-1}(-\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\zeta)-\Xi_{t}^{2}\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\zeta))=-\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\zeta)=-\tau_{N}R_{t}(\zeta)

(4.45)

and
\phi_{j}=-\tau_{{}_{N}}u_{j}
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

The assumption
\partial\Omega
being
C^{1,r}
,
r>1/2
, imposed in this section could be replaced by
\partial\Omega
being Lipschitz and
\Omega
quasi-convex, see
[
69
, Section 8]
for the definition. As proved in
[
69
]
, these weaker assumptions are sufficient for the domains of the Dirichlet and Neumann Laplacians to belong to
H^{2}(\Omega)
, which in turn is equivalent to (
4.27
) to hold. Also, for the case of Lipschitz domains Lemma
4.9
and, as demonstrated in
[
69
]
, leading to it Lemmas
C.3
,
C.4
,
C.5
hold with the Sobolev spaces
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and
H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)
replaced by
N^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and its adjoint
\big(N^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\big)^{*}
, respectively, where the space
N^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
is defined as
\{f\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega):f\nu_{j}\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\}
,
\nu=(\nu_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
, and is equal to
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
provided
\partial\Omega
is
C^{1,r}
,
r>1/2
. In the context of Lipschitz domains we also mention an important paper
[
18
]
.

\Diamond

Remark 4.13
.

Our motivation to consider the boundary condition in Theorem
4.11
stems from
[
44
,
67
,
95
]
. More generally, the boundary condition described in Theorem
4.11
can be replaced by
X_{t}\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}u+Y_{t}\tau_{{}_{N}}u=0
for
X_{t},Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))
satisfying (
2.16
), (
2.17
). In this case, as in Theorem
4.11
, continuity of the mappings
t\mapsto X_{t}
,
t\mapsto Y_{t}
yields continuity of the resolvent operator with respect to
t
. Moreover, differentiability of the mappings
t\mapsto X_{t}
,
t\mapsto Y_{t}
yields differentiability of the resolvent operator with respect to
t
as well as the Reccati equation and the formula for the slopes of the eigenvalue curves (both obtained by dropping the potential terms
V_{t}
in (
4.13
), (
4.14
), respectively).

\Diamond

4.3.
Quantum graphs

The main result of this section is Theorem
4.14
in which we derive the resolvent difference formula, Riccati equation, and Hadamard-type formula for Schrödinger operators on metric graphs. To that end, we employ the abstract results discussed in Theorem
4.5
with an boundary triplet specifically defined for quantum graphs. Examples
4.15
and
4.16
give two applications of Theorem
4.14
. Both examples concern monotonicity of eigenvalue curves of Schrödinger operators with respect to some natural parameter present in the boundary conditions.

We begin by discussing differential operators on metric graphs. To set the stage, let us fix a discrete graph
({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
where
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
denote the set of vertices and edges respectively. We assume that
({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{E}})
consists of a finite number of vertices,
|{\mathcal{V}}|
, and a finite number of edges,
|{\mathcal{E}}|
. We assign to each edge
e\in{\mathcal{E}}
a positive and finite length
\ell_{e}\in(0,\infty)
. The corresponding metric graph is denoted by
{\mathcal{G}}
. The boundary
\partial{\mathcal{G}}
of the metric graph is defined by

\partial{\mathcal{G}}:=\cup_{e\in{\mathcal{E}}}\{a_{e},b_{e}\},

(4.46)

where
a_{e},b_{e}
denote the end points of the edge
e
. It is convenient to treat
2|{\mathcal{E}}|
dimensional vectors as functions on the boundary
\partial{\mathcal{G}}
, in particular,

L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{2|{\mathcal{E}}|}
,
where the space
L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})=\bigoplus_{e\in{\mathcal{E}}}\left(L^{2}(\{a_{e}\})\times L^{2}(\{b_{e}\})\right)
corresponds to the discrete Dirac measure with support
\cup_{e\in{\mathcal{E}}}\{a_{e},b_{e}\}
. In addition to the space of functions on the boundary we consider the Sobolev spaces of functions on the graph
{\mathcal{G}}
,

\displaystyle L^{2}({\mathcal{G}}):=\bigoplus_{e\in{\mathcal{E}}}L^{2}(e),\ \widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}}):=\bigoplus_{e\in{\mathcal{E}}}H^{2}(e),

where
H^{2}(e)
is the standard
L^{2}
based Sobolev space.
As in the setting of Laplace operators on bounded domains, the spaces
{L}^{2}({\mathcal{G}})
and
L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})
are related via the trace maps. We define the trace operators
(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
by the formulas

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}:\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}})\rightarrow L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}}),\ \Gamma_{0}u:=u|_{\partial{\mathcal{G}}},u\in\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}}),

(4.47)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}:\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}})\rightarrow L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}}),\ \Gamma_{1}u:=\partial_{n}u|_{\partial{\mathcal{G}}},u\in\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}}),

(4.48)

where
\partial_{n}u
denotes the derivative of
u
taken in the
inward
direction. The trace operator is a bounded, linear operator given by

\mathrm{T}:=[\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}]^{\top},\,\mathrm{T}:\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}})\rightarrow L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})\times L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{4|{\mathcal{E}}|}.

(4.49)

The Sobolev space of functions vanishing on the boundary
\partial{\mathcal{G}}
together with their derivatives is denoted by

H^{2}_{0}({\mathcal{G}}):=\left\{u\in\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}}):\mathrm{T}u=0\right\}.

Using our notation for the trace maps, the Green identity can be written as follows,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{G}}}(-u^{\prime\prime})\overline{v}-u\overline{(-v^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial{\mathcal{G}}}\partial_{n}u\overline{v}-u\overline{\partial_{n}v}

(4.50)

\displaystyle=\langle[J\otimes I_{2|{\mathcal{E}}|}]\mathrm{T}u,\mathrm{T}v\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{4|{\mathcal{E}}|}},\ u,v\in\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}}).

The right-hand side of the Green identity defines a symplectic form

\displaystyle\omega:\ \prescript{d\!}{}{L}^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})\times\,\prescript{d\!}{}{L}^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}},

(4.51)

\displaystyle\omega((f_{1},f_{2}),(g_{1},g_{2})):=\int_{\partial{\mathcal{G}}}f_{2}\overline{g_{1}}-f_{1}\overline{g_{2}},

(4.52)

\displaystyle(f_{1},f_{2}),(g_{1},g_{2})\in\prescript{d\!}{}{L}^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}}),

(4.53)

where
\prescript{d\!}{}{L}^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}}):=L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})\times L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})
.

Next, we introduce the minimal Laplace operator
A_{min}
and its adjoint
A_{max}
. The operator

A_{min}:=-\frac{\hbox{\rm d}^{2}}{\hbox{\rm d}x^{2}},\quad\operatorname{dom}(A_{min})=\widehat{H}^{2}_{0}({\mathcal{G}}),

(4.54)

is symmetric in
L^{2}({\mathcal{G}})
. Its adjoint
A_{max}:=A_{min}^{*}
is given by

A_{max}:=-\frac{\hbox{\rm d}^{2}}{\hbox{\rm d}x^{2}},\quad\operatorname{dom}(A_{max})=\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathcal{G}}).

(4.55)

The dificiency indices of
A_{min}
are finite and equal, that is,

0<\dim\operatorname{ker}(A_{max}-{\bf i})=\dim\operatorname{ker}(A_{max}+{\bf i})<\infty.

(4.56)

Theorem 4.14
.

Assume that

\displaystyle t\mapsto V_{t}\text{ is in }C^{1}([0,1],L^{\infty}({\mathcal{G}})),

(4.57)

\displaystyle t\mapsto X_{t},Y_{t}\text{ is in }C^{1}([0,1],{\mathbb{C}}^{2|{\mathcal{E}}|\times 2|{\mathcal{E}}|}),\ \operatorname{det}(X_{t}X^{*}_{t}+Y_{t}Y_{t}^{*})\not=0,X_{t}Y_{t}^{*}=Y_{t}^{*}Y_{t}.

(4.58)

Then the operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{t}:L^{2}({\mathcal{G}})\rightarrow L^{2}({\mathcal{G}}),\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}):=\{u\in H^{2}({\mathcal{G}}):[X_{t},Y_{t}]\mathrm{T}u=0\},

(4.59)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{t}u=-u^{\prime\prime},u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}),

(4.60)

is a self-adjoint extension of
A_{min}
. The operator-valued function

t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta_{0}):=({\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}-\zeta_{0})^{-1}\text{ for all }\zeta_{0}\not\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})

(4.61)

is in
C^{1}([0,1],{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathcal{G}})))
and for any
t_{0}\in[0,1]
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\dot{R}_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})=-R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\dot{V}_{t_{0}}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0})\\
&+(\mathrm{T}R_{t_{0}}(\overline{\zeta_{0}}))^{*}\big(W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\big)^{*}(\dot{X}_{t_{0}}{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\big(W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\big)\mathrm{T}R_{t_{0}}(\zeta_{0}),\end{split}

(4.62)

where
W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})
is as in (
2.20
).
Furthermore, if
\lambda(t_{0})
is an eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}+V_{t_{0}}
of multiplicity
m\geq 1
then there exist a choice of orthonormal eigenfunctions

\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{A}}_{t_{0}}+V_{t_{0}}-\lambda(t_{0}))

and a labeling of eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
, for
t
near
t_{0}
, such that

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\langle\dot{V}_{t_{0}}u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathcal{G}})}+\big\langle(X_{t_{0}}\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-Y_{t_{0}}\dot{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\phi_{j},\phi_{j}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})},

(4.63)

where
\phi_{j}=W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}u_{j}
is a unique
2|{\mathcal{E}}|
dimensional vector satisfying
\Gamma_{0}u_{j}=-Y_{t_{0}}^{*}\phi_{j}
and
\Gamma_{1}u_{j}=X_{t_{0}}^{*}\phi_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq m
.

Proof.

Since
(L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}}),\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is an boundary triplet, equations (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) in Theorem
4.5

give (
4.62
) and (
4.63
) respectively.
∎

Example 4.15
.

Consider the Schrödinger operator
\displaystyle{H_{t}=-\frac{\hbox{\rm d}^{2}}{\hbox{\rm d}x^{2}}+V}
on a compact star graph
{\mathcal{G}}=({\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{V})
with a bounded real-valued potential
V
subject to arbitrary self-adjoint vertex conditions at the vertices of degree one and the following
\delta
-type condition at the center
{\rm v_{c}}\in\mathcal{V}
,

\sum_{e\sim{\rm v_{c}}}{\partial_{n}u_{e}({\rm v_{c}})}=tu({\rm v_{c}}),\ t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(4.64)

We recall that the spectrum of
H_{t}
can be described via secular equations
[
21
]
. In this example, we will derive an Hadamard-type formula (
4.67
) for the derivative of the eigenvalues of
H_{t}
. Such a formula is discussed in
[
21
, Proposition 3.1.6]
for simple eigenvalues. The general case can be treated using (
4.63
) as follows. The boundary matrices describing the vertex conditions are given by
\widetilde{X}\times X_{t}
and
\widetilde{Y}\times Y
where

X_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-1&&\cdots&0\\
0&1&-1&\cdots&0\\
&&\ddots\\
0&&&1&-1\\
-t&0&\cdots&&0\end{bmatrix},\quad Y=\begin{bmatrix}0&&\cdots&&0\\
0&&\cdots&&0\\
&&\ddots\\
0&&\cdots&&0\\
1&1&\cdots&&1\end{bmatrix},

and the matrices
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{Y}
correspond to the vertex conditions at
{\mathcal{V}}\setminus\{{\rm v_{c}}\}
. A direct computation gives

X_{t}^{*}Y=Y^{*}X_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&\cdots&&0\\
0&0&\cdots&&0\\
&&\ddots\\
0&0&\cdots&&-t\end{bmatrix}.\

(4.65)

For the eigenvalue
\lambda(t_{0})
of
H_{t_{0}}
of multiplicity
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
we use (
4.63
) to get

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\big\langle(X_{t_{0}}\dot{Y}_{t_{0}}^{*}-Y_{t_{0}}\dot{X}_{t_{0}}^{*})\phi_{j},\phi_{j}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial{\mathcal{G}})}=|\phi_{j}({\rm v_{c}})|^{2},

(4.66)

where
1\leq j\leq m
,
\phi_{j}=W(X_{t_{0}},Y_{t_{0}})\mathrm{T}u_{j}
, and
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
are the eigenfunctions of
H_{t_{0}}
corresponding to
\lambda(t_{0})
. Furthermore, using (
3.83
) we obtain
\phi_{j}({\rm v_{c}})=-u_{j}({\rm v_{c}})
, hence,

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=|u_{j}({\rm v_{c}})|^{2},\,1\leq j\leq m.

(4.67)

\Diamond

Example 4.16
.

This example concerns monotonicity of eigenvalue curves of a class of Schrödinger operators on a compact interval arising in the spectral theory of periodic Hamiltonians. Specifically, we consider the Schrödinger operators
H_{\vartheta}
with a real valued potential
V\in L^{\infty}(0,1)
which are parameterized by
\vartheta\in[0,2\pi)
and defined as follows,

\displaystyle H_{\vartheta}={\mathcal{A}}_{\vartheta}+V,\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\vartheta}:L^{2}(0,1)\rightarrow L^{2}(0,1),{\mathcal{A}}_{\vartheta}u=-u^{\prime\prime},u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{\vartheta}),

(4.68)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{\vartheta}):=\{u\in H^{2}(0,1):e^{{\bf i}\vartheta}u(0)=u(1),e^{{\bf i}\vartheta}u^{\prime}(0)=u^{\prime}(1)\}.

(4.69)

Such operators are of interest, in particular, because their eigenvalues fill up the spectral bands of the Schrödinger operator
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
with the potential given by the periodic extension of
V
, see
[
110
, Theorems XIII.89, XIII.90]
. We claim that the eigenvalue curves satisfy

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(\vartheta_{0})=2\operatorname{Im}(u_{j}^{\prime}(0)\overline{u_{j}(0)})\text{ for all }\vartheta_{0}\in(0,2\pi),

(4.70)

where, as usual,
u_{j}\in\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{A}}_{\vartheta_{0}}-\lambda_{j}(\vartheta_{0}))
,
j=1,2
(in fact all but, possibly, periodic and antiperiodic operators have simple spectra). We derive this formula from (
4.14
) by defining trace operators appropriately. It is well known that ordinary differential operators fit well into the scheme of boundary triplets, cf. e.g.
[
71
, Chapter 3]
, however, for completeness we recall the setting. We set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}:=L^{2}(0,1),{\mathcal{H}}_{+}:=H^{2}(0,1),A=-\frac{\hbox{\rm d}^{2}}{\hbox{\rm d}x^{2}},\operatorname{dom}(A)=H^{2}_{0}(0,1)

(4.71)

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}:H^{2}(0,1)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{4},\Gamma_{0}u:=(u(0),u(1))^{\top},\Gamma_{1}u:=(u^{\prime}(0),-u^{\prime}(1))^{\top}.

(4.72)

Next, to utilize (
4.14
) we first rewrite the boundary conditions in (
4.69
) as follows,

X_{\vartheta}\Gamma_{0}u+Y_{\vartheta}\Gamma_{1}u=0,\text{ where }X_{\vartheta}:=\begin{bmatrix}-\hbox{\rm e}^{{\bf i}\vartheta}&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},Y_{\vartheta}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
\hbox{\rm e}^{{\bf i}\vartheta}&1\end{bmatrix},

(4.73)

and compute

\displaystyle\phi_{j}=W(X_{\vartheta},Y_{\vartheta})\mathrm{T}u_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(-Y_{\vartheta_{0}}\Gamma_{0}u_{j}+X_{\vartheta_{0}}\Gamma_{1}u_{j})=-e^{{\bf i}\vartheta_{0}}(u_{j}^{\prime}(0),u_{j}(0))^{\top},

(4.74)

\displaystyle X_{\vartheta_{0}}\dot{Y}^{*}_{\vartheta_{0}}-Y_{\vartheta_{0}}X^{*}_{\vartheta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&{\bf i}\\
-{\bf i}&0\end{bmatrix}.

(4.75)

Plugging this in (
4.63
) yields (
4.70
). Monotonicity of the eigenvalues follows from linear independence of
u_{j},\overline{u_{j}}
and the formula

2|\operatorname{Im}(u_{j}^{\prime}(0)\overline{u_{j}(0)})|=|{\mathcal{W}}(u_{j},\overline{u_{j}})(0)|\not=0,\vartheta_{0}\in(0,2\pi).

(4.76)

involving the Wronskian.
\Diamond

4.4.
Periodic Kronig–Penney model

7
7

7

An alternative approach applicable to a very broad class of second-order operators is discussed in the upcoming work of D. Damanik, J. Fillman and the second author. See also
[
24
]
.

In this section we give yet another application of Theorem
4.5
proving a version of B. Simon’s theorem
[
120
]
which states that a certain open gap property (described below) of periodic Schrödinger operators is generic in the class of periodic
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
potentials. The main result of this section, Theorem
4.17
, states this assertion for singular
\delta-
type potentials. Its proof is based on a perturbative argument inspired by
[
120
]
and technically made available by Theorem
4.5
.

The spectrum of the Schrödinger operator with periodic potential on the line has a band-gap structure, that is, in general, it consists of closed segments, called
bands
, such that two adjacent bands can either have a common endpoint or be separated by an open interval, a
gap
, of the resolvent set; in the latter case we say that the gap is open.
We will now use Theorem
4.5
to prove that
all gaps
of a generic periodic Kronig–Penney model are open. The operators in question are the Schrödinger operators with
\delta-
type potentials which in physics literature are written as follows,

\displaystyle H_{{\alpha}}:=-\frac{\hbox{\rm d}^{2}}{\hbox{\rm d}x^{2}}+\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\alpha_{k}\delta(x-k),

(4.77)

and mathematically are defined by

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}u:=-u^{\prime\prime},u\in\operatorname{dom}(H_{\alpha}),H_{\alpha}:\operatorname{dom}(H_{\alpha})\subset L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}),

(4.78)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(H_{{\alpha}})=\{u\in\widehat{H}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}):u\ \text{satisfies}\ \eqref{6.8new}\text{\ for all}\ k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\},

(4.79)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89418ptu(k^{+})=u(k^{-}),\ u^{\prime}(k^{+})-u^{\prime}(k^{-})=\alpha_{k}u(k),

(4.80)

where
\alpha=\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}};{\mathbb{R}})
,
u(k^{\pm})
are the one-sided limits, and
\widehat{H}^{2}
denotes the direct sum of the Sobolev spaces on respective intervals. The spectrum of
H_{\alpha}
for the case of periodic sequence
\alpha
has a band-gap structure, see
[
3
, Theorem 2.3.3]
. This was originally proved for
1-
periodic sequences but can be directly extended to any
p-
periodic ones. Specifically, given a
p-
periodic sequence
\alpha=\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}};{\mathbb{R}})
, the operator
H_{\alpha}
is unitary equivalent to the direct integral

\int_{[0,2\pi)}^{\oplus}H_{{\alpha^{(p)}},\vartheta}\frac{\hbox{\rm d}\vartheta}{2\pi},\,\text{ where we denote $\alpha^{(p)}:=\{\alpha_{0},...,\alpha_{p-1}\}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}$,}

(4.81)

and
H_{{\alpha^{(p)}},\vartheta}
for
\vartheta\in[0,2\pi)
is the operator defined in
L^{2}(I_{p})
with
I_{p}:=(-1/2,p-1/2)
by

\displaystyle H_{{\alpha^{(p)}},\vartheta}u:=-u^{\prime\prime},\ H_{{\alpha^{(p)}},\vartheta}:\operatorname{dom}(H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta})\subset L^{2}(I_{p})\rightarrow L^{2}(I_{p}),

(4.82)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta})=\Big\{u\in\widehat{H}^{2}(I_{p}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}):u\ \text{satisfies}\ \eqref{6.8new}\text{\ for $k\in I_{p}\cap{\mathbb{Z}}$ and\ }\eqref{6.9new}\Big\},

(4.83)

\displaystyle u(-1/2^{+})=e^{{\bf i}\vartheta}u((p-1/2)^{-}),\,u^{\prime}(-1/2^{+})=e^{{\bf i}\vartheta}u^{\prime}((p-1/2)^{-}),

(4.84)

where

\widehat{H}^{2}(I_{p}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}):=H^{2}(-1/2,0)\oplus H^{2}(0,1)\oplus...\oplus H^{2}(p-2,p-1)\oplus H^{2}(p-1,p-1/2).

(4.85)

Denoting the eigenvalues of
H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}
(ordered in non-decreasing order) by

\lambda_{j}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta),j=1,2,\dots,

we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},0)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta)\leq\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi)\leq\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi)\leq\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta)\leq\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},0)

(4.86)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{3}(\alpha^{(p)},0)\leq\lambda_{3}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta)\leq\lambda_{3}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi)\leq\dots\text{ for $\vartheta\in[0,\pi]$.}

(4.87)

Then the spectrum of
H_{\alpha}
is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Spec}(H_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\bigcup\limits_{\vartheta\in[0,\pi]}\operatorname{Spec}(H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta})

(4.88)

\displaystyle=[\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},0),\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi)]\cup[\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi),\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},0)]\cup\dots.

(4.89)

The intervals
[\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},0),\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi)]
,
[\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi),\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},0)],\dots
are called
bands
. The endpoints of two adjacent bands may coincide. In this case we say that the respective gap is closed; otherwise the respective
gap
,
\big(\lambda_{1}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi),\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},\pi)\big)
,
\big(\lambda_{2}(\alpha^{(p)},0),\lambda_{3}(\alpha^{(p)},0)\big),\dots
is said to be open.
In the following theorem we show that all gaps are open for a generic periodic sequence
\alpha
.

Theorem 4.17
.

There is a dense
G_{\delta}-
set
{\mathcal{S}}\subset\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}};{\mathbb{R}})
of sequences
\alpha
such that for each
\alpha\in{\mathcal{S}}
all gaps in the spectrum of
H_{\alpha}
are open.

Proof.

We let

{\mathcal{S}}_{n}:=\{\alpha\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}};{\mathbb{R}}):\alpha\text{\ is $p-$periodic and the $n$-th gap of $H_{\alpha}$ is open}\}.

It is enough to prove that each
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
is open and dense (then
\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
gives the required dense
G_{\delta}
-set of potentials). To begin,
let us rewrite
\operatorname{dom}(H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta})
in terms of Lagrangian planes in
\Lambda({\mathbb{C}}^{4(p+1)})
. For
u\in\widehat{H}^{2}(I_{p}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}})
we introduce the traces
\Gamma_{0}u,\Gamma_{1}u\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2(p+1)}
by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}u:=\{u(-1/2^{+}),u((p-1/2)^{-}),u(0^{-}),u(0^{+}),\dots,u(k^{-}),u(k^{+}),\dots,

(4.90)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378ptu((p-1)^{-}),u((p-1)^{+})\}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2(p+1)},

(4.91)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}u:=\{u^{\prime}(-1/2^{+}),-u^{\prime}((p-1/2)^{-}),-u^{\prime}(0^{-}),u^{\prime}(0^{+}),\dots,-u^{\prime}(k^{-}),u^{\prime}(k^{+}),\dots,

(4.92)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt-u^{\prime}((p-1)^{-}),u^{\prime}((p-1)^{+})\}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2(p+1)}.

(4.93)

Also, let us introduce
2(p+1)\times 2(p+1)
matrices

\displaystyle X_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}:=\begin{bmatrix}-e^{{\bf i}\vartheta}&1\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\oplus\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
-\alpha_{0}&0\end{bmatrix}\oplus...\oplus\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
-\alpha_{p-1}&0\end{bmatrix},

(4.94)

\displaystyle Y_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
e^{{\bf i}\vartheta}&1\end{bmatrix}\oplus\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\oplus...\oplus\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}.

(4.95)

Then one has

\operatorname{dom}(H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta})=\{u\in\widehat{H}^{2}(I_{p}\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}):X_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}\Gamma_{0}u+Y_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}\Gamma_{1}u=0\}.

(4.96)

That is, the Lagrangian plane corresponding to
H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}
is given by

\operatorname{ker}[X_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta},Y_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}].

In order to prove that
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
is open, let us recall that the edges of the spectral gaps are given by consecutive eigenvalues of the periodic,
H_{\alpha^{(p)},0}
, or anti-periodic,
H_{\alpha^{(p)},\pi}
, operators. Suppose that
\alpha\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
and that the edges of the
n-
th gap satisfy
\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta)<\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta)
with either
\vartheta=0
or
\vartheta=\pi
. We claim that this strict inequality holds for all
\widetilde{\alpha}^{(p)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
near
\alpha^{(p)}
, i.e. that the gap is open under small perturbations of
\alpha^{(p)}
. Indeed, since the mapping

{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\ni\alpha^{(p)}\mapsto[X_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta},Y_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}]\text{ for $\vartheta=0$ or $\vartheta=\pi$}

(4.97)

is continuous, Theorem
4.5
yields continuity of the mapping

{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\ni\alpha^{(p)}\mapsto(H_{\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta}-{\bf i})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(I_{p}))\text{ for $\vartheta=0$ or $\vartheta=\pi$};

(4.98)

hence, the mappings

\alpha^{(p)}\mapsto\lambda_{j}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta),\alpha^{(p)}\mapsto\lambda_{j+1}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta),\text{ for $\vartheta=0$ or $\vartheta=\pi$}

(4.99)

are also continuous, which implies the asserted strict inequality

\lambda_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}^{(p)},\vartheta)<\lambda_{n+1}(\widetilde{\alpha}^{(p)},\vartheta)

for all
\widetilde{\alpha}^{(p)}
near
{\alpha}^{(p)}
.

In order to prove that
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
is dense we need to show that for both cases
\vartheta=0
and
\vartheta=\pi
the equality
\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta)=\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)},\vartheta)
will not hold if
\alpha^{(p)}
is replaced by its small perturbation. We will consider the case
\vartheta=0
, that is, we will assume that
\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)},0)=\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)},0)
; the case
\vartheta=\pi
is treated analogously. For
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let us introduce the perturbation
\alpha^{(p)}(t):=\{t+\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p-1}\}
. We claim that for every
\varepsilon>0
there is a
t_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon)
with

\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)}(t_{0}),0)<\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)}(t_{0}),0).

(4.100)

When proven, this inequality shows that there exist arbitrarily close to
\alpha^{(p)}
perturbations which open the closed gap. To prove the claim we utilize the Hadamard-type formula (
4.63
) for the boundary matrices
X_{\alpha^{(p)}(t),0}
,
Y_{\alpha^{(p)}(t),0}
. We recall that
\lambda:=\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)},0)=\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)},0)
is an eigenvalue of
H_{\alpha^{(p)},0}
of multiplicity two. By Theorem
4.14
, there is a basis
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
in
\operatorname{ker}(H_{\alpha^{(p)},0}-\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)}(t),0)=|u_{1}(0)|^{2},

(4.101)

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)}(t),0)=|u_{2}(0)|^{2}.

(4.102)

Next, we will prove that the values of the derivatives in (
4.101
), (
4.102
) are not equal to each other. This fact implies that the eigenvalue curves
t\mapsto\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)}(t),0)
and
t\mapsto\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)}(t),0)
do not coincide for
t
near
t=0
, which, in turn, yields (
4.100
) as needed. Starting the proof of the fact, we first remark that the eigenfunctions
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are real valued because the boundary conditions for
\vartheta=0
are real. Upon multiplying the eigenfunctions by appropriate constants
we may and will assume that
u_{1}(0)
and
u_{2}(0)
are non-negative. If
u_{1}(0)\not=u_{2}(0)
then the left-hand sides of (
4.101
), (
4.102
) are not equal as required. If
u_{1}(0)=u_{2}(0)
then for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
the function
u_{1}-u_{2}
satisfies the boundary condition at
x=0
with
\alpha_{0}
replaced by
t+\alpha_{0}
. Therefore,

u_{1}-u_{2}\in\operatorname{ker}(H_{\alpha^{(p)}(t),0}-\lambda)\setminus\{0\}

and thus
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
H_{\alpha^{(p)}(t),0}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. That is, either
\lambda_{n}(\alpha^{(p)}(t),0)
or
\lambda_{n+1}(\alpha^{(p)}(t),0)
should be identically equal to
\lambda
for all
t
near
0
. Hence, one of the derivatives in (
4.101
), (
4.102
) vanishes, say, the first one. Then
u_{1}(0)=0
. But in this case
u_{2}(0)\not=0
for otherwise
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
would be linearly dependent. Thus, the value of the derivative in (
4.101
) is equal to zero while the value of the derivative in (
4.102
) is not, as required.
∎

4.5.
Maslov crossing form for abstract boundary triplets

In this section, we discuss an infinitesimal version of the formula equating the Maslov index and the spectral flow for the family of operators
H_{t}={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
satisfying Hypothesis
4.3
, which is assumed throughout this section. Formulas relating these two quantities are quite classical, and we refer the reader to the papers
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
35
,
44
,
45
,
63
,
92
,
94
,
95
,
113
]
and the literature therein. Employing the abstract Hadamard-type formula obtained in Theorem
3.25
, we prove in Theorem
4.20
that the signature of the Maslov crossing form defined in (
4.109
) at an eigenvalue
\lambda
of the operator
H_{t_{0}}
is equal to the difference between the number of monotonically decreasing and the number of monotonically increasing eigenvalue curves for
H_{t}
bifurcating from
\lambda
.

For
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
t\in[0,1]
we introduce the following subspaces,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}:=\mathrm{T}_{t}\big(\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}+V_{t}-\lambda)\big)\subset\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},

(4.103)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})\subset\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},

(4.104)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}:={\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\subset\big((\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\oplus(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\big),

(4.105)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}:=\{{\mathbf{p}}=(p,p)^{\top}:p\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}\}\subset\big((\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\oplus(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\big).

(4.106)

Since
\mathrm{T}_{t}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t}))=\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})
by Hypothesis
4.3
, the following assertions are equivalent

(i)\,\,\operatorname{ker}(H_{t}-\lambda)\not=\{0\},\quad(ii)\,\,{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\neq\{0\},\quad(iii)\,\,\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}\cap\mathfrak{D}\neq\{0\}

(4.107)

since
\mathfrak{D}
is the diagonal subspace in
(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\oplus(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
. In fact, using a fundamental Proposition 3.5 in
[
26
]
, one can deduce deeper connections between the spectral information for
H_{t}
and the behavior of Lagrangian planes under the following hypotheses.

Hypothesis 4.18
.

Given
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
t_{0}\in[0,1]
, we assume that

(i)

\lambda\not\in\operatorname{Spec}_{\rm ess}(H_{t_{0}})
.

Moreover, there exists an interval
{\mathcal{J}}\subset[0,1]
centered at
t_{0}
such that

(ii)

the mappings
t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}
,
t\mapsto V_{t}
,
t\mapsto Q_{t}
are
C^{1}
on
{\mathcal{J}}
,

(iii)

\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}+V_{t}-\lambda)\cap\operatorname{dom}(A)=\{0\}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{J}}
.

Hypothesis
4.18
will be assumed through this section.
Part (iii) of this hypothesis is an abstract version of the unique continuation principle for PDEs, and we refer to Theorems 3.2 and Hypothesis 5.9 in
[
92
]
for a discussion of this connection.
Part (i) implies that the operator
H_{t_{0}}-\lambda
is Fredholm.
Since
\operatorname{ker}(\mathrm{T})=\operatorname{dom}(A)
by Proposition
2.2
(1), parts (i) and (iii) of Hypothesis
4.18
imply that
\mathrm{T}\big|_{\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda)}
is an isomorphism between
\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda)
and

{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
, cf. (
4.107
). Moreover,
the subspaces
{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t_{0}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}
form a Fredholm pair (i.e. their intersection is finite dimensional and their sum is closed and has finite co-dimension). The latter fact has been established in
[
26
, Proposition 3.5]
in the setting of Lagrangian planes in
\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})/\operatorname{dom}(A)
; using this one can readily deduce the Fredholm property of the pair in the present setting via the symplectomorphism introduced in
[
92
, Proposition 5.3]
. The subspace
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
is Lagrangian by Hypothesis
4.3
. The subspace
{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}
is also Lagrangian again by
[
26
, Proposition 3.5]
.
Furthermore, part (ii) of Hypothesis
4.18
yields continuity in
t
of the resolvent operators for
H_{t}
by Theorem
3.18
. This, together with part (i), shows that
\lambda\not\in\operatorname{Spec}_{\rm ess}(H_{t})
for
t
near
t_{0}
, hence, the subspaces
{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t},{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
form a Fredholm pair of Lagrangian subspaces for each
t
near
t_{0}
. Hence,
\big(\Upsilon_{\lambda,t},\mathfrak{D}\big)
is a Fredholm pair of Lagrangian subspaces for each
t
near
t_{0}
.

Let
\Pi_{\lambda,t}
be the orthogonal projection onto
\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}
from (
4.105
) so that the mapping
t\mapsto\Pi_{\lambda,t}
is continuously differentiable on
[0,1]
for each
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, see
[
92
, pp.480–481]
. Furthermore, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
t_{0}\in[0,1]
satisfying Hypothesis
4.18
there is an interval
{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq{\mathcal{J}}\subset[0,1]
centered at
t_{0}
and a family of operators
t\mapsto{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}
,
t\in{\mathcal{I}}
, which is in
C^{1}\big({\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{B}}(\Upsilon_{\lambda,t_{0}},(\Upsilon_{\lambda,t_{0}})^{\bot})\big)
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t_{0}}=0
such that

\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}=\big\{{\mathbf{q}}+{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}{\mathbf{q}}\ \big|\,{\mathbf{q}}\in\Upsilon_{\lambda,t_{0}}\big\},t\in{\mathcal{I}},

(4.108)

see, e.g.,
[
44
, Lemma 3.8]
. We call
(\lambda,t_{0})
a conjugate point if
\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda)\not=\{0\}
, or equivalently, if assertions (ii) and (iii) in (
4.107
) hold for
t=t_{0}
. The Maslov crossing form
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
for
\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}
relative to
\mathfrak{D}
at the conjugate point
{(\lambda,t_{0})}

is defined on the finite dimensional intersection
\Upsilon_{\lambda,t_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{D}
of the Lagrangian subspaces by the formula

\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}({\mathbf{q}},{\mathbf{p}}):=\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}\big|_{t=t_{0}}\widehat{\omega}({\mathbf{q}},{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}{\mathbf{p}})=\widehat{\omega}({\mathbf{q}},\dot{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\lambda,t_{0}}{\mathbf{p}}),\ {\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}}\in\Upsilon_{\lambda,t_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{D},

(4.109)

where
\widehat{\omega}={\omega}\oplus(-{\omega})
is a symplectic form on
(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\oplus(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
and, as usual, we abbreviate
\displaystyle{\dot{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\lambda,t_{0}}=\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}\big|_{t=t_{0}}}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
(\lambda,t_{0})
be a conjugate point satisfying Hypothesis
4.18
and let
u\in\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{0}}-\lambda)
. Then there exist an open interval
{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq{\mathcal{J}}
centered at
t_{0}
, a family
t\mapsto w_{t}
in
C^{1}({\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})
, and
a family
t\mapsto g_{t}\in\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})
in
C^{1}({\mathcal{I}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
such that

\displaystyle w_{{t_{0}}}

\displaystyle=u,\quad g_{{t_{0}}}=\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u,

(4.110)

\displaystyle w_{t}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}+V_{t}-\lambda),

(4.111)

\displaystyle\big(\mathrm{T}_{t}w_{t},g_{t}\big)^{\top}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u,\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u\big)^{\top}+{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u,\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u\big)^{\top},\ t\in{\mathcal{I}},

(4.112)

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}
is as in (
4.108
).

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma 2.6 in
[
94
, p.355]
. For brevity, we denote
N_{t}:=\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}+V_{t}-\lambda)
,
q:=\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u
,
{\mathbf{q}}:=(q,q)
and let
P_{t}
be the orthogonal projections onto
{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}
. Then
P_{t}\in C^{1}\big({\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\big)
for some open interval
{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq{\mathcal{J}}
centered at
t_{0}
(see, e.g.,
[
26
, Theorem 3.9]
,
[
92
, Theorem 5.10]
). We now consider the projections in
(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\times(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
given by

\widehat{P}_{t}:=\begin{bmatrix}P_{t}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},\ \widehat{Q}_{t}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&Q_{t}\end{bmatrix},

so that
\widehat{P}_{t}+\widehat{Q}_{t}=\Pi_{\lambda,t}
,
\text{\rm{ran}}(\Pi_{\lambda,t})=\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}={\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
.
Using the definition of
\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}
, see (
4.105
) and (
4.108
), we define

h_{t}\in\text{\rm{ran}}(P_{t})\subset\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},\ g_{t}\in\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t})\subset\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},

(4.113)

such that

(h_{t},0)^{\top}=\widehat{P}_{t}({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}{\mathbf{q}})\text{ and $(0,g_{t})^{\top}=\widehat{Q}_{t}({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}{\mathbf{q}})$},

(4.114)

and so
h_{{t_{0}}}=g_{{t_{0}}}=q
. Since
t\mapsto{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}
,
t\mapsto P_{t}
and
t\mapsto Q_{t}
are
C^{1}
, we know that the maps
t\mapsto h_{t}
and
t\mapsto g_{t}
are
C^{1}
.
As above, employing Hypothesis
4.18
and
\operatorname{ker}\mathrm{T}_{t}=\operatorname{dom}(A)
, see Proposition
2.2
(1), we conclude that the restriction

\mathrm{T}_{t}\upharpoonright_{N_{t}}:N_{t}\to\text{\rm{ran}}(P_{t})\subset\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H},

(4.115)

of
\mathrm{T}_{t}
to
N_{t}
is a bijection. Therefore, there is a unique vector
w_{t}\in N_{t}
satisfying
\mathrm{T}_{t}w_{t}=h_{t}
. Assertions (
4.111
), (
4.112
) hold with this choice of
w_{t}
and
g_{t}
.

It remains to show that the function
t\mapsto w_{t}
is in
C^{1}({\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+})
.
Let
U_{t}
denote the
C^{1}
family of boundedly invertible transformation operators in
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
that split the projections
{\mathcal{P}}_{N_{t}}
onto
N_{t}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{N_{{t_{0}}}}
onto
N_{{t_{0}}}
so that the identity

U_{t}{\mathcal{P}}_{N_{{t_{0}}}}={\mathcal{P}}_{N_{t}}U_{t}
holds, and
U_{t}:N_{{t_{0}}}\mapsto N_{t}
are bijections for
t
near
t_{0}
, cf.
[
94
, Remark 2.4]
,

[
44
, Remark 3.5]
,
[
47
, Section IV.1]
,
[
63
, Remark 6.11]
. We temporarily introduce
v_{t}\in N_{{t_{0}}}
by
v_{t}=U_{t}^{-1}w_{t}
so that
\mathrm{T}_{t}w_{t}=h_{t}
yields
(\mathrm{T}_{t}\circ\,U_{t})v_{t}=h_{t}
.
The map
\mathrm{T}_{t}\circ\,U_{t}\big|_{N_{{t_{0}}}}:N_{{t_{0}}}\to\text{\rm{ran}}(P_{t})
is a bijection and

t\mapsto\mathrm{T}_{t}\circ\,U_{t}\big|_{N_{{t_{0}}}}
is in
C^{1}\big({\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{B}}(N_{{t_{0}}},\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})\big)
by the assumptions in the lemma. Since

w_{t}=U_{t}\circ\big(\mathrm{T}_{t}\circ\,U_{t}\big)^{-1}h_{t}
, the function
t\mapsto w_{t}
is
C^{1}
because each of the three terms in the composition is
C^{1}
.
∎

Theorem 4.20
.

Under Hypothesis
4.3
, let
(\lambda,t_{0})
be a conjugate point satisfying Hypothesis
4.18
. Let
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
, with
\lambda=\lambda(t_{0})
,
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
be as in Theorem
4.5
, and let
{\mathbf{q}}_{j}:=(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})^{\top}\in\Upsilon_{\lambda,t_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{D}
. Then the slope of the eigenvalue curves satisfies

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}({\mathbf{q}}_{j},{\mathbf{q}}_{j}),\ 1\leq j\leq m,

(4.116)

where
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
is the Maslov form introduced in (
4.109
).

Proof.

For a fixed
j
, let
(w_{t},g_{t})
be as in Lemma
4.19
with
u:=u_{j}
. Differentiating

A^{*}w_{t}+V_{t}w_{t}-\lambda w_{t}=0,

(4.117)

at
t_{0}
and multiplying the result by
w_{{t_{0}}}=u_{j}
we get

\langle(A^{*}+V_{{t_{0}}}-\lambda)\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}},\,w_{{t_{0}}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\langle\dot{V}_{{t_{0}}}{w}_{{t_{0}}},\,w_{{t_{0}}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0.

(4.118)

Using the Green identity (
4.1
) with
u=\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}}
and
v=w_{{t_{0}}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle(A^{*}+V_{{t_{0}}}-\lambda)\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}},\,w_{{t_{0}}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}},\,(A^{*}+V_{{t_{0}}}-\lambda)w_{{t_{0}}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\\
&\quad+\langle\Gamma_{1{t_{0}}}\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}},\,\Gamma_{0{t_{0}}}w_{{t_{0}}}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}-\langle\Gamma_{0{t_{0}}}\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}},\,\Gamma_{1{t_{0}}}w_{{t_{0}}}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}\end{split}

(4.119)

Combining (
4.117
) and (
4.119
) yields

\omega\big(\mathrm{T}_{{t_{0}}}\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}},\,\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\big)+\langle\dot{V}_{{t_{0}}}u_{j},\,u_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0.

(4.120)

Next, (
4.109
) and (
4.112
) yield

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{{t_{0}}}({\mathbf{q}}_{j},{\mathbf{q}}_{j})=\omega\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}\big|_{t={t_{0}}}(\mathrm{T}_{t}w_{t})\big)-\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\,\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}}).

(4.121)

Since
g_{t}=Q_{t}g_{t}
we have

\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}}=\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}g_{t_{0}}+Q_{{t_{0}}}\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}}=\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}+Q_{{t_{0}}}\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}}.

Utilizing this, the fact that
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{{t_{0}}})
is Lagrangian and
\mathrm{T}u_{j}\in\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{t_{0}})
we get

\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\,\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}})=\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\,\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}+Q_{{t_{0}}}\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}})=\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}).

(4.122)

Then (
4.120
), (
4.121
), and (
4.122
) yield

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{m}_{{t_{0}}}({\mathbf{q}}_{j},{\mathbf{q}}_{j})&=\omega\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\big)+\omega\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\,\mathrm{T}_{{t_{0}}}\dot{w}_{{t_{0}}}\big)\\
&\hskip 133.72786pt-\omega(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})\\
&\quad=\omega\big(\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\dot{\mathrm{T}}_{t_{0}}u_{j}\big)+\langle\dot{V}_{{t_{0}}}u_{j},\,u_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\\
&\hskip 133.72786pt+\omega(\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}),\end{split}

(4.123)

where we used
\omega(\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j},\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}
, see (
3.77
). Comparing (
4.123
) and (
3.75
) one infers (
4.116
) as required.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

Formula (
4.116
) in Theorem
4.20
yields a fundamental relation between the Maslov index and the spectral flow of the family of operators
H_{t}={\mathcal{A}}_{t}+V_{t}
satisfying the condition
\mathrm{T}_{t}(\operatorname{dom}(H_{t}))={\mathcal{F}}_{t}
for a given family of Lagrangian subspaces
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
. This relation goes back to the celebrated Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Theorem and it has been a subject of intensive research ever since, see, e.g.,
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
35
,
44
,
113
]
and many more references therein. We will briefly comment on the equality of the Maslov index and the spectral flow. First, we recall the definition of the Maslov index via crossing forms. For a fixed
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
from now on we assume that Hypothesis
4.18
is satisfied for all
t=t_{0}\in[0,1]
. Then, given the subspaces defined in (
4.105
), and assuming that all conjugate points
(\lambda,t_{0})
for
t_{0}\in[0,1]
are non-degenerate (in the sense that the quadratic form
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
from (
4.109
) is non-degenerate), one defines the Maslov index by the formula

{\rm Mas}\big(\Upsilon_{\lambda_{0},t}:t\in[0,1]\big)=-m_{-}(0)+\sum_{0<t_{0}<1}\big(m_{+}(t_{0})-m_{-}(t_{0})\big)+m_{+}(1),

(4.124)

where the summation is taken over all
t_{0}
such that
(\lambda,t_{0})
is a conjugate point and we denote by
m_{+}(t_{0})
, respectively,
m_{-}(t_{0})
the number of positive, respectively, negative squares of the quadratic form
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
at the conjugate point. Next, we recall the definition of the spectral flow: The spectral flow
{\rm SpF}_{\lambda_{0}}(H_{t}:t\in[0,1])
for the family of operators
H_{t}
is the net count of the eigenvalues of
H_{t}
passing through
\lambda_{0}
as
t
changes from
t=0
to
t=1
and is defined as follows, cf., e.g.,
[
28
, Appendix]
. Take a partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=1
and
N
intervals
[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}]
such that
a_{\ell}<\lambda_{0}<b_{\ell}
and

a_{\ell},b_{\ell}\notin\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})
for all
t\in[t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}]
,
1\leq\ell\leq N
. Then the spectral flow is defined by

{\rm SpF}_{\lambda_{0}}(H_{t}:t\in[0,1])=\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\sum_{a_{\ell}\leq\lambda<\lambda_{0}}\big(\dim\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{\ell-1}}-\lambda)-\dim\operatorname{ker}(H_{t_{\ell}}-\lambda)\big).

(4.125)

By our assumptions, due to part (i) in Hypothesis
4.18
,
\lambda_{0}
does not belong to the essential spectrum of the operator
H_{t}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Moreover, let us assume, in addition, that for each
t_{0}\in[0,1]
such that
\lambda_{0}\in\operatorname{Spec}_{\rm disc}(H_{t_{0}})

the inequality
\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})\neq 0
holds for all
j=1,\dots,m
.
Here,
m=m(t_{0})
is the multiplicity of the isolated eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
of
H_{t_{0}}
, and

\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}
are the eigenvalues of
H_{t}
as in Theorem
3.26
(2) and Theorem
4.5
(2) for
t\in[t_{0}^{\prime},t_{0}^{\prime\prime}]
near
t_{0}
. With no loss of generality
t=t_{0}
could be assumed to be the only point in
[t_{0}^{\prime},t_{0}^{\prime\prime}]
such that
\lambda_{0}\in\operatorname{Spec}(H_{t})
. By our assumptions and formula (
4.116
) in Theorem
4.20
the quadratic
form
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
defined in (
4.109
) is non-degenerate and
m_{+}(t_{0})
, respectively,
m_{-}(t_{0})
is equal to the number of
j
’s such that the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(t)
moves through
\lambda_{0}
in the positive, respectively, negative direction as
t
changes from
t_{0}^{\prime}
to
t_{0}^{\prime\prime}
. Formulas (
4.124
) and (
4.125
) now show that

{\rm Mas}\big(\Upsilon_{\lambda_{0},t}:t\in[t_{0}^{\prime},t_{0}^{\prime\prime}]\big)={\rm SpF}_{\lambda_{0}}(H_{t}:t\in[t_{0}^{\prime},t_{0}^{\prime\prime}])
.
Passing to a partition of
[0,1]
then gives

{\rm Mas}\big(\Upsilon_{\lambda_{0},t}:t\in[0,1]\big)={\rm SpF}_{\lambda_{0}}(H_{t}:t\in[0,1]),

(4.126)

the desired equality of the Maslov index and the spectral flow.

\Diamond

5.
Hadamard-type formula for elliptic operators via Dirichlet and Neumann traces

In this section concerns self-adjoint realizations of second order elliptic operators on bounded domains. We begin by discussing a resolvent difference formula, see Proposition
5.1
, an Hadamard-type formula, (
5.20
), and asymptotic resolvent expansions, Theorem
5.2
, for the elliptic operators (
5.1
) posted on bounded domains with smooth boundary. We deduce all these results from Theorem
3.26
by appropriately choosing the trace maps. The main technical issue is to validate Hypotheses
3.23
and
3.13
, which is done in Proposition
5.4
. Next, these results are utilized to give simple and unified proofs of Friedlander’s Theorem
[
62
, Theorem 1.1]
, see Example
5.5
, and Rohleder’s Theorem
[
115
, Theorem 3.2]
, see Example
5.6
. Furthermore, in Section
5.3
we consider the heat equation with space-dependent diffusion coefficient equipped with Robin boundary conditions so that both the equation and the boundary conditions contain a physically relevant parameter, the thermal conductivity. The results in this section provide, in particular, a new proof of the fact that the temperature of a non-homogeneous material immersed into a surrounding medium of constant temperature depends continuously on the thermal conductivity of the material.

5.1.
Elliptic operators

On a
C^{\infty}
-smooth bounded domain
\Omega
we consider the following differential expression,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}:&=-\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\partial_{j}\mathtt{a}_{jk}\partial_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathtt{a}_{j}\partial_{j}-\partial_{j}{\mathtt{a}_{j}}+\mathtt{q},\\
&=-\operatorname{div}(\mathtt{A}\nabla)+\mathtt{a}\cdot\nabla-\nabla\cdot\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{q},\end{split}

(5.1)

with coefficients
\mathtt{A}=\{\mathtt{a}_{ij}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
,
\mathtt{a}:=\{\mathtt{a}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
satisfying, for some
c=c({\mathcal{L}})>0
,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j,k=1}^{n}\mathtt{a}_{jk}(x)\xi_{k}\overline{\xi_{j}}\geq c\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}|\xi_{j}|^{2},x\in\overline{\Omega},\xi=\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathtt{a}_{jk},\mathtt{a}_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega};{\mathbb{R}}),\mathtt{q}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}),\mathtt{a}_{jk}(x)={\mathtt{a}_{kj}(x)},1\leq j,k\leq n.

(5.3)

Associated with
{\mathcal{L}}
is the following space of distributions,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{s}(\Omega):=\{u\in H^{s}(\Omega):{\mathcal{L}}u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\},\ s\geq 0,

(5.4)

equipped with the norm

\|u\|_{s}:=\left(\|u\|_{H^{s}({\Omega})}^{2}+\|{\mathcal{L}}u\|_{L^{2}({\Omega})}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

(5.5)

where
{\mathcal{L}}u
should be understood in the sense of distributions. Let us introduce two operators acting in
L^{2}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{min}f:={\mathcal{L}}f,\ f\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{min}):=H^{2}_{0}(\Omega),

(5.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{max}f:={\mathcal{L}}f,\ f\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{max}):={\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega).

(5.7)

The operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{min}
is closed, symmetric, and
({\mathcal{L}}_{min})^{*}={\mathcal{L}}_{max}
. Associated with
{\mathcal{L}}
is a first order trace operator
\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega))
which is a unique extension of the co-normal derivative

{\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}}u:=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\mathtt{a}_{jk}\nu_{j}\gamma_{{}_{D}}(\partial_{k}u)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\mathtt{a}_{j}}\nu_{j}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u,u\in H^{2}(\Omega)

(5.8)

to the space
{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
(here,
(\nu_{1},\cdots,\,\nu_{n})
is the outward unit normal on
\partial\Omega
). Then the following Green identity holds,

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{L}}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\langle u,{\mathcal{L}}v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}={\langle{\gamma_{{}_{D}}u,\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}}v\rangle}_{{-1/2}}-\overline{\langle{\gamma_{{}_{D}}v,\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}}u\rangle_{{-1/2}}},

(5.9)

for all
u,v\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
. In order to rewrite this identity in a form compatible with (
2.5
) let
\Phi
denote the Riesz isomorphism
\Phi\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))
as in (
4.31
)
and define

\Gamma_{0}:=\gamma_{{}_{D}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)),\ \Gamma_{1}:=-\Phi\gamma_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)).

(5.10)

Then we have, for all
u,v\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle{\mathcal{L}}_{max}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&-\langle u,{\mathcal{L}}_{max}v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&={\langle\Gamma_{1}u,\Gamma_{0}v\rangle_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}u,\Gamma_{1}v\rangle_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.\end{split}

(5.11)

We claim that Hypotheses
2.1
and
3.1
are satisfied for

A={\mathcal{L}}_{min},{\mathcal{H}}_{+}={\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),\Gamma_{0}=\gamma_{{}_{D}},\Gamma_{1}=-\Phi\gamma_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}.

(5.12)

Since we already checked the Green identity, (
5.11
),
to justify the claim it remains to show that
\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})
is dense in
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and that
{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
is dense in
{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
. By
[
73
, Proposition 2.1]
,
[
18
, Section 4.3]
one has

(\gamma_{{}_{D}},\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}})(H^{2}(\Omega))=H^{3/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),

and the right-hand side is dense in
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
. By
[
73
, Theorem 3.2]
,
H^{2}(\Omega)
is dense in
{\mathcal{D}}^{s}(\Omega),s<2
, hence
{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
is dense in
{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Under the assumptions on
{\mathcal{L}}
imposed in this section, for any two self-adjoint extensions
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{min}
with domains containing in
{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
and
\zeta\not\in(\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})\cup\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{2}))
, the following resolvent difference formula holds,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{2}-\zeta)^{-1}-({\mathcal{L}}_{1}-\zeta)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{L}}_{2}-\overline{\zeta})^{-1})^{*}\,J\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}-\zeta)^{-1},

(5.13)

where
\mathrm{T}=[\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}]^{\top}
is defined in (
5.10
), and

(\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{L}}_{2}-\overline{\zeta})^{-1})^{*}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),L^{2}(\Omega)).

Proof.

The results follows directly from (
2.22
).
∎

5.2.
Hadamard-type formulas for Robin elliptic operators, L. Friedlander’s and J. Rohleder’s inequalities

In this section we obtain an Hadamard-type formula for a one-parameter family of differential operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}u={\mathcal{L}}u
as in (
5.1
) for which the dependence on the parameter
t
enters through the Robin boundary condition
\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u=\Theta_{t}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u
, see Theorem
5.2
. We will utilize Theorem
3.26
by choosing the symmetric operator
A
, the function spaces
{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{+},\mathfrak{H}
, and the trace operator
\mathrm{T}
as indicated in (
5.12
). The main challenge is to check Hypothesis
3.16
which in this setting reads as follows,

\big\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega))}=o(1),\ t\rightarrow t_{0},

(5.14)

and can be reduced to showing that for some constant
c>0
one has
the inequality

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq c\big(\|{\mathcal{L}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\big),u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}),

(5.15)

for
t
near
t_{0}
. We discuss the reduction and give the proof of this inequality in Proposition
5.4
. Throughout this section we will make use of the continuous embedding
\iota:H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
and its adjoint
\iota^{*}:L^{2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose that, in addition to the assumptions on
{\mathcal{L}}
listed in Subsection
5.1
, we are given a mapping
t\mapsto\Theta_{t}
belonging to
C^{1}([0,1],L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega,{\mathbb{R}}))
. Then for
t\in[0,1]
the Robbin elliptic operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{L}}_{t}:\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t})\subset L^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega),\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{t}u={\mathcal{L}}u,\\
&u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t})=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega):\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u=\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\gamma_{{}_{D}}u\},\end{split}

(5.16)

is self-adjoint, where
\iota
denotes the embedding of
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
into
L^{2}(\Omega)
. The following resolvent difference formula holds,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}-({\mathcal{L}}_{s}-\zeta)^{-1}=\big(\gamma_{{}_{D}}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\overline{\zeta})^{-1}\big)^{*}(\Theta_{t}-\Theta_{s})\big(\gamma_{{}_{D}}({\mathcal{L}}_{s}-\zeta)^{-1}\big),

(5.17)

for
t,s\in[0,1]
,
\zeta\not\in(\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t})\cup\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{s}))
. Moreover, the mapping

t\mapsto({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))

(5.18)

is well defined for
t
near
t_{0}
as long as
\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}})
. This mapping is differentiable at
t_{0}
and satisfies the following Riccati equaiton,

\displaystyle\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}|_{t=t_{0}}\big(({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}\big)=\big(\gamma_{{}_{D}}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-\overline{\zeta})^{-1}\big)^{*}\big(\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}|_{t=t_{0}}\Theta_{t}\big)\big(\gamma_{{}_{D}}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-\zeta)^{-1}\big).

(5.19)

Finally, if
\lambda(t_{0})
is an isolated eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}
of multiplicity
m\geq 1
then there exist a choice of orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-\lambda(t_{0}))

and a labeling of eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
, for
t
near
t_{0}
, such that

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=-\langle\dot{\Theta}_{t_{0}}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{j},\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},1\leq j\leq m.

(5.20)

Proof.

We will employ Theorem
3.26
. The proof consists of two steps. First, we derive (
5.17
) from (
3.79
). We can use (
3.79
) because Hypothesis
3.4
is trivially satisfied. Second, we derive
(
5.19
) and (
5.20
) from (
3.81
) and (
3.82
). To apply (
3.81
) and (
3.82
) we need to verify Hypotheses
3.13
and
3.16
. They are satisfied by Proposition
5.4
given next; the proof of this proposition uses formula (
5.17
) proved in the first step.

To proceed, we choose
{\mathcal{H}}_{+},{\mathcal{D}},A
as in (
5.12
) and rewrite the Robin condition
\gamma_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u=\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\gamma_{{}_{D}}u
in the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
as
\Phi\gamma_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u=\Phi\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\gamma_{{}_{D}}u

X_{t}\Gamma_{0}u+Y_{t}\Gamma_{1}u=0,\text{ where we set }\,X_{t}:=\Phi\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota,Y_{t}:=I.

(5.21)

It is worth noting that
X_{t}
just defined is self-adjoint in
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
since for
\phi,\psi\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
one has

\displaystyle\langle\Phi\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\phi,\psi\rangle_{1/2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\langle\psi,\Phi\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\phi,\psi\rangle_{1/2}}=\overline{\langle\psi,\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\phi,\psi\rangle_{-1/2}}

(5.22)

\displaystyle=\overline{\langle\iota\psi,\Theta_{t}\iota\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}}=\langle\iota\phi,\Theta_{t}\iota\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}

(5.23)

\displaystyle=\langle\phi,\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\psi\rangle_{-1/2}=\langle\phi,\Phi\iota^{*}\Theta_{t}\iota\psi\rangle_{1/2}.

(5.24)

Continuity of
\Theta_{t}
with respect to
t
and Theorem
3.18
with
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}:={\mathcal{L}}_{t}
,
V_{t}:=0
,
\mathrm{T}_{t}:=[\gamma_{{}_{D}},-\Phi\gamma_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}]^{\top}
yield that the map
t\mapsto R_{t}(\zeta):=({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}
is well defined for
t
near
t_{0}
.
Next, with
W
defined in (
2.20
), we observe that
R_{t}(\zeta)u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{t})
yields

\big(W(X_{t},I)\big)\mathrm{T}R_{t}(\zeta)u=-\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\zeta)u=-\gamma_{{}_{D}}R_{t}(\zeta)u\text{ for all $u\in L^{2}(\Omega)$}.

(5.25)

This can be checked directly or by noting that
\phi=\big(W(X_{t},I)\big)\mathrm{T}R_{t}(\zeta)u
is the unique vector satisfying the relations

\Gamma_{0}R_{t}(\zeta)u=-\phi
,
\Gamma_{1}R_{t}(\zeta)u=X_{t}\phi
, cf. (
3.83
).
This observation together with (
3.79
) yield (
5.17
).
We can now involve Proposition
5.4
given next and verify
Hypotheses
3.13
and
3.16
in the present setting.
Thus, Theorem
3.26
applies and therefore
(
5.19
) and (
5.20
) follow from (
3.81
) and (
3.82
) with
\phi_{j}=-\Gamma_{0}u_{j}
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

It is worth comparing Theorems
4.11
and
5.2
for the case
{\mathcal{L}}=-\Delta
where both theorems apply. The major difference is in the type of trace operators utilized in each theorem. In Theorem
4.11
we use
\mathrm{T}=[-\tau_{{}_{N}},\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}]^{\top}
which is defined on the entire space
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}=\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{max})
and is surjective, while in Theorem
5.2
we have
\mathrm{T}=[\gamma_{{}_{D}},-\Phi\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}]^{\top}
which is defined only on a dense subset
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}={\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
. We note that the latter trace operator is local while the former is not. In addition, these trace maps do not match even on smooth functions on
\Omega
. Another major technical difference is that Hypotheses
3.13
and
3.16
are automatically satisfied in one case but not in the other.

\Diamond

Proposition 5.4
.

Under assumptions of Theorem
5.2
one has

\displaystyle\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega))}={\mathcal{O}}(1),\ t\rightarrow t_{0},

(5.26)

\displaystyle\big\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega))}=o(1),\ t\rightarrow t_{0},

(5.27)

for all
t_{0}\in[0,1]
. In other words, Hypotheses
3.13
and
3.16
hold for
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}:={\mathcal{L}}_{t}
.

Proof.

To prove (
5.26
) it is enough to show that there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\|u\|_{{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq c\|{\mathcal{L}}u-{\bf i}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)},u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}),

(5.28)

for all
t\in[0,1]
. By the definition of
{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
-norm, see (
5.5
), we need to prove that

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq c(\|{\mathcal{L}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}),u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}).

(5.29)

To show this, we first notice that for
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t})
one has

\langle\mathtt{A}\nabla u,\nabla u\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\langle{\mathcal{L}}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\langle\mathtt{q}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-\langle\Theta_{t}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u,\gamma_{{}_{D}}u\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}.

(5.30)

Using the Cauchy–Schwartz inequality and (
5.2
) we get

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq c(\|{\mathcal{L}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\Theta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}\|\gamma_{{}_{D}}u\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}),

(5.31)

for
c>0
(which is
t-
and
u-
independent). Let us recall from
[
67
, Lemma 2.5]
the inequality

\|\gamma_{{}_{D}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\beta(\varepsilon)\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)},\text{ where $\varepsilon>0$ and $\beta(\varepsilon)\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{=}{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-1})$}.

(5.32)

Thus, continuing (
5.31
) we infer

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq c\Big(\|{\mathcal{L}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\varepsilon\|\Theta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

(5.33)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt+\beta(\varepsilon)\|\Theta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Big)

(5.34)

for some
c>0
. Taking
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small yields (
5.29
) and thus (
5.26
).

Starting the proof of (
5.27
), we first show that

\big\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}-({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}\big\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega))}=o(1),\ t\rightarrow t_{0}.

(5.35)

We denote
R(t):=({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}
and
recall that we may use resolvent difference formula (
5.17
) already established in the first part of the proof of Theorem
5.2
. It yields

\displaystyle\langle R(t)u-R(t_{0})u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\langle(\Theta_{t_{0}}-\Theta_{t})\gamma_{{}_{D}}R(t)u,\gamma_{{}_{D}}R(t_{0})v\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}

(5.36)

for all
u,v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
.
For
v\in(H^{1}(\Omega))^{*}=H^{-1}(\Omega)
we view
w:=R(t_{0})v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
as the solution to the boundary value problem
({\mathcal{L}}-{\bf i})w=v
,
\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}w=\Theta_{t_{0}}\gamma_{{}_{D}}w
. Using a well-known elliptic estimate
\|w\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\leq c\|v\|_{H^{-1}(\Omega)}
from
[
103
, Theorem 4.11(i)]
, the operator
R(t_{0})
can be extended to an operator in
{\mathcal{B}}((H^{1}(\Omega))^{*},H^{1}(\Omega))
.
So, (
5.36
) can be extended as follows,

{}_{H^{1}(\Omega))}\langle R(t)u-R(t_{0})u,v\rangle_{(H^{1}(\Omega))^{*}}=\langle(\Theta_{t_{0}}-\Theta_{t})\gamma_{{}_{D}}R(t)u,\gamma_{{}_{D}}R(t_{0})v\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)},

(5.37)

now for all
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
v\in(H^{1}(\Omega))^{*}
. Hence,

\displaystyle|_{H^{1}(\Omega))}\langle R(t)u

\displaystyle-R(t_{0})u,v\rangle_{(H^{1}(\Omega))^{*}}|\leq\|\Theta_{t_{0}}-\Theta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}\|\gamma_{{}_{D}}\|^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))}

(5.38)

\displaystyle\times\|R(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega))}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|R(t_{0})\|_{{\mathcal{B}}((H^{1}(\Omega))^{*},H^{1}(\Omega))}\|v\|_{(H^{1}(\Omega))^{*}}.

(5.39)

Since
\|R(t)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega))}={\mathcal{O}}(1)
by (
5.26
), and
\|\Theta_{t_{0}}-\Theta_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}=o(1)
,
t\rightarrow t_{0},
the above inequality gives (
5.35
).
We now combine (
5.35
) with the estimate

\displaystyle\big\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}u

\displaystyle-({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}u\big\|_{{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}=\big\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}u-({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}u\big\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}

(5.40)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\|{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}u-{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}u\big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

(5.41)

\displaystyle\leq 2\big\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}u-({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-{\bf i})^{-1}u\big\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2},u\in L^{2}(\Omega),

(5.42)

finishing the proof of (
5.27
). ∎

Example 5.5
.

Theorem
5.2
can be used in proving the celebrated Friedlander Inequalities
\lambda_{D,k}\geq\lambda_{N,k+1}
,
k=1,2,\dots
, for the eigenvalues of the Dirichlet and Neumann Laplacians, see
[
62
]
, which was improved in
[
60
]
to state that
\lambda_{D,k}>\lambda_{N,k+1}
, see also
[
19
,
61
,
68
,
116
]
for further advances, detailed bibliography and a historical account of this beautiful subject. Also, we refer to Example
5.10
for connections to the Maslov index.
The proof of the Friedlander Inequalities consists of two major steps. First, one proves that the counting functions of the
Dirichlet and Neumann boundary problems differ by a number of negative eigenvalues of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator, see (
5.68
) below. Second, one proves the existence of a nonnegative eigenvalue of the latter. The first step involves a one-parameter family of Robin boundary value problems giving a homotopy of the Dirichlet to the Neumann boundary problem.
The critical issue here is to show monotonicity of the eigenvalues of the Robin problems
with respect to the parameter, and this is where the results of the current paper help. (In fact, monotonicity holds not merely for the Laplacian but for general elliptic operators as described in Subsection
5.1
).
Indeed, formula (
5.20
) in Theorem
5.2
with

{\mathcal{L}}=-\Delta
and
\Theta_{t}=-\cot(\frac{\pi}{2}t)
shows that the eigenvalues
\lambda=\lambda(t)
of the Robin problem

\begin{cases}{\mathcal{L}}u=\lambda u\text{\ in\ }\ \Omega,\\
\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}t)\gamma_{{}_{N}}u+\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}t)\gamma_{{}_{D}}u=0\text{\ on $\partial\Omega$ for $t\in[0,1]$,}\end{cases}

(5.43)

are monotonically decreasing with respect to
t\in[0,1]
. We note that

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(0)=\lambda_{D,k}\leq\lambda_{D,k+1}=\lambda_{k+1}(0)\text{ and }

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(1)=\lambda_{N,k}\leq\lambda_{N,k+1}=\lambda_{k+1}(1),k=1,2,\ldots,

are the Dirichlet and Neumann eigenvalues. From this point on, the arguments given in
[
62
]
and
[
60
]
are as follows. Monotonicity in
t
of the Robin eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}(t)
just proved, and the standard inequalities
\lambda_{D,k}\geq\lambda_{N,k}
show the strict inequalities
\lambda_{D,k}>\lambda_{N,k+1}
provided we know the fact, cf.
[
62
, Lemma 1.3]
, that for each
\lambda
there is a
t\in[0,1]
such that (
5.43
) has a nontrivial solution. This fact is equivalent to the existence of a positive eigenvalue
\cot(\frac{\pi}{2}t)
of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator when
\lambda\notin\operatorname{Spec}(-\Delta_{D})
, and its proof has been carried out in
[
62
]
and
[
60
]
for the Laplacian using the minimax principle and infinitely many linearly independent explicit functions
e^{i\eta\cdot x}
, with
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\|\eta\|^{2}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=\lambda
, that satisfy
-\Delta(e^{i\eta\cdot x})=\lambda e^{i\eta\cdot x}
.

\Diamond

Example 5.6
.

We will now derive from Theorem
5.2
an elegant result in
[
115
, Theorem 3.2]
regarding monotonicity of Robin eigenvalues. Given
\Theta^{(\ell)}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}})
,
\ell=0,1
, we define the Robin operators
{\mathcal{L}}^{(\ell)}u={\mathcal{L}}u
such that

\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}^{(\ell)})=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega):\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u=\Theta^{(\ell)}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u\}

for the elliptic differential expression in (
5.1
). We let
