\lambda_{1}({\mathcal{L}}^{(\ell)})\leq\lambda_{2}({\mathcal{L}}^{(\ell)})\leq\dots
denote the eigenvalues of

{\mathcal{L}}^{(\ell)}
counting multiplicities. Assume that
\Theta^{(0)}\leq\Theta^{(1)}
. We will give a new proof of J. Rohleder’s result stating that

if
\Theta^{(0)}<\Theta^{(1)}
on a set of positive measure then
\lambda_{k}({\mathcal{L}}^{(0)})>\lambda_{k}({\mathcal{L}}^{(1)})

(5.44)

for
k=1,2,\dots
. Denote
\Theta_{t}=\Theta^{(0)}+t(\Theta^{(1)}-\Theta^{(0)})
for
t\in[0,1]
and introduce operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
as in Theorem
5.2
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}={\mathcal{L}}^{(0)}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}={\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}
. Denoting by
\lambda_{k}(t):=\lambda_{k}({\mathcal{L}}_{t})
the eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
counting multiplicities and by
u_{k}
the respective eigenfunctions, formula (
5.20
) implies

\frac{\hbox{\rm d}\lambda_{k}(t)}{\hbox{\rm d}t}=-\langle(\Theta^{(1)}-\Theta^{(0)})\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{k},\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}<0\text{, $k=1,2,\dots$, $t\in[0,1]$}

(5.45)

because
\Theta^{(0)}<\Theta^{(1)}
on a set of positive measure, thus proving (
5.44
). Let us elaborate on some additional consequences of monotonicity of eigenvalues.
As the eigenvalue curves
t\mapsto\lambda_{k}(t)
are strictly monotone and continuous we obtain the following count for the eigenvalues, see Figure
1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\#\{k:\lambda_{k}({\mathcal{L}}^{(1)})<\lambda\})-(\#\{k:\lambda_{k}&({\mathcal{L}}^{(0)})<\lambda\})\\
&=\sum_{t\in[0,1]}\dim\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\lambda).\end{split}

(5.46)

A weaker version of this counting formula

\displaystyle(\#\{k:\lambda_{k}({\mathcal{L}}^{(1)})<\lambda\})-(\#\{k:\lambda_{k}({\mathcal{L}}^{(0)})<\lambda\})\geq\dim\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{L}}^{(0)}-\lambda),

(5.47)

was obtained by J. Rohleder
[
115
, (3.4)]
by variational methods. This is a key estimate in
[
115
]
leading to (
5.44
) in the original proof. Now, (
5.46
) can be viewed as a prequel to Section
5.5
, where the left-hand side of (
5.46
) is treated as the spectral flow of the family
\{{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
through
\lambda
and the right-hand side is viewed as the Maslov index of a certain path of Lagrangian planes. The equality between the Maslov index and the spectral flow in a very general setting has been recently investigated in, for example,
[
44
,
45
,
46
,
92
,
94
]
and the vast literature cited therein.

Figure 1.
Illustration of (
5.45
), (
5.46
)

\Diamond

5.3.
Continuous dependence of solutions to heat equation on thermal conductivity

In this section we apply our general results to give a new proof that solutions to the linear homogeneous heat equation depend continuously on a certain physically relevant parameter present in both the operator and the boundary condition. The assertions of this type have a long and distinguished history, and have been resolved even for quite general Wentzell boundary conditions. We refer the reader to
[
42
,
43
]
where one can also find further literature. We did not attempt to cover the case of Wentzell boundary conditions anywhere in this paper but remark parenthetically
that it is an interesting open area to develop a version of the asymptotic perturbation theory for operators equipped with this type of dynamical boundary conditions. At the moment, as in
[
70
]
, we consider the following heat equation,

\begin{cases}&u_{\mathtt{t}}(\mathtt{t},x)=\kappa\rho(x)\Delta_{x}u(\mathtt{t},x),x\in\Omega,\mathtt{t}\geq 0,\\
&-\kappa\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}=u,\text{\ on\ }\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

(5.48)

describing the temperature
u
of a material in the region
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with thermal conductivity
\kappa
immersed in a surrounding medium of zero temperature. Here,
1/\rho(x)
is the product of the density of the material times its heat capacity. The continuous dependence of the temperature
u
on the thermal conductivity
\kappa
with respect to
L^{2}(\Omega)
norm follows from Theorem
5.7
proved below, which is a version of Theorem
5.2
. To sketch the argument, we consider the self-adjoint operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}:=-\kappa\Delta
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}:\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})\subset L^{2}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
with
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega):-\kappa\gamma_{{}_{N}}u=\gamma_{{}_{D}}u\}
. Then by Trotter–Kato Approximation Theorem
[
58
, Theorem III.4.8]
, the family of semigroups
\{e^{-\mathtt{t}\rho{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}}\}_{\mathtt{t}\geq 0}
is strongly continuous in
\kappa
uniformly for
\mathtt{t}
from compact subsets whenever
\kappa\mapsto(\rho{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}-\zeta)^{-1}
is continuous as a mapping from
(0,+\infty)
to
{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))
for some
\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})
(we note that
\rho{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
is not necessarily self-adjoint). The next theorem gives a rigorous argument for the required continuity of the resolvent in a slightly more general form. (In the next theorem, to keep up with notation used in the rest of the paper, we denote the parameter with respect to which the continuity is established by
t
, not by
\kappa
; this is not to be confused with notation
\mathtt{t}
for time used in (
5.48
)).

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be a bounded open set with
C^{\infty}
-smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
. We assume that
t\mapsto\alpha_{t}
,
t\mapsto\beta_{t}
are mappings in
C([0,1],L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}))
such that
\alpha_{t}^{2}(x)+\beta_{t}^{2}(x)\not=0
for
x\in\partial\Omega
,
t\in[0,1]
, and
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
is a mapping in
C([0,1],C(\overline{\Omega};{\mathbb{R}}))
such that
\inf\{\rho_{t}(x):t\in[0,1],x\in\overline{\Omega}\}>0
. Recall the differential expression
{\mathcal{L}}
from (
5.1
) and define the following operator acting in
L^{2}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}u:=\rho_{t}{\mathcal{L}}u,u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}),

(5.49)

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}):=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega):\alpha_{t}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u+\beta_{t}\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u=0\}.

(5.50)

Then the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}
is sectorial and the mapping
t\mapsto({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}-\zeta)^{-1}
lies in
C([0,1],{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega)))
for all
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho})
.

Proof.

To prove that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}
is sectorial we have to show the existence of such
\theta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
M=M(\theta)>0
that

\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho})\,\text{ and }\,\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}-\zeta)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq M|\zeta|^{-1}

provided
\zeta\neq 0
and
|\arg\zeta|\in(\theta,\pi]
.
First, we introduce a self-adjoint operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
acting in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and defined by

{\mathcal{L}}_{t}u:={\mathcal{L}}u
for
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}):=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho})

so that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}=\rho_{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
is bounded from below we may assume without loss of generality that
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\geq 0
and, given a
\theta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, use the estimate

\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\xi)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq(|\xi|\sin\theta)^{-1}\text{ for all $\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}$ such that $|\arg\xi|\in(\theta,\pi]$.}

(5.51)

Indeed, (
5.51
) follows from the estimate

\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\xi)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq|\operatorname{Im}\xi|^{-1}\leq(|\xi|\sin\theta)^{-1}

provided
|\arg\xi|\in(\theta,\frac{\pi}{2}]
and

\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\xi)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))}=\big(\operatorname{dist}(\xi,\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}))\big)^{-1}\leq|\xi|^{-1}\leq(|\xi|\sin\theta)^{-1}

provided
|\arg\xi|\in(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi]
.

Throughout the rest of this proof we take all
\inf
’s and
\sup
’s over
(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\overline{\Omega}
. We pick
\theta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
such that

(1-\sin^{2}\theta)\sup\rho_{t}(x)<\inf\rho_{t}(x)

(5.52)

and fix any
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
such that
|\arg\zeta|\in(\theta,\pi]
.
Using (
5.52
), we can choose
\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}

such that
\arg\xi=\arg\zeta
with
|\xi|
that satisfies the inequality

(1-\sin^{2}\theta)\sup\rho_{t}(x)<|\zeta||\xi|^{-1}<\inf\rho_{t}(x).

(5.53)

Dividing this by
\rho_{t}(x)
we infer

\sup\big|(|\zeta|(|\xi|\rho_{t}(x))^{-1}-1)\big|\leq\sin^{2}\theta.

(5.54)

Since
\xi\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t})
we have

\rho_{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\zeta=\rho_{t}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\xi)\big(I-({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\xi)^{-1}(\zeta\rho_{t}^{-1}-\xi)\big).

(5.55)

Combining (
5.51
) and (
5.54
)
we infer

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\xi)^{-1}(\zeta\rho_{t}^{-1}-\xi)\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))}&\leq(|\xi|\sin\theta)^{-1}\sup\big|e^{{\bf i}\arg\zeta}(|\zeta|\rho_{t}(x)^{-1}-|\xi|)\big|\\
&\leq\sin\theta<1,\end{split}

(5.56)

which by (
5.55
) gives
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}(\rho_{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{t})
and, using the second inequality in (
5.53
), the required resolvent estimate
\|({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}-\zeta)^{-1}\|_{{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq M|\zeta|^{-1}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}
is sectorial.

It is enough to prove continuity of the resolvent mapping at any
\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}
in the resolvent set of
{\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho}
. We note that if

\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t,\rho})
then
0\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\rho_{t}^{-1}\zeta)
and the identity
(\rho_{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\zeta)^{-1}=({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\rho_{t}^{-1}\zeta)^{-1}\rho_{t}^{-1}
holds. Since the map
t\mapsto\rho_{t}^{-1}
is continuous, it remains to prove continuity of the map
t\mapsto({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\rho_{t}^{-1}\zeta)^{-1}
, that is, of the resolvent of the operator
H_{t}={\mathcal{L}}_{t}-\rho_{t}^{-1}\zeta
at zero. This follows from Theorem
3.26
with
{\mathcal{A}}_{t}={\mathcal{L}}_{t}
,
V_{t}=-\rho_{t}^{-1}\zeta
,

\mathrm{T}:=(\gamma_{{}_{D}},\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}})\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{{1}/{2}}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{-{1}/{2}}(\partial\Omega))
and

\displaystyle Z_{t,s}:=[W(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t})]^{*}(\alpha_{t}\beta_{s}-\beta_{t}\alpha_{s})[W(\alpha_{s},\beta_{s})]\rightarrow 0,s\rightarrow t.

(5.57)

To justify the use of Theorem
3.26
, we note that Hypothesis
3.13
in the theorem is satisfied, that is,
({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}={\mathcal{O}}(1)
as
t\rightarrow s
in
{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}(\Omega),{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega))
. The proof of this assertion is similar to that of (
5.26
) (one imposes Robin boundary condition with
\Theta_{t}(x):=-\alpha_{t}(x)\beta_{t}^{-1}(x)
on the portion of the boundary where
\beta_{t}^{-1}(x)\not=0
and the Dirichlet condition elsewhere).
∎

5.4.
The Hadamard formula for star-shaped domains

In this section we show how
to use Theorem
3.26

to derive the classical Hadamard formula for the Schrödinger operators subject to the Dirichlet boundary condition on variable star-shaped domains.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a smooth star-shaped domain centered at zero and
\Omega_{t}=\{tx:x\in\Omega\}
be its variation for
t\in(0,1]
. We consider a smooth (
N\times N
)-matrix potential
V=V(x)
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
taking symmetric values. Suppose that
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}
is such that
\dim\operatorname{ker}(-\Delta_{D,\Omega}+V-\mu)=m\geq 1
, where
-\Delta_{D,\Omega}
denotes the Dirichlet Laplacian acting in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. We claim that there exists a choice of orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset(-\Delta_{D,\Omega}+V-\mu)
and a labeling of the eigenvalues
\{\mu_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
-\Delta_{D,\Omega_{t}}+V\upharpoonright_{\Omega_{t}}
, for
t
near
1
, such that
\mu_{j}(1)=\mu
for each
j
, and that the following classical Rayleigh–Hadamard–Rellich formula holds,
cf.
[
80
, Chapter 5]
,

\dot{\mu}_{j}(1)=-\int_{\partial\Omega}(\nu\cdot x)(\nu\cdot\nabla u_{j})^{2}{\rm d}x,1\leq j\leq m.

(5.58)

Rescaling
\Omega\ni t\mapsto tx\in\Omega_{t}

of the operator
\big(-\Delta_{D,\Omega_{t}}+V\big)\big|_{\Omega_{t}}
back to
\Omega
yields a one-parameter family of self-adjoint operators
H_{t}=-\Delta_{D,\Omega}+t^{2}V(tx)
,
t\in(0,1]
acting in the fixed space
L^{2}(\Omega)
. This family of operators fits the framework of Theorem
3.26
with

{\mathcal{A}}_{t}\equiv-\Delta_{\Omega}
,
V_{t}(x)=t^{2}V(tx)
,
\mathrm{T}_{t}=[\gamma_{{}_{D}},-t^{-1}\Phi\gamma_{{}_{N}}]^{\top}
, cf (
5.10
),
t_{0}=1
,
\lambda(t_{0})=\mu
and
Q_{t}
given by the
t
-independent projection onto the Dirichlet subspace
\{(0,g):g\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\}
for all
t
. All assumptions of Theorem
3.26
are clearly satisfied in the present setting. By the theorem there exists a choice of orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\operatorname{ker}(-\Delta_{D,\Omega}+V-\mu)
and a labeling of the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
H_{t}
, for
t
near
1
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{\lambda}_{j}(1)&=\left\langle\frac{{\rm d}(t^{2}V(tx))}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=1}u_{j},u_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&=2\langle Vu_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\langle(\nabla V\cdot x)u_{j},u_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)},1\leq j\leq m.\end{split}

(5.59)

By the same rescaling as above, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}(t)
uniquely determine the eigenvalues
\mu_{j}(t)
for
t
near
1
, and one has
\lambda_{j}(t)=t^{2}\mu_{j}(t)
. Our next objective is to use this identity together with (
5.59
) to derive (
5.58
).

We pause to consider the case of the Laplace operator with no potential. If
V\equiv 0
then the proof is essentially completed as
H_{t}
does not depend on
t
and
0=\dot{\lambda}_{j}(1)=2\mu_{j}(1)+\dot{\mu}_{j}(1)
. This yields (
5.58
) by the celebrated Rellich formula
[
111
]
expressing the eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}(1)=\mu_{j}(1)
of the Dirichlet Laplacian via the Neumann boundary values of the respective eigenfunctions (this formula in turn easily follows from the Pokhozaev–Rellich identity, see, e.g.,
[
12
, p.201]
,
[
85
, p.237]
, and formula (
5.62
) below).

Returning to the general case of nonzero potential, to derive (
5.58
) from (
5.59
) we will follow the strategy of
[
44
, Lemma 5.5]
. Let us fix
j
and denote, for brevity,
u:=u_{j}
and
\lambda(t):=\lambda_{j}(t)
,
\mu(t)=\mu_{j}(t)
. First, integration by parts for
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
yields

\langle(\nabla V\cdot x)u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=-\langle Vu,2(\nabla u\cdot x)+nu\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\text{ and }\langle u,\nabla u\cdot x\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=-{n}/{2}.

(5.60)

Using
-\Delta u+Vu=\lambda(1)u
and replacing
Vu
by
\Delta u+\lambda(1)u
in (
5.59
) and (
5.60
),
a short calculation gives

\dot{\mu}(1)=\dot{\lambda}(1)-2\lambda(1)=(2-n)\langle\Delta u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-2\langle\Delta u,\nabla u\cdot x\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(5.61)

The standard Rellich’s identity, see, e.g.,
[
12
, p.201]
, yields

\displaystyle\langle\Delta u,\nabla u\cdot x\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega}\big((\nu\cdot\nabla u)(x\cdot\nabla u)-\frac{1}{2}(x\cdot\nu)\|\nabla u\|^{2}\big){\rm d}x

(5.62)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\frac{n-2}{2}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u\|^{2}{\rm d}x.

Since
u
satisfies the Dirichlet condition,
\partial\Omega
is a level curve, and thus
\nabla u
and
\nu
are parallel, that is,
\nabla u=(\nu\cdot\nabla u)\nu
. Using all this in (
5.61
) yields (
5.58
) because

\dot{\mu}(1)=\int_{\partial\Omega}\big(-2(\nu\cdot\nabla u)(x\cdot\nabla u)+(x\cdot\nu)\|\nabla u\|^{2}\big){\rm d}x=-\int_{\partial\Omega}(\nu\cdot\nabla u)^{2}(\nu\cdot x){\rm d}x.

5.5.
Maslov crossing form for elliptic operators

In this section we continue the discussion began in Section
4.5
on the relation between the Maslov crossing form and the slopes of the eigenvalue curves bifurcating from a multiple eigenvalue of the unperturbed elliptic operator. Here,
we assume the setting of Theorem
5.2
and obtain a version of formula (
4.116
) for the Robin-type elliptic operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
, see Proposition
5.8
below.
For
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
we let

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{{\mathcal{K}}}_{\lambda}:=\mathrm{T}\Big(\Big\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega):\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\langle\mathtt{a}_{jk}\partial_{k}u,\partial_{j}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle\mathtt{a}_{j}\partial_{j}u,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle u,\mathtt{a}_{j}\partial_{j}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\langle vu-\lambda u,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=0,\ \varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\Big\}\Big),\end{split}

where the trace operator
\mathrm{T}=[\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}]^{\top}
is as in (
5.12
). This is a “weak” version of the set
{\mathbb{K}}_{\lambda,t}
from Section
4.5
. The mapping
\lambda\mapsto{\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda}
is in
C^{1}({\mathbb{R}},\Lambda(H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)))
by
[
45
, Proposition 3.5]
.

Let
t\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\{(f,-\Theta_{t}f):f\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\}
, then for
t_{0}\in[0,1]
there is an interval
{\mathcal{I}}\subset[0,1]
centered at
t_{0}
and a family of operators
t\mapsto{\mathcal{M}}_{t},t\in{\mathcal{I}}
, which is in
C^{1}\big({\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}},{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{\bot})\big)
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{0}}=0
and

{\mathcal{F}}_{t}=\big\{{\mathbf{q}}+{\mathcal{M}}_{t}{\mathbf{q}}\ \big|\,{\mathbf{q}}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}\big\},t\in{\mathcal{I}},

(5.63)

see, e.g.,
[
44
, Lemma 3.8]
. In other words,
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
can be written locally as the graph of the operator
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
, which is a replacement of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}
from Section
4.5
. We say that
(\lambda,t_{0})
is a conjugate point if
{\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}\not=\{0\}
or, equivalently, if
\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}-\lambda)\not=\{0\}
.

We recall
\lambda(t_{0})\in\operatorname{Spec}_{\rm disc}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}})
from Theorem
5.2
and let
\lambda:=\lambda(t_{0})
. Then
(\lambda,t_{0})
is a conjugate point at which the Maslov crossing form
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
for the path
t\mapsto{\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
relative to the diagonal subspace
\mathfrak{D}=\{{\mathbf{p}}=(p,p):p\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))\}

is defined by the formula

\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}({\mathbf{q}},{\mathbf{p}}):=\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}\big|_{t=t_{0}}\widehat{\omega}({\mathbf{q}},{\mathcal{M}}_{t}{\mathbf{p}})=\widehat{\omega}({\mathbf{q}},\dot{{\mathcal{M}}}_{t_{0}}{\mathbf{p}}),\ {\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}}\in({\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}})\cap\mathfrak{D},

(5.64)

where
\widehat{\omega}={\omega}\oplus(-{\omega})
and

\displaystyle{\dot{{\mathcal{M}}}_{t_{0}}=\frac{\hbox{\rm d}}{\hbox{\rm d}t}{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\big|_{t=t_{0}}}
. We stress that the pair of Lagrangian subspaces
\big({\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda},{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}\big)
is Fredholm since
\lambda=\lambda(t_{0})\not\in\operatorname{Spec}_{\rm{ess}}({\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}})
, see
[
92
, Theorem 3.2]
. Hence,
\dim\big(({\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}})\cap\mathfrak{D}\big)<\infty
and
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
is a finite dimensional bilinear form. In fact,
the pair of Lagrangian subspaces
\big({\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda},{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\big)
is Fredholm for
t
near
t_{0}
due to continuity of the path of the resolvent operators
t\mapsto({\mathcal{L}}_{t}-{\bf i})^{-1}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
\lambda(t_{0})
,
\{\lambda_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{m}
and
\{u_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
be as in Theorem
5.2
, and denote
{\mathbf{q}}_{j}:=(\mathrm{T}u_{j},\mathrm{T}u_{j})
. Then
{\mathbf{q}}_{j}\in({\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda(t_{0})}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}})\cap\mathfrak{D}
and

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}({\mathbf{q}}_{j},{\mathbf{q}}_{j}),\ 1\leq j\leq m,

(5.65)

where
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
is the Maslov crossing form introduced in (
5.64
).

Proof.

The inclusion
{\mathbf{q}}_{j}\in({\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda(t_{0})}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}})\cap\mathfrak{D}
holds since
u_{j}
is an eigenfunction of
{\mathcal{L}}_{t_{0}}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda(t_{0})
. For a fixed
j

we abbreviate
{\mathbf{q}}:={\mathbf{q}}_{j}=\mathrm{T}u_{j}
and introduce
g_{t}\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)\times H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
as in (
4.114
)
but with
{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,t}
replaced by
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
. In particular,
g_{t_{0}}=\mathrm{T}u_{j}
because
{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{0}}=0
.
Since
g_{t}=Q_{t}g_{t}
where
Q_{t}
is the orthogonal projection onto
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
, we have

\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}}=\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}g_{t_{0}}+Q_{{t_{0}}}\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}}=\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}u_{j}+Q_{{t_{0}}}\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}}.

This and that
\text{\rm{ran}}(Q_{{t_{0}}})
is Lagrangian yields, as in (
4.122
),

\omega(\mathrm{T}u_{j},\,\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}})=\omega(\mathrm{T}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}_{t_{0}}u_{j}).

(5.66)

As in (
4.123
), by definition of
\mathfrak{m}_{t_{0}}
this implies

\mathfrak{m}_{{t_{0}}}({\mathbf{q}}_{j},{\mathbf{q}}_{j})=-\omega(\mathrm{T}u_{j},\,\dot{g}_{{t_{0}}})=-\omega(\mathrm{T}u_{j},\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}u_{j})=\omega(\dot{Q}_{{t_{0}}}\mathrm{T}u_{j},\mathrm{T}u_{j}).

(5.67)

By formula (
5.20
) in Theorem
5.2
we have

\dot{\lambda}_{j}(t_{0})=-\langle\dot{\Theta}_{t_{0}}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{j},\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}
. Thus, it remains to show that

\omega(\dot{Q}_{t_{0}}\mathrm{T}u_{j},\mathrm{T}u_{j})=-\langle\dot{\Theta}_{t_{0}}\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{j},\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}.

The latter assertion follows from (
3.86
) with
\phi_{j}=-\gamma_{{}_{D}}u_{j}
and
X_{t}=\Theta_{t}
,
Y_{t}=I
as

{\mathcal{F}}_{t}={\text{graph}}(-\Theta_{t})=\operatorname{ker}([X_{t},Y_{t}])

with this choice of
X_{t}
and
Y_{t}
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

As discussed in Remark
4.21
, formula (
5.65
)
relating the derivative of the eigenvalues of the elliptic operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
with respect to the parameter
t
and the value of the (Maslov) crossing form for the flow
t\mapsto{\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda(t)}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
of Lagrangian planes could be viewed as an infinitesimal version of the fundamental relation between the spectral flow and the Maslov index. Indeed, as in Remark
4.21
, formula (
5.65
) implies relation (
4.126
) with
H_{t}
replaced by
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}
and
\Upsilon_{\lambda,t}
replaced by
{\mathcal{K}}_{\lambda(t)}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
.
\Diamond

Example 5.10
.

We will now briefly return to the Robin eigenvalue problem (
5.43
) related to the Friedlander Inequalities but at once for the general elliptic operator
{\mathcal{L}}
described in Subsection
5.1
.
We recall that for
\lambda\notin\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{D})
the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
M_{D,N}(\lambda)
is defined by
f\mapsto-\gamma_{{}_{N}}u
(in the relevant papers
[
46
,
62
]
,
M_{D,N}
is defined by
f\mapsto\gamma_{{}_{N}}u
) where
u
is the solution to
{\mathcal{L}}u=\lambda u
,
\gamma_{{}_{D}}u=f
. It is easy to see that (
5.43
) has a nontrivial solution if and only if
\mu=\cot(\frac{\pi}{2}t)
is an eigenvalue of
M_{DN}(\lambda)
. Combining Remarks
4.21
,
5.9

and Example
5.5
with Proposition
5.8
can be used to show the following formula relating the spectral counting functions
of the Dirichlet and Neumann realizations
{\mathcal{L}}_{D}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{N}
and the Dirichlet-to-Neuman map
M_{D,N}(0)
,

\begin{split}\#\{\lambda\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{N}):\lambda<0\}&-\#\{\lambda\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{L}}_{D}):\lambda<0\}\\
&=\#\{\mu\in\operatorname{Spec}(M_{D,N}(0)):\mu\geq 0\},\end{split}

(5.68)

see
[
62
]
and,
specifically,
[
46
, Theorem 3]
and the literature therein (in
[
46
,
62
]
the RHS of (
5.68
) is given by the number of
negative
eigenvalues of
M_{D,N}(\lambda)
, this is due to sign discrepancy in the definition of
M_{D,N}(\lambda)
).
We omit details and just mention that the monotonicity of the eigenvalue curves
\lambda_{k}(t)
,
k=1,2,\dots
, established in Example
5.5
and formula (
5.65
) show that the Maslov crossing form is sign definite at each conjugate point on the vertical line through
\lambda

when
t
changes from
0
to
1
(Figure
1
serves as a schematic illustration of this assertion). By a standard calculation, see, e.g., Step 1 in the proof of
[
92
, Theorem 3.3]
, the Maslov crossing form is also sign definite at each conjugate point on the horizontal lines through
t=0
and
t=1
when
\lambda
is changing.
These two properties are sometimes referred to as the monotonicity of the Maslov index. Thus, cf. Remark
4.21
, the spectral flow through zero given by the LHS of (
5.68
) is equal to the Maslov index along the vertical line through
\lambda
which, in turn, is equal to the RHS.

\Diamond

6.
Symplectic resolvent difference formulas for dual pairs

In this section we give a generalization of the resolvent difference formula (
2.21
) to the case of boundary triplets for an adjoint pair
A,\widetilde{A}
, see, e.g.,
[
1
,
30
,
32
]
and the literature cited therein. The theory of adjoint pairs goes back to
[
98
,
123
]
, see also
[
7
,
16
,
31
,
101
]
. It allows one to describe non-selfadjoint extensions for an adjoint pair of densely defined closed (but not neceserely symmetric) operators. A typical example of the adjoint pair, see, e.g.,
[
30
,
32
]
, is furnished by a non-symmetric elliptic second order partial differential operator and its formal adjoint; this example is discussed in detail in the end of this section.

We follow
[
32
]
to recall the definition of the adjoint pair and its boundary triplet. Let
A,\widetilde{A}
be closed densely defined operators on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
forming an adjoint pair, that is, we assume that
\widetilde{A}\subseteq A^{*}
and
A\subseteq(\widetilde{A})^{*}
. We denote by
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
, respectively,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}
the domain
\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})
, respectively,
\operatorname{dom}((\widetilde{A})^{*})
equipped with the graph-scalar product and graph norm for
A^{*}
, respectively,
(\widetilde{A})^{*}
, cf. Section
2
. Let
\mathfrak{H}
and
\mathfrak{K}
be some “boundary” Hilbert spaces and

\Gamma_{0}:\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}\to\mathfrak{H},\quad\Gamma_{1}:\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}\to\mathfrak{K},\quad\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}:{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}\to\mathfrak{K},\quad\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}:{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}\to\mathfrak{H}

(6.1)

be some “boundary trace operators”. The collection
\{\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{K},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}\}
is called a

boundary triplet for the adjoint pair
A,\widetilde{A}
when the following hypothesis is satisfied.

Hypothesis 6.1
.

Suppose that
A,\widetilde{A}
is an adjoint pair of densely defined closed operators such that
\widetilde{A}\subseteq A^{*}
and
A\subseteq(\widetilde{A})^{*}
. Consider linear operators, called the trace operators,

\mathrm{T}:=[\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}]^{\top}:\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}\to\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{K},\,\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}:=[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}]^{\top}:{\mathcal{H}}_{+}\to\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{H}.\,

(6.2)

Assume that the operators
\mathrm{T}
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}
are surjective and satisfy

\langle(\widetilde{A})^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\Gamma_{1}u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{K}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}},

(6.3)

for all
u\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}
and
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
.

The existence of a boundary triplet for every adjoint pair
A,\widetilde{A}
was proved in
[
98
]
, where, in addition, it was shown that

\operatorname{dom}(A)=\operatorname{dom}((\widetilde{A})^{*})\cap\operatorname{ker}\Gamma_{0}\cap\operatorname{ker}\Gamma_{1},\,\operatorname{dom}(\widetilde{A})=\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})\cap\operatorname{ker}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}\cap\operatorname{ker}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}.

(6.4)

It is well-known that the operators
T,\widetilde{T}
are bounded, cf.
[
101
]
,
[
119
, Lemma 14.13]
.

The following resolvent difference formula is a direct generalization of Theorem
2.5
. It gives the difference of the resolvent operators of any two (not necessarily sel-adjoint) extensions of the operator
A
that are parts of
(\widetilde{A})^{*}
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let

\{\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{K},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}\}
be a boundary triplet for an adjoint pair
A,\widetilde{A}
, and let
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
for
j=1,2
be any two closed extensions of
A
acting in
{\mathcal{H}}
and satisfying
A\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{j}\subseteq(\widetilde{A})^{*}
. Suppose that
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})\cup\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{2}))
and denote
R_{j}(\zeta):=({\mathcal{A}}_{j}-\zeta)^{-1}
for
j=1,2
. Then one has

\displaystyle R_{2}(\zeta)-R_{1}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Gamma_{1}R_{1}(\zeta)-\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta),

(6.5)

\displaystyle R_{2}(\zeta)-R_{1}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}Q_{2}JQ_{1}\big(\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta)\big),

(6.6)

where
R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)=(({\mathcal{A}}_{2})^{*}-\overline{\zeta})^{-1}
,
the operator
\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)=\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta),\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)
is considered as an operator in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}},\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{H})
and the adjoint operators in (
6.5
), (
6.6
) are defined correspondingly,
Q_{1}
, respectively,
Q_{2}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto
\overline{\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1}))}
in the space
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{K}
, respectively, onto
\overline{\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}(\operatorname{dom}(({\mathcal{A}}_{2})^{*}))}
in the space
\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{H}
, and the operator
J
maps a pair
(f,g)
from
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{K}
into the pair

(g,-f)
from
\mathfrak{K}\times\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
A\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{j}\subseteq(\widetilde{A})^{*}
yields

\widetilde{A}\subseteq({\mathcal{A}}_{j})^{*}\subseteq A^{*}
for
j=1,2

[
83
, Section III.5.5]
. The operator
R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is also bounded from
{\mathcal{H}}
onto
\operatorname{dom}(({\mathcal{A}}_{2})^{*})\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}_{+}=\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})
, cf. (
2.10
). Thus, the operator
\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)
is well defined, and, analogously, the operator

\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta)
is well defined. Moreover, for all
u,v\in{\mathcal{H}}
one has

(A^{*}-\overline{\zeta})R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v=({\mathcal{A}}_{2}-\zeta)^{*}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v=v,\,\big((\widetilde{A})^{*}-\zeta\big)R_{1}(\zeta)u=({\mathcal{A}}_{1}-\zeta)R_{1}(\zeta)u=u.

(6.7)

We also have
Q_{2}\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)=\widetilde{\mathrm{T}}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)
and
Q_{1}\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta)=\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta)
by the definition of the orthogonal projections
Q_{2}
and
Q_{1}
. Thus, (
6.6
) is just a reformulation of (
6.5
). For the proof of (
6.5
), we use (
6.3
) and (
6.7
) to write

\displaystyle\langle\big(R_{2}(\zeta)

\displaystyle-R_{1}(\zeta)\big)u,v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}=\langle R_{2}(\zeta)u-R_{1}(\zeta)u,({\mathcal{A}}_{2}-\overline{\zeta})^{*}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}

\displaystyle=\langle({\mathcal{A}}_{2}-\zeta)R_{2}(\zeta)u,R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\langle R_{1}(\zeta)u,(A^{*}-\overline{\zeta})R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}

\displaystyle=\langle u,R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}-\langle\big((\widetilde{A})^{*}-\zeta\big)R_{1}(\zeta)u,R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\langle\Gamma_{1}R_{1}(\zeta)u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v\rangle_{\mathfrak{K}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=\langle\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Gamma_{1}R_{1}(\zeta)u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

for all
u,v\in{\mathcal{H}}
, yielding (
6.5
).
∎

In particular, for
j=1,2
, given an operator

\Psi_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{K})
(not necessarily self-adjoint), we consider in
{\mathcal{H}}
the extension
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
of
A
satisfying
A\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{j}\subseteq(\widetilde{A})^{*}
and defined by the formulas

{\mathcal{A}}_{j}u=(\widetilde{A})^{*}u\,\text{ for }\,u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{j}):=\{u\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}:\Gamma_{1}u=\Psi_{j}\Gamma_{0}u\},\quad j=1,2.

Corollary 6.3
.

Under assumptions in Theorem
6.2
one has

R_{2}(\zeta)-R_{1}(\zeta)=\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\big(\Psi_{1}-\Psi_{2}\big)\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta).

(6.8)

Proof.

We claim that
\widetilde{A}\subset({\mathcal{A}}_{j})^{*}\subset A^{*}
satisfies

\operatorname{dom}(({\mathcal{A}}_{j})^{*})=\{v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{+}:\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}v=(\Psi_{j})^{*}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}v\}
. Indeed, to see the inclusion “
\subseteq
” we note that for
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{j})
and
v\in\operatorname{dom}(({\mathcal{A}}_{j})^{*})
by (
6.3
)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{j}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,{\mathcal{A}}_{j}^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle(\widetilde{A})^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\langle\Gamma_{1}u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{K}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}=\langle\Psi_{j}\Gamma_{0}u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{K}}-\langle\Gamma_{0}u,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}v\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}

yielding
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}v=(\Psi_{j})^{*}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}v
as
\Gamma_{0}
is surjective, while the opposite inclusion follows by running this chain of equalities backwards, thus proving the claim. Next, we note that
\Gamma_{1}R_{1}(\zeta)=\Psi_{1}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)
and

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)=(\Psi_{2})^{*}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)
since the resolvents map
{\mathcal{H}}
into the domains of respective operators.
Now (
6.5
) yelds

\displaystyle R_{2}(\zeta)-R_{1}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Gamma_{1}R_{1}(\zeta)-\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Psi_{1}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)-\big((\Psi_{2})^{*}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}R_{2}^{*}(\zeta)\big)^{*}\big(\Psi_{1}-\Psi_{2}\big)\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta),

as required.
∎

We conclude this section with an example of the abstract boundary triplet for the adjoint pair formed by an elliptic second order partial differential operator and its formal adjoint, cf.
[
13
,
14
,
18
,
30
,
32
]
. In particular, resolvent difference formulas in Theorem
6.2
and Corollary
6.3
hold for these operators. The discussion below regarding the boundary traces is based on the paper
[
73
]
, see also
[
32
]
, dealing with general elliptic operators on smooth domains and is related to the material in Appendix
C
taken from

[
67
,
69
]
where the case of the Laplacian on Lipschitz domains was considered.

We assume throughout that
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a bounded domain with
C^{\infty}
-boundary, and consider the following
mutually formally adjoint differential expressions, cf. (
5.1
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}:&=-\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\partial_{j}\mathtt{a}_{jk}\partial_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(\mathtt{a}_{j}\partial_{j}-\partial_{j}{\mathtt{b}_{j}}\big)+\mathtt{q}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt=-\operatorname{div}(\mathtt{A}\nabla)+\mathtt{a}\cdot\nabla-\nabla\cdot\mathtt{b}+\mathtt{q},\\
\end{split}

(6.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}:&=-\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\partial_{k}\overline{\mathtt{a}}_{jk}\partial_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(\overline{\mathtt{b}}_{j}\partial_{j}-\partial_{j}\overline{\mathtt{a}}_{j}\big)+\overline{\mathtt{q}}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt=-\operatorname{div}(\mathtt{A}^{*}\nabla)+\mathtt{b}^{*}\cdot\nabla-\nabla\cdot\mathtt{a}^{*}+\overline{\mathtt{q}},\end{split}

(6.10)

where the bar stands for complex conjugation, with coefficients
\mathtt{A}=\{\mathtt{a}_{ij}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
,
\mathtt{a}:=\{\mathtt{a}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
,

\mathtt{b}:=\{\mathtt{b}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
and
\mathtt{q}
satisfying

\mathtt{a}_{jk},\mathtt{a}_{j},\mathtt{b}_{j},\mathtt{q}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega};{\mathbb{C}})
,
1\leq j,k\leq n
. In addition, we assume the ellipticity condition: For
c>0
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\Big(\sum\limits_{j,k=1}^{n}\mathtt{a}_{jk}(x)\xi_{k}\overline{\xi_{j}}\Big)\geq c\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}|\xi_{j}|^{2},x\in\overline{\Omega},\xi=\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}.

(6.11)

As in (
5.4
), we associate with
{\mathcal{L}}
from
(
6.9
) the space
{\mathcal{D}}^{s}(\Omega)
equipped with the
{\mathcal{L}}
-graph norm (
5.5
), and with
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}
from
(
6.10
)
the space

\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{s}(\Omega):=\{u\in H^{s}(\Omega):\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}

equipped with the
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}
-graph norm

\|u\|_{\widetilde{s}}:=\big(\|u\|_{H^{s}({\Omega})}^{2}+\|\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}u\|_{L^{2}({\Omega})}^{2}\big)^{1/2}
,
s\geq 0
,
where
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}u
should be understood in the sense of distributions.
We introduce operators acting in
L^{2}(\Omega)
by

{\mathcal{L}}_{0}u={\mathcal{L}}u
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}u
for
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{0})=\operatorname{dom}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}):=C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
, the space of functions compactly supported in
\Omega
, and recall from
[
119
, Section 11.3]
that these operators are closable. We will denote by
{\mathcal{L}}_{min}
the closure of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
and by
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{min}
the closure of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}
.
Also, we will consider the maximal operators,
{\mathcal{L}}_{max}u:={\mathcal{L}}u
for
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{L}}_{max}):={\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{max}u:=\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}u
for
u\in\operatorname{dom}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{max}):=\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega)
. It is known, see, e.g.,

[
119
, Proposition 1.14]
, that

{\mathcal{L}}_{min}\subset(\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{min})^{*}={\mathcal{L}}_{max},\quad\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{min}\subset({\mathcal{L}}_{min})^{*}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{max}.

(6.12)

Thus,
A={\mathcal{L}}_{min}
and
\widetilde{A}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{min}
is an adjoint pair of operators. We will now construct the boundary traces so that the Green identity (
6.3
) holds.

It will be convenient to use the notation

\gamma_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}=\gamma_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}:=\gamma_{{}_{D}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{1}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))

for the usual Dirichlet trace. As in Subsection
5.1
, we will associate with
{\mathcal{L}}
from (
6.9
) the first order trace operator
\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega))

which is a unique extension of the co-normal derivative
\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{2}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\Omega))
defined in (
5.8
). Analogously, associated with
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}
from (
6.10
) is the first order trace operator
\gamma_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega))

which is a unique extension of the co-normal derivative
\gamma_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{2}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\Omega))
defined by the formula

\displaystyle{\gamma_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}}u:=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\overline{\mathtt{a}}_{kj}\nu_{j}\gamma_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}(\partial_{k}u)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\overline{\mathtt{b}}_{j}}\nu_{j}\gamma_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u,\quad u\in H^{2}(\Omega).

(6.13)

Then the following Green identity,

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{L}}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle-\langle u,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}v\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

(6.14)

\displaystyle={\langle\gamma_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}u,\gamma_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}v\rangle_{{-1/2}}}-\overline{\langle\gamma_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}v,\gamma_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u\rangle_{{-1/2}}},

holds for all
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
and
v\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{1}(\Omega)
. In order to rewrite this identity in a form compatible with (
6.3
) we will need to take four more steps.

First, we will extend further the usual Dirichlet and weak Neumann trace operators

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}=\gamma_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{1}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)),

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)),\,\gamma_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{1}(\Omega),H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega))

defined on the spaces
H^{1}(\Omega)
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{1}(\Omega)
,
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{1}(\Omega)
, respectively, to
{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega)
, the domains of the maximal operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{max}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{max}
, and obtain the bounded and surjective traces

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega),H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)),\,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega),H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)),

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega),H^{-3/2}(\partial\Omega)),\,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega),H^{-3/2}(\partial\Omega)),

cf. Lemmas
C.3
and
C.4
,
that agree with the usual Dirichlet and Neumann trace operators on their respective domains. The existence of such extensions for smooth domains and general elliptic operators is given in
[
73
, Sections II.1, III.1]
, and for Lipschitz domains and the Laplacian is given in
[
69
]
.

Armed with the trace operators defined on the domains
{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega)
of the maximal operators, we proceed, following
[
73
, Section III.1]
, with the second step that involves the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators,
M_{D,N}
and
\widetilde{M}_{D,N}
, associated with the operators
{\mathcal{L}}
from (
6.9
) and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}
from (
6.10
), respectively. We define
M_{D,N}
next; one deals with
\widetilde{M}_{D,N}
analogously. By adding to
\mathtt{q}
a constant, if needed, we may and will assume that zero is not in the spectrum of the Dirichlet realizations of
{\mathcal{L}}
as defined in
[
73
, Section I]
or
[
119
, Section 11.3]
. Thus, for each
f\in H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)
there is a unique solution
u=u_{f}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
to the
boundary value problem
{\mathcal{L}}u=0
,
\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}u=f
. We may now define
M_{D,N}f:=-\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u_{f}
as an operator acting from
H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)
to
H^{-3/2}(\partial\Omega)
.

Our third step is to introduce yet another Neumann trace,

\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}
, defined by the formula

\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u:=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u+M_{D,N}\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}u
for
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
. The following remarkable property of
\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}
is a consequence of elliptic regularity of solutions to the Dirichlet problem, see
[
73
, Theorem III.1.2]
and
[
69
, Theorem 12.1]
(or Lemma
C.5
below): Although both distributions
\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u
and
M_{D,N}\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}u
belong to
H^{-3/2}(\partial\Omega)
, we claim that the sum
\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u
of these two distributions is, in fact, a function from
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
. Indeed, given a
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
and letting
f=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}u
we observe that

\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}(u-u_{f})
with
u_{f}
as in the definition of
M_{D,N}
in step two above. But then

\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}(u-u_{f})=0
, which shows that
u-u_{f}
is in the domain of the Dririclet realization of
{\mathcal{L}}
. By elliptic regularity then

u-u_{f}\in H^{2}(\Omega)
, see, e.g.,
[
73
, Theorem I.3.1]
, and thus
\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}u=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}(u-u_{f})={\gamma}_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}(u-u_{f})\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
as claimed. Analogously, we define

\tau_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u:=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u+\widetilde{M}_{D,N}\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u
for
u\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega)
.
Moreover, the operators
\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))
and
\tau_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}\in{\mathcal{B}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega),H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega))
are surjective, and the following Green identity,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}u,v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle-(u,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

(6.15)

\displaystyle=\overline{\langle\tau_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}v,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{{D,{\mathcal{L}}}}}u\rangle_{{-1/2}}}-{{\langle\tau_{{}_{N,{{\mathcal{L}}}}}u,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}v\rangle_{{-1/2}}}},

holds for all
u\in{\mathcal{D}}^{0}(\Omega)
,
v\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega)
by
[
73
, Theorem III.1.2]
, see also
[
32
, Theorem 7.4]
and
[
69
, Theorem 12.1]
or Lemma
C.5
below.

Finally, in the last step we use the Riesz isomorphism
\Phi:H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
defined in (
4.31
). We are ready to define the boundary triplet in question: the function spaces are given by

{\mathcal{H}}_{+}=\operatorname{dom}(({\mathcal{L}}_{min})^{*})=\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega),\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{+}=\operatorname{dom}((\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{min})^{*})={{\mathcal{D}}}^{0}(\Omega),\mathfrak{H}=\mathfrak{K}=H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)

(6.16)

and the trace operators are given by

\Gamma_{0}=-\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,{\mathcal{L}}}},\Gamma_{1}=\tau_{{}_{N,{\mathcal{L}}}},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{0}=-\Phi\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}},\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}=\tau_{{}_{N,\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}}}.

(6.17)

Now (
6.15
) shows that this collection is indeed a boundary triplet for
A={\mathcal{L}}_{min}
and
\widetilde{A}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{min}
as (
6.3
) readily holds.

Appendix A
Lagrangian planes and self-adjoint extensions

In this appendix we elaborate on the assumption of the second part of Theorem
2.5
– that the image of the domain of a self-adjoint extension is a Lagrangian plane. It is well known that self-adjoint extensions of
A
can be parameterized by Lagrangian planes,
see, e.g.,
[
71
, Theorem 3.1.6]
,
[
77
]
,
[
105
]
,
[
119
, Proposition 14.7]
. Such parameterization depends on the choice of the trace operator
\mathrm{T}
and the “boundary” space
\mathfrak{H}
, see, e.g.,
[
14
, Proposition 2.4]
and
[
71
, Chapter 3]
. Theorems
A.1
and
A.2
and Corollary
A.5

below give yet another variant of the parameterization.

To formulate these results we will need some elementary preliminaries.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a subspace in
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
and
\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}):=\{u\in{\mathcal{D}}:\mathrm{T}u\in{\mathcal{F}}\}
denote the preimage of
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Consider the linear operator
{\mathcal{A}}:=A^{*}\big|_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
acting in
{\mathcal{H}}
and given by

{\mathcal{A}}u:=A^{*}u,\ u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}):={\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}.

(A.1)

Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is a part of the closed operator
A^{*}
, the operator
{\mathcal{A}}
is closable. We denote by
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
the closure of
{\mathcal{A}}
, that is, we let

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})=\big\{u\in{\mathcal{H}}:\exists\,\{u_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\subset\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\text{ such that
$u_{n}\to u$ in ${\mathcal{H}}$ }

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\text{ and $\{{\mathcal{A}}u_{n}\}$ converges to some $w\in{\mathcal{H}}$}\big\}

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}u:=w,u\in\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}).

In particular, we have

\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}=A^{*}\big|_{\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})}\text{ where
$\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})=\overline{{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}=\overline{\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{+}}$}.

(A.2)

Using the general definition of the adjoint operator we record the following,

\begin{split}\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}&=\{u\in{\mathcal{H}}:\exists\,w\in{\mathcal{H}}\text{ such that
$\langle w,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,{\mathcal{A}}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0$
for all $v\in\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{A}}$\lx@text@rbrace},\\
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}u&:=w\text{ for $u\in\operatorname{dom}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}$}.\end{split}

(A.3)

Since
\operatorname{dom}(A)\subset\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
, due to
\operatorname{dom}(A)=\operatorname{ker}\mathrm{T}\subset\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})
, from Proposition
2.2
(1), taking
v\in\operatorname{dom}(A)
in (
A.3
) shows that
u\in\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})
and
w=A^{*}u={\mathcal{A}}^{*}u
. Thus, using

{\mathcal{A}}=A^{*}\big|_{\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})}=A^{*}\big|_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
, we get

{\mathcal{A}}^{*}=A^{*}\big|_{\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})},\,\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})=\{u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{+}:\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0\,\forall\,v\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})\}.

(A.4)

We are ready to present a result saying that pre-images
of Lagrangian planes in
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
under the trace map give domains of self-adjoint extensions of
A
.

Theorem A.1
.

Assume Hypothesis
2.1
and that

{\mathcal{F}}\in\Lambda(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
is a Lagrangian subspace in
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
such that

\displaystyle\text{${\mathcal{F}}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})=\mathrm{T}\big(\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})\big)$ is $(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})$-dense in ${\mathcal{F}}$}.

(A.5)

Then the operator
{\mathcal{A}}=A^{*}\big|_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
defined in (
A.1
) is essentially self-adjoint, that is,
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{*}
, if and only if

\displaystyle\text{$\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})\cap{\mathcal{D}}$ is $({\mathcal{H}}_{+})$-dense in $\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})$}.

(A.6)

Proof.

Assume (
A.6
) .
We derive
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{*}
in three steps. First,
we show
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
. If
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})=\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})
then for any
v\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
the Green identity (
2.12
) gives

\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\omega(\mathrm{T}u,\mathrm{T}v)=0

(A.7)

because both
\mathrm{T}u
and
\mathrm{T}v
are in
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
as
{\mathcal{F}}
is isotropic by the assumption. Now (
A.4
) and (
A.7
) yield
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
as required. Second, we show that
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})\cap{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
. If
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
then for any
v\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})
we have

\omega(\mathrm{T}u,\mathrm{T}v)=\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0

(A.8)

because
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
and
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
, see (
A.4
). We now claim that
\omega(\mathrm{T}u,g)=0
for any
g\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Indeed, we use (
A.5
) to approximate
g\in{\mathcal{F}}
by a sequence
g_{n}\in{\mathcal{F}}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})
. For each
n
choose
v\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})
such that
g_{n}=\mathrm{T}v
. By (
A.8
) then
\omega(\mathrm{T}u,g)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\omega(\mathrm{T}u,g_{n})=0
, thus proving the claim. Therefore,
\mathrm{T}u\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}
as
{\mathcal{F}}
is maximally isotropic by the assumption, and then
u\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
as required.
Third, taking
({\mathcal{H}}_{+})
-closures in the inclusions

\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})\cap{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})

just proved and using (
A.6
) yield
\overline{\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})}=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
and therefore
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{*}
, see (
A.2
).

Conversely, assume that
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{*}
. To show (
A.6
) we need to prove that
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
is dense in
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})
. By (
A.2
) we know that
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})=\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
and thus it remains to show that
\overline{\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}\cap{\mathcal{D}}}=\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
. The inclusion “
\subseteq
” follows from
\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}\cap{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
. To prove “
\supseteq
”, we take
u\in\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
and a sequence
u_{n}\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})
approximating
u
. Since
\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}
we have
u_{n}\in\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}\cap{\mathcal{D}}
and thus
u\in\overline{\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}\cap{\mathcal{D}}}
as required.
∎

Next, we present a result saying that the traces of the domains of self-adjoint extensions of
A
form Lagrangian planes in
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
.

Theorem A.2
.

Assume Hypothesis
2.1
and that there exists
a self-adjoint restriction
{\mathcal{A}}
of
A^{*}
on a subspace
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
such that

\text{$\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}$ is $({\mathcal{H}}_{+})$-dense in $\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})$}.

(A.9)

Then the
(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
-closure of the subspace
{\mathcal{F}}
defined by
{\mathcal{F}}:=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})
is Lagrangian, that is,

\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}={\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
, if and only if

\displaystyle\text{${\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})$ is $(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})$-dense in ${\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}$}.

(A.10)

Proof.

Assume (
A.10
). We derive
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}={\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
in three steps. First, we show that
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
. If
f\in{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})
then
f=\mathrm{T}u
for some
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
. Since
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}^{*}
by the assumption, we conclude that
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
. Pick any
g\in{\mathcal{F}}
and let
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
be such that
g=\mathrm{T}v
. Then

\omega(f,g)=\omega(\mathrm{T}u,\mathrm{T}v)=\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0

(A.11)

because
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
and
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
, see (
A.4
). But (
A.11
) yields
f\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
, as required. Second, we show that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}
. If
f\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})
then
f=\mathrm{T}u
for some
u\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
\omega(f,g)=0
for all
g\in{\mathcal{F}}
. In particular, if
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
and
g=\mathrm{T}v\in{\mathcal{F}}

then

\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\omega(\mathrm{T}u,\mathrm{T}v)=0.

(A.12)

Due to
(
A.9
) we conclude from (
A.12
) that
\langle A^{*}u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u,A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0
for all
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
. Thus
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
by (
A.4
).
Since
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}
by the assumption, we have
u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
and so
f=\mathrm{T}u\in\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})={\mathcal{F}}
as required. Third, taking
(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
-closures in the inclusions

{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}

just proved and using (
A.10
) yield
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}={\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
.

Conversely, assume that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}={\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
. To show (
A.10
) we need to prove that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})
is dense in
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
, that is, that

\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})}=\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
.
Since
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})
, this follows analogously to the last part of the proof of Theorem
A.1
.
∎

We note that conditions (
A.5
), (
A.6
), (
A.9
), (
A.10
) automatically hold for all classes of PDE, ODE and quantum graphs operators and all examples that we know; these conditions trivially hold provided
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{H}}_{+}
and
\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})=\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
, that is, when
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is an abstract boundary triplet, see Section
4
.
We recall Remark
2.4
regarding the existence of self-adjoint extensions of
A
under Hypothesis
2.1
.

Remark A.3
.

The density assumptions
\overline{\operatorname{dom}(A)}={\mathcal{H}},\overline{\text{\rm{ran}}(\mathrm{T})}=\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
introduced in Hypothesis
2.1
are absolutely critical for Theorems
A.1
and
A.2
to hold. Indeed,
[
53
, Example 6.6]
gives a scenario in which dropping the above mentioned density assumptions facilitates a Lagrangian plane in
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
whose preimage is equal to
\operatorname{dom}(A)
, which is evidently not a domain of self-adjoint extension of
A
.

Assuming Hypothesis
2.1
, for the sake of brevity,
in the sequel we will use the following terminology.

Definition A.4
.

(i)
Given a subspace
{\mathcal{F}}
in
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
, we call

{\mathcal{A}}=A^{*}\big|_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
defined in (
A.1
) the
operator associated with
{\mathcal{F}}
.

(ii)
Given an operator
{\mathcal{A}}
, we call
{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{T}\big(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}\big)
the
subspace associated with
{\mathcal{A}}
.

(iii)
We say that a Lagrangian subspace
{\mathcal{F}}\in\Lambda(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
is
(\mathrm{T},{\mathcal{D}})
-aligned
or, when there is no confusion, simply
aligned
if (
A.5
) holds and the adjoint to the associated with
{\mathcal{F}}
operator
{\mathcal{A}}
satisfies (
A.6
).

(iv)
We say that a self-adjoint restriction
{\mathcal{A}}
of
A^{*}
is
(\mathrm{T},{\mathcal{D}})
-aligned
or, when there is no confusion, simply
aligned
if
(
A.9
) holds and the annihilator of the associated with
{\mathcal{A}}
subspace
{\mathcal{F}}
satisfies (
A.10
).

Definition
A.4
yields the following short rephrasing of Theorems
A.1
and
A.2
.

Corollary A.5
.

If
{\mathcal{F}}
is an aligned Lagrangian subspace
then the operator
{\mathcal{A}}
associated with
{\mathcal{F}}

is essentially self-adjoint and its closure
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
is aligned; in particular, the closure of the subspace associated with
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
is equal to
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Conversely, if
{\mathcal{A}}
is an aligned self-adjoint restriction of
A^{*}
then
the closure
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
of the subspace
{\mathcal{F}}
associated with
{\mathcal{A}}
is an aligned Lagrangian subspace; in particular, the closure of the operator associated with
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
is equal to
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be an aligned Lagrangian plane. Then (
A.5
) and (
A.6
) hold and imply
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{*}
by Theorem
A.1
. Let us consider the subspace
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})
associated with the self-adjoint operator
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
. To show that
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
is aligned we will have to prove that (a)
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
is dense in
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})
and that (b)
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime\circ}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}})
is dense in
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime\circ}
.
Assertion (a) follows from (
A.6
) since
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
. By Theorem
A.2
applied to the operator
\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}
assertion (b) is equivalent to the fact that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}
is Lagrangian. Thus it remains to show that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}={\mathcal{F}}
. To begin the proof of the latter equality we first recall from
(
A.2
) that
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})=\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}
. Since
\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}\cap{\mathcal{D}}\supseteq\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{D}})
we infer

{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})=\mathrm{T}\big(\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}\cap{\mathcal{D}}\big)\supseteq\mathrm{T}\big(\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{D}})\big)={\mathcal{F}}\cap\mathrm{T}({\mathcal{D}}),

where the last equality is checked directly. Taking closure and using (
A.6
) yields
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\supseteq{\mathcal{F}}
.
It remains to show that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}
.
We claim that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
is isotropic, that is,
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime\circ}
. To show this, we take any
f,g\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
so that
f=\mathrm{T}u
,
g=\mathrm{T}v
for some
u,v\in\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})}\cap{\mathcal{D}}
and pick sequences
u_{n},v_{n}\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})
such that
u_{n}\to u
and
v_{n}\to v
as
n\to\infty
. Since
\mathrm{T}u_{n},\mathrm{T}v_{n}\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
by the assumption, we conclude that
\omega(f,g)=\omega(\mathrm{T}u,\mathrm{T}v)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\omega(\mathrm{T}u_{n},\mathrm{T}v_{n})=0
as claimed. It follows from
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\supseteq{\mathcal{F}}
and the claim that
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime\circ}
and therefore
that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}={\mathcal{F}}
as
{\mathcal{F}}
is Lagrangian.
Hence,
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}
as required.

To begin the proof of the second part of the corollary, let
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}^{*}
be an aligned restriction of
A^{*}
and denote
{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})
. Then
(
A.9
) and (
A.10
) hold and imply that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
is Lagrangian by Theorem
A.2
. To check that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
is aligned we need to show that (a)
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}\cap{\mathcal{D}}
is dense in
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
and that (b)
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})}^{*})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
is dense in
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})}^{*})
. Since
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}=(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})^{\circ}={\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
, assertion (a) is the same as (
A.10
) and therefore holds. By Theorem
A.1
for
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
assertion (b) is equivalent to the fact that
the closure of the operator
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})}
associated with
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
is self-adjoint. So, to complete the proof it suffices to show that the closure of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})}
is equal to
{\mathcal{A}}
or that
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})=\operatorname{dom}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})}})
.
In other words, see (
A.2
), we want to check the equality

\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})=\overline{\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})})}.

(A.13)

The inclusion “
\subseteq
” in (
A.13
) follows from (
A.9
) by taking closure in

\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}=\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}))\subseteq\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})}).

Thus, it remains to prove the inclusion “
\supseteq
” in (
A.13
). Take a
u
from the RHS of (
A.13
) and select a sequence
u_{n}\in\mathrm{T}^{-1}(\overline{\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})})
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{+}
as
n\to\infty
.
Since
\mathrm{T}u_{n}\in\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}
as
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
is Lagrangian,
\omega(\mathrm{T}u_{n},g)=0
for any
g\in{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}})
. In particular, for all
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}
we have

\langle A^{*}u_{n},v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u_{n},A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\omega(\mathrm{T}u_{n},\mathrm{T}v)=0.

Using (
A.9
) we then conclude that
\langle A^{*}u_{n},v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle u_{n},A^{*}v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=0
for all
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
.
This shows that
u_{n}\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})
and therefore
u=\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}^{*})=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
thus completing the proof of
the inclusion “
\supseteq
” in (
A.13
).
∎

A particularly transparent and widely studied scenario of aligned Lagrangian subspaces and self-adjoint operators is discussed in Section
4
, see, in particular, Remark
4.2
.

Appendix B
The Krein–Naimark resolvent formula revisited

In this appendix, we revisit the classical Krein–Naimark (
B.5
) and Krein (
B.15
) formulas for the difference of resolvents of two self-adjoint extensions of an abstract symmetric operator, see, e.g,
[
119
, Section 14.6]
. As we demonstrate in the proof of Proposition
B.1
the Krein–Naimark formula (
B.5
) can be naturally derived from formula (
2.21
) in Theorem
2.5
by specializing it to the case of boundary triplets. Conversely, in Remark
B.2
we show how to derive (
2.21
) from (
B.5
). The poof of Krein’s resolvent formula for the case of finite deficiency indices is given in Proposition
B.3
.

Let
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
be a boundary triplet as described in Definition
4.1
. Following common convention we define one of the two self-adjoint extensions of
A
in the Krein-Naimark formula by

{\mathcal{A}}_{0}:=A^{*}\upharpoonright_{\operatorname{ker}(\Gamma_{0})},

(B.1)

and subtract from its resolvent the resolvent of yet another, arbitrary, self-adjoint extension.

First, we recall some known facts, see, e.g.,
[
119
, Section 14]
. Since

\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})=\operatorname{dom}(A_{0})\dot{+}\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}-\zeta)\text{ for }\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}},

the map

\Gamma_{0}\upharpoonright_{\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}-\zeta)}:\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}-\zeta)\to\mathfrak{H}
is bijective
and thus we define
\gamma(\zeta):=(\Gamma_{0}\upharpoonright_{\operatorname{ker}(A^{*}-\zeta)})^{-1}
and notice that
\gamma(\zeta)\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H},{\mathcal{H}})
and
\Gamma_{0}\gamma(\zeta)h=h
for any
h\in\mathfrak{H}
. In particular,
\gamma(\zeta)
is injective. We will use the well-known Derkach-Malamud Lemma saying that
\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})=\Gamma_{1}({\mathcal{A}}_{0}-\zeta)^{-1}
, see

[
56
, Lemma 1]
or
[
119
, Proposition 14.14(i)]
. The operator-valued function
\gamma(\cdot)
can be extended analytically to
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{0})
giving rise to the abstract Weyl function
M(\zeta):=\Gamma_{1}\gamma(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{0})
.

Next, let
{\mathcal{A}}
be an arbitrary self-adjoint extension of
A
, and let

{\mathcal{F}}\in\Lambda(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H})
be the Lagrangian subspace such that
{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}))
, cf. Theorems
A.1
,
A.2
and Remark
4.2
. We will treat
{\mathcal{F}}
as a linear relation,
see, e.g.,
[
119
, Section 14.1]
.
Slightly abusing notation we do not distinguish between the operator
M(\zeta)
and its graph, in particular, we write
{\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta):={\mathcal{F}}-{\text{graph}}(M(\zeta))
and treat both terms in the right-hand side as linear relations. The linear relation
{\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta)
is called
invertible
whenever

\displaystyle\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\mathfrak{H}:(f,0)\in({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))\}=\{0\},\text{ and }

(B.2)

\displaystyle\text{\rm{ran}}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))

\displaystyle:=\{g\in\mathfrak{H}:\exists f\in\mathfrak{H}\ {s.t.}\ (f,g)\in({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))\}=\mathfrak{H}.

(B.3)

In this case there exists an operator in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{H})
whose graph is given by

\{(g,f)\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}:(f,g)\in({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))\};

(B.4)

this operator is denoted by
({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))^{-1}
.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
be a boundary triplet for the symmetric operator
A
, see Definition
4.1
, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
be the self-adjoint extension of
A
from (
B.1
), let
{\mathcal{A}}
be an arbitrary self-adjoint extension of
A
and
{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}))
.
Then
{\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta)
is invertible and

({\mathcal{A}}-\zeta)^{-1}=({\mathcal{A}}_{0}-\zeta)^{-1}+\gamma(\zeta)({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))^{-1}\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})\text{ for $\zeta\not\in\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{0})\cup\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}})$. }

(B.5)

Proof.

We denote
R_{0}(\zeta):=({\mathcal{A}}_{0}-\zeta)^{-1}
and

R(\zeta)=({\mathcal{A}}-\zeta)^{-1}
. Since
\Gamma_{0}R_{0}(\overline{\zeta})=0
by (
B.1
), the resolvent difference formula from Theorem
2.5
and the Derkach-Malamud Lemma above yield

R_{0}(\zeta)-R(\zeta)=(\Gamma_{0}R_{0}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)-(\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)=-\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta).

It remains to prove (
B.2
), (
B.3
), and that

\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)=({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))^{-1}\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta}).

(B.6)

The main identity needed for the proofs is that

\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})u=\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)u=\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)u-M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u\,\text{ for all $u\in{\mathcal{H}}$}.

(B.7)

To justify the second equality in (
B.7
), we use
(A^{*}-\zeta)\gamma(\zeta)=0

and
\Gamma_{0}(I_{\mathcal{H}}-\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0})=0
, yielding

\text{\rm{ran}}(I_{\mathcal{H}}-\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0})\subset\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{0})
, and write

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)({\mathcal{A}}-\zeta)R(\zeta)=\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)(A^{*}-\zeta)R(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)(A^{*}-\zeta)(I_{\mathcal{H}}-\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0})R(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)({\mathcal{A}}_{0}-\zeta)(I_{\mathcal{H}}-\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0})R(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{1}(I_{\mathcal{H}}-\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0})R(\zeta)=\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)-M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta),

thus proving (
B.7
).
Since
R(\zeta)
is a bijection of
{\mathcal{H}}
onto
\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
, we have
{\mathcal{F}}=\{(\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)u):u\in{\mathcal{H}}\}
. This and (
B.7
) yield

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\{(f,g-M(\zeta)f):(f,g)\in{\mathcal{F}}\}

(B.8)

\displaystyle=\big\{\big(\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)u-M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u\big):u\in{\mathcal{H}}\big\}

(B.9)

\displaystyle=\big\{\big(\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})u\big):u\in{\mathcal{H}}\big\}.

Since
\mathrm{T}
is surjective, (
B.3
) follows from (
B.8
). Indeed, for any
g\in\mathfrak{H}
there is some
v\in\operatorname{dom}(A^{*})
such that
\Gamma_{0}v=0
and
\Gamma_{1}v=g
. Since
v\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{0})
, there is some
u\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
v=R_{0}(\zeta)u
and so
g=\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)u\in\text{\rm{ran}}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))
by (
B.8
) and (
B.7
). To begin the proof of (
B.2
), we first notice that

\gamma(\zeta)\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))\subset\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}})
. Indeed,
by (
B.8
) and (
B.7
) we have
\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))=\big\{\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u:\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)u=M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,u\in{\mathcal{H}}\big\}
and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}

\displaystyle\gamma(\zeta)\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))

(B.10)

\displaystyle=\{\big(\Gamma_{0}\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,\Gamma_{1}\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u\big):\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)u=M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,u\in{\mathcal{H}}\}

(B.11)

\displaystyle=\{\big(\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u\big):\Gamma_{1}R(\zeta)u=M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u,u\in{\mathcal{H}}\}

(B.12)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}}\cap{\rm graph}(M(\zeta)).

(B.13)

Therefore,
({\mathcal{A}}-\zeta)\gamma(\zeta)\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))=(A^{*}-\zeta)\gamma(\zeta)\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))=\{0\}
yields
the inclusion
\gamma(\zeta)\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))\subset\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{A}}-\zeta)=\{0\}

and thus
\operatorname{ker}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))=\{0\}
because
\gamma(\zeta)
is injective, thus finishing the proof of (
B.2
).
Finally, using (
B.8
) again,

\displaystyle{\rm graph}({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))^{-1}

\displaystyle=\big\{(g,f)\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}:(f,g)\in({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{\big(\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})u,\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)u\big):u\in{\mathcal{H}}\big\}

yielding
({\mathcal{F}}-M(\zeta))^{-1}\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})=\Gamma_{0}R(\zeta)
,
as required to finish the proof of (
B.6
) and thus (
B.5
).
∎

Remark B.2
.

In the course of proof of the Krein-Naimark formula (
B.5
) we established relation (
B.6
). Using this relation we now show how to derive formula (
2.21
) in Theorem
2.5
from formula (
B.5
), cf. the proofs of Theorem 2 and Corollary 4 in
[
56
]
. For any two self-adjoint extensions
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
and the extension
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
given by (
B.1
) we denote

R_{j}(\zeta)=({\mathcal{A}}_{j}-\zeta)^{-1}
for any
\zeta
which is not in the spectrum of
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
,
j=0,1,2
. Applying (
B.5
) and using (
B.6
) for
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
yields

R_{1}(\zeta)=R_{0}(\zeta)+\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta),\quad R_{2}(\zeta)=R_{0}(\zeta)+\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R_{2}(\zeta).

(B.14)

Multiplying (
B.14
) by
\Gamma_{1}
and using formulas
\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})=\Gamma_{1}R_{0}(\zeta)
and
M(\zeta)=\Gamma_{1}\gamma(\zeta)
gives

\Gamma_{1}R_{1}(\zeta)=\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})+M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta),\quad\Gamma_{1}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})=\gamma^{*}({\zeta})+M(\overline{\zeta})\Gamma_{0}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta}).

Plugging this in the RHS of formula (
2.21
) and using
the property
M^{*}(\zeta)=M(\overline{\zeta})
of the Weyl function, see, e.g.,
[
119
, Proposition 14.15(ii)]
, yileds

\displaystyle\big(

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}\Gamma_{1}R_{1}({\zeta})-\big(\Gamma_{1}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}({\zeta})

\displaystyle=\big(\Gamma_{0}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}\big(\gamma^{*}(\overline{\zeta})+M(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)\big)-\big(\gamma^{*}({\zeta})+M(\overline{\zeta})\Gamma_{0}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}\Gamma_{0}R_{1}({\zeta})

\displaystyle=\big(\gamma(\overline{\zeta})\Gamma_{0}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}-\big(\gamma(\zeta)\Gamma_{0}R_{1}(\zeta)\big)+\big(\Gamma_{0}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}\big(M(\zeta)-M^{*}(\overline{\zeta})\big)\Gamma_{0}R_{1}({\zeta})

\displaystyle=\big(R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})-R_{0}(\overline{\zeta})\big)^{*}-\big(R_{1}(\zeta)-R_{0}(\zeta)\big)=R_{2}(\zeta)-R_{1}(\zeta),

where, to pass to the last line, we used (
B.14
) again.
This proves (
2.21
) as required.

\Diamond

We will conclude this section by deriving from formula (
2.22
) in Theorem
2.5
yet another classical Krein’s resolvent formula
(
B.15
) valid under the temporary assumption that the equal deficiency indices of
A
are finite and for which we refer to the classical text

[
2
, Section VIII.106]
and a very nice newer exposition in
[
41
, Appendix A]
.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
(\mathfrak{H},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
be a boundary triplet for the symmetric operator
A
with equal and finite deficiency indices, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
be any two self-adjoint extensions of
A
, let
(m,m)
denote the deficiency indices of the operator
A_{0}=A^{*}\big|_{\operatorname{dom}(A_{0})}
defined by the equality
\operatorname{dom}(A_{0}):=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})\cap\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})
, and let
\{u_{k}(\zeta)\}_{1\leq k\leq m}
be any basis in the subspace
\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\zeta)
. Then there exists a unique non-singular matrix
{\mathcal{P}}(\zeta)=\big(p_{lj}(\zeta)\big)_{1\leq l,j\leq m}
, cf. (
B.20
), such that the resolvents of the operators
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
for each
u\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfy

\big(R_{1}(\zeta)-R_{2}(\zeta)\big)u=\sum_{l,j}p_{lj}(\zeta)\langle u,u_{j}(\overline{\zeta})\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}u_{l}(\zeta)\,

(B.15)

for all
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})\cup\operatorname{Spec}({\mathcal{A}}_{2}))
.

Proof.

We temporarily denote by
R(\zeta)
the RHS of (
2.22
) in Theorem
2.5
, that is, we set
R(\zeta)=(\mathrm{T}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta))
. Thus, our objective is to prove that
R(\zeta)
is equal to the RHS of (
B.15
).
First, we will use the fact that the subspaces

{\mathcal{F}}_{1}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1}))
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2}))
are Lagrangian in
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
, cf. Remark
4.2
, and prove the following elementary assertions:

{\rm(i)}\,\ \operatorname{ker}(R(\zeta))=\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-\zeta);\quad{\rm(ii)}\,\ \text{\rm{ran}}(R(\zeta))=\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\zeta).

(B.16)

To begin the proof we notice that for any
u,v\in{\mathcal{H}}
by (
2.11
) one has

\begin{split}\langle R(\zeta)u,v\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}&=\langle(\mathrm{T}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta}))^{*}J(\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta))u,v\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}\\
&=\langle J(\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta))u,\mathrm{T}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})v\rangle_{{}_{\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}}}=\omega\big(\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta)u,\mathrm{T}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})v\big).\end{split}

(B.17)

If
u=(A_{0}-\zeta)w
for some
w\in\operatorname{dom}(A_{0})=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})\cap\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})
then

R_{1}(\zeta)u=R_{1}(\zeta)(A_{0}-\zeta)w=R_{1}(\zeta)({\mathcal{A}}_{1}-\zeta)w=w

because
w\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})
and thus
\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta)u=\mathrm{T}w\in{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
because
