w\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is isotropic, (
B.17
) yields
R(\zeta)u=0
and thus
\overline{\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-z)}\subseteq\operatorname{ker}(R(\zeta))
. On the other hand, if
R(\zeta)u=0
then
\mathrm{T}R_{1}(\zeta)u\in{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
by (
B.17
) since
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\mathrm{T}(\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2}))
is co-isotropic. Then
w:=R_{1}(\zeta)u\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})\cap\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})
and thus
u=(A_{0}-\zeta)w
yielding
\operatorname{ker}(R(\zeta))\subseteq\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-z)
. This proves (i) in (
B.16
). In particular,
\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-\zeta)
is closed and thus
{\mathcal{H}}=\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-\zeta)\oplus\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})
where the sum is orthogonal. To show that
R(\zeta)u\in\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\zeta)=\big(\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-\overline{\zeta})\big)^{\perp}
for each
u\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we pick any vector
v=(A_{0}-\overline{\zeta})w\in\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-\overline{\zeta})
with some
w\in\operatorname{dom}(A_{0}):=\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})\cap\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})
. As above,

R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})v=R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})(A_{0}-\overline{\zeta})w=R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})({\mathcal{A}}_{2}-\overline{\zeta})w=w

because
w\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})
and thus
\mathrm{T}R_{2}(\overline{\zeta})v=\mathrm{T}w\in{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
because
w\in\operatorname{dom}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
is isotropic, (
B.17
) yields
\langle R(\zeta)u,v\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}=0
and so
the inclusion
\text{\rm{ran}}(R(\zeta))\subseteq\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\zeta)
in assertion (ii) of (
B.16
) does hold. What we have proved so far shows that the finite dimensional operator

R(\zeta)\big|_{\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})}:\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})\to\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-{\zeta})\text{ is an isomorphism}

(B.18)

as it is injective by
assertion (i) of (
B.16
) and
\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})\cap\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-\zeta)=\{0\}
. This implies assertion (ii)
and finishes the proof of (
B.16
).

The rest easily follows by representing the isomorphism in (
B.18
) as an
(m\times m)
matrix
{\mathbf{r}}(\zeta)
using the bases
(u_{k}(\overline{\zeta}))_{1\leq k\leq m}
and
(u_{k}(\zeta))_{1\leq k\leq m}
in
\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})
and
\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\zeta)
, respectively. Indeed, let
{\mathbf{r}}(\zeta)=\big(r_{lk}(\zeta)\big)_{1\leq l,k\leq m}
be chosen such that
R(\zeta)u_{k}(\overline{\zeta})=\sum_{l=1}^{m}r_{lk}(\zeta)u_{l}(\zeta)
, and let

G(\zeta)=\big(\langle u_{k}(\zeta),u_{l}(\zeta)\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}\big)_{1\leq k,l\leq m}
denote the Gramm matrix so that
G(\zeta)^{\top}=\overline{G(z)}
while its inverse will be written as
G(\zeta)^{-1}=\big(G^{-1}_{kj}(\zeta)\big)_{1\leq k,j\leq m}
. If
u=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{k}u_{k}(\overline{\zeta})\in\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})
then
\overline{G(\overline{\zeta})}(c_{k})_{1\leq k\leq m}=\big(\langle u,u_{j}(\overline{\zeta})\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}\big)_{1\leq j\leq m}
as vectors in
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
and therefore

\begin{split}R(\zeta)u&=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{k}R(\zeta)u_{k}(\overline{\zeta})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{G^{-1}_{kj}(\overline{\zeta})}\langle u,u_{j}(\overline{\zeta})\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}\big)\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}r_{lk}(\zeta)u_{l}(\zeta)\big)\\
&=\sum_{l,j}\big(\sum_{k=1}^{m}r_{lk}(\zeta)\overline{G^{-1}_{kj}(\overline{\zeta})}\big)\langle u,u_{j}(\overline{\zeta})\rangle_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}u_{l}(\zeta).\end{split}

(B.19)

We now define
{\mathcal{P}}(\zeta)=(p_{lj}(\zeta))_{1\leq l,j\leq m}
by the formula

{\mathcal{P}}(\zeta):={\mathbf{r}}(\zeta)\overline{G(\overline{\zeta})^{-1}}

(B.20)

and obtain equation
(
B.15
) for

u\in\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})
. By
{\mathcal{H}}=\text{\rm{ran}}(A_{0}-\zeta)\oplus\operatorname{ker}(A_{0}^{*}-\overline{\zeta})
and assertion (i) in (
B.16
)
it also holds for all
u\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

Appendix C
Dirichlet and Neumann trace operators

In this appendix we recall definitions and some facts about
various types of Dirichlet and Neumann trace operators which are discussed in detail in
[
67
]
,
[
69
]
.

Hypothesis C.1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N},n\geq 2
, and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain with
C^{1,r}
,
r>1/2
, boundary.

First, we define the strong trace operators.
Let us introduce the boundary trace operator
\gamma_{{}_{D}}^{0}
(the Dirichlet
trace) by

\gamma_{{}_{D}}^{0}\colon C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\to C^{0}({\partial\Omega}),\quad\gamma_{{}_{D}}^{0}u=u|_{\partial\Omega}.

(C.1)

By the standard trace theorem, see, e.g.,
[
121
, Proposition 4.4.5]
, there exists a bounded, surjective Dirichlet
trace operator

\gamma_{{}_{D}}\colon H^{s}(\Omega)\to H^{s-1/2}({\partial\Omega})\hookrightarrow L^{2}({\partial\Omega}),\quad 1/2<s<3/2.

(C.2)

Next, retaining Hypothesis
C.1
, we introduce the Neumann trace operator
\gamma_{{}_{N}}
by

\gamma_{{}_{N}}=\nu\cdot\gamma_{{}_{D}}\nabla\colon H^{s+1}(\Omega)\to L^{2}({\partial\Omega}),\quad 1/2<s<3/2,

(C.3)

where
\nu
denotes the outward pointing normal unit vector to
{\partial\Omega}
.
Furthermore, one can extend
\gamma_{{}_{N}}
to the weak Neumann trace operator still denoted by
\gamma_{{}_{N}}
such that

\gamma_{{}_{N}}:\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega)\,|\,\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\to H^{-1/2}({\partial\Omega}).

Lemma C.2

(
[
69
]
, Lemma 6.3)
.

Assume Hypothesis
C.1
. Then the Neumann trace operator
\gamma_{{}_{N}}
considered in the context

\gamma_{{}_{N}}:H^{2}(\Omega)\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\to H^{1/2}({\partial\Omega}),

(C.4)

is well-defined, linear, bounded, onto, and with a linear bounded right-inverse.
In addition, the null space of
\gamma_{{}_{N}}
in (
C.4
)
is
H^{2}_{0}(\Omega)
, the closure of
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
in
H^{2}(\Omega)
.

Lemma C.3

(
[
69
]
, Corollary 6.6)
.

Assume Hypothesis
C.1
. Then there exists a unique linear bounded operator

\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}:\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\,|\,\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\to H^{-1/2}({\partial\Omega}),

which is compatible with the Dirichlet
trace introduced in (
C.2
). This extension of the Dirichlet trace operator has dense range and allows for the following integration by
parts formula,

{}_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\langle\gamma_{{}_{N}}w,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)}=(\Delta w,u)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-(w,\Delta u)_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(C.5)

valid for every
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
with
\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and every
w\in H^{2}(\Omega)\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
.

Lemma C.4

(
[
69
]
, Corollary 6.11)
.

Assume Hypothesis
C.1
. Then there exists a unique linear bounded operator

\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}:\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\,|\,\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\to H^{-3/2}({\partial\Omega}),

(C.6)

which is compatable with the Neumann
trace, introduced in (
C.3
). This extension of the Neumann trace operator has dense range and allows for the following integration by parts formula,

{}_{H^{3/2}({\partial\Omega})}\langle\gamma_{{}_{D}}w,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}u\rangle_{H^{-3/2}({\partial\Omega})}=(w,\Delta u)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-(\Delta w,u)_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(C.7)

valid for every
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
with
\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and every
w\in H^{2}(\Omega)
with
\gamma_{{}_{N}}w=0
.

Next, we introduce the Dirichet-to-Neumann map
M_{D,N}
associated with
-\Delta
on
\Omega
as

M_{D,N}:H^{-1/2}({\partial\Omega})\to H^{-3/2}({\partial\Omega}):g\mapsto-\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}(u_{D}),

(C.8)

where
u_{D}
is the unique solution of the boundary value problem

-\Delta u=0\,\,\hbox{in}\,\,\Omega,\,\,\,\,u\in L^{2}(\Omega),\,\,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u=g\,\,\hbox{in}\,\,{\partial\Omega}.

(C.9)

Lemma C.5

(
[
69
]
, Theorem 12.1)
.

Assume Hypothesis
C.1
. Then the map

\displaystyle\tau_{{}_{N}}:\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)|\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega),\,\tau_{{}_{N}}u:=\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{N}}u+M_{D,N}(\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u),

(C.10)

is bounded when the space

\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)|\Delta u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\}=\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{\max})
is equipped with the natural graph norm

(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\Delta u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2})^{1/2}
. Moreover, this operator is onto. In fact,

\tau_{{}_{N}}(H^{2}(\Omega)\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega))=H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega).

(C.11)

Also, the null space of the map
\tau_{{}_{N}}
is given by

\operatorname{ker}(\tau_{{}_{N}})=H_{0}^{2}(\Omega)\dot{+}\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega),\,\,-\Delta u=0\}.

(C.12)

Finally, the following Green formula holds for every
u,v\in\operatorname{dom}(-\Delta_{\max})
,

\displaystyle(-\Delta u,v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-(u,-\Delta v)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=-{{}_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\langle\tau_{{}_{N}}u,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}v\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)}}+\overline{{}_{H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\langle\tau_{{}_{N}}v,\widehat{\gamma}_{{}_{D}}u\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)}}.

(C.13)
